Can Past Prices and Public Information Predict Returns?

Slide 1 in the PDF
Eugene F. Fama. Photo: Bengt Nyman (2013); CC BY 2.0; Wikimedia Commons
“A market in which prices always ‘fully reflect’ available information is called ‘efficient.’ ” — Fama (1970)
  • Lag-1 autocorrelation of daily returns: \(-0.099\) for the S&P 500 and \(0.109\) for the BET, both significant
    • Bitcoin: \(-0.022\), inside the robust test band, so we do not reject \(\rho_1 = 0\)
  • Yesterday's return explains \(0.98\%\) of the variance of today's return for the S&P 500 and \(1.19\%\) for the BET
  • Dividend–price ratio, 680 monthly forecasts for the S&P 500: out-of-sample \(R^2 = -0.51\%\)
    • the forecast is worse than the historical mean
  • Of 2 000 random trading rules, \(5.2\%\) are “significant” at 5%
    • the best one has \(|t| = 3.30\), although it is pure chance
lll Central chart: Lag-1 Autocorrelation Across Markets

The chapter asks whether past prices, news or other public information can predict returns, after adjusting for risk and costs. The definition of Fama (1970) depends on the information set: the weak form of efficiency concerns past prices and returns, the semi-strong form all public information, and the strong form private information as well. Every test also needs a model of the expected return, so a rejection does not by itself prove inefficiency: this is the joint hypothesis problem.

Across 16 series of daily log returns, the lag-1 autocorrelation is positive and significant for the BET (0.109), IPC (0.071), Nifty 50 (0.048) and EUR/RON (0.170), and negative and significant for the S&P 500 (−0.099). For Bitcoin (−0.022) and Ethereum (−0.012) it stays inside the robust test band. The effect is small: yesterday's return explains 0.98% of the variance of today's return for the S&P 500 and 1.19% for the BET. The proposed mechanisms, infrequent trading, the managed-float regime and overreaction, are not tested here.

Out-of-sample forecasts and multiple testing call for the same caution. With the dividend–price ratio, the 680 monthly forecasts for the S&P 500 have an out-of-sample \(R^2\) of −0.51%, and the Clark–West test gives \(t = 0.42\), so we do not reject the absence of predictability. Of 2,000 random trading rules, 5.2% are “significant” at 5%, as the test level implies, and the best one has \(|t| = 3.30\), although it is pure chance. The number of rules tried matters as much as the threshold chosen.

What you will be able to do

  • Derive the random-walk restrictions and the variance ratio from the autocorrelations.
  • Compute the lag-1 autocorrelation and the variance ratio with robust standard errors.
  • Compute the out-of-sample \(R^2\) and the Clark–West test.
  • Compute the cumulative abnormal return in an event study.
  • Interpret an anomaly after the Holm correction.

The Further Topics and the Appendix are reading for familiarity.

Returns and information

The section makes the statement "past prices contain no information" precise. Market efficiency is defined relative to an information set, and the weak, semi-strong and strong forms differ in what that set contains. Every test of efficiency also needs a model of expected returns, so a rejection is always a joint rejection. The section then sets out the random-walk model of log prices and its three versions, RW1, RW2 and RW3, which differ only in what they assume about the shocks, and shows with simulated series why the difference between zero correlation and independence matters for returns.

1. Returns and information

Slide 3 in the PDF

The random walk comes in three versions of decreasing strength:

  • RW1 (Random Walk 1) assumes independent and identically distributed increments;
  • RW2 keeps independence but lets the distribution change over time;
  • RW3 requires only that the increments be uncorrelated, so they may still be dependent.

The question of the section is what it means, precisely, that past prices contain no information about future returns.

The version matters for testing. A test built under RW1 can reject only because volatility varies over time, although the increments remain uncorrelated. A second difficulty is independent of the version: every test also needs a model of expected returns, so a rejection does not by itself prove that the market is inefficient.

In brief

  • RW1: Random Walk 1 — independent, identically distributed increments.
  • RW2: Random Walk 2 — independent increments, possibly with different distributions.
  • RW3: Random Walk 3 — uncorrelated increments, possibly dependent.
  • The question: what does it mean, precisely, that past prices contain no information?
    • information about future returns
  • The version matters: a test built under RW1 can reject only because volatility varies over time.
  • Every test also needs a model of expected returns, so a rejection does not by itself prove inefficiency.

A Game: Which Series Is the Real Market? (1/2)

Slide 4 in the PDF
    Question for the room One of the four series is the S&P 500 from 2010 to 2026; the other three are random walks with the same drift and volatility. All four paths show trends and sharp falls. What can we infer from these features alone?
    Answer

    A random walk with drift can produce trends, and Normal innovations can produce large moves. These visual features alone do not constitute a statistical rejection. The real series is D; recognising it does not establish return predictability. This is why efficiency is tested with statistics rather than by inspection.

    The chart shows four price paths. One of them is the S&P 500 (Standard & Poor's 500); the other three are simulated random walks. The reader is asked to pick the real market before the answer is given on the next slide.

    In the interactive version, the slider moves the trading day along the four paths and the menu reveals the real series. The button "New random walks" draws three new simulated paths, so the game can be repeated with different pictures.

    In brief

    S&P 500: Standard & Poor's 500. MFM_ch2_random_walk_game

    A Game: Which Series Is the Real Market? (2/2)

    Slide 5 in the PDF

    The real series is panel D. The point of the game is not whether the reader guessed it: Roberts (1959) showed that simulated random walks produce "patterns" by chance, trends and sharp falls that the eye reads as structure. Visual inspection therefore cannot separate a market from a random walk, and the chapter relies on statistical tests instead.

    The economic argument goes back to Bachelier (1900) and Samuelson (1965): prices that are properly anticipated fluctuate randomly. If all the information about future cash flows is already in today's price, the next price change reflects only news, which cannot be foreseen. Unpredictability is then the mark of a well-functioning market, not of a disorderly one.

    In brief

    • Which panel is the S&P 500? Vote: A, B, C or D?
    • <2-> Answer: D.
    • <2-> Roberts (1959): simulated random walks produce “patterns” by chance
    • <2-> We need statistical tests, not visual inspection
    • <2-> Bachelier (1900) and Samuelson (1965): properly anticipated prices fluctuate randomly
      • so unpredictability is the mark of a well-functioning market
    lll MFM_ch2_random_walk_game

    Efficiency and the Information Set

    Slide 6 in the PDF

    Fama (1970) defines a market as efficient if prices "fully reflect" all available information \(\mathcal{I}_t\); this is the Efficient Market Hypothesis (EMH). Formally, no strategy that uses \(\mathcal{I}_t\) earns expected returns above the fair, risk-adjusted return. The definition is about expected returns: a strategy may be lucky in a given sample without contradicting efficiency.

    The definition depends on what \(\mathcal{I}_t\) contains. In the weak form it contains past prices and returns, and the typical tests are autocorrelations and variance ratios, with runs and long-memory tests in the appendix. In the semi-strong form it contains all public information, such as news and reports, and the typical test is the event study. In the strong form it contains private, insider information as well, and the tests look at the returns of insiders and fund managers.

    The three forms are nested. The strong form implies the semi-strong form, which implies the weak form, because each information set contains the previous one. Adjusting returns for risk with factor models, which the fair return requires, is the subject of Chapter 3.

    In brief

    • Fama (1970): a market is efficient if prices “fully reflect” all available information \(\mathcal{I}_t\)
      • Efficient Market Hypothesis, EMH
      • portrait and surveys: Appendix
      • precursors: Appendix
      • formally: no strategy using \(\mathcal{I}_t\) earns expected returns above the fair (risk-adjusted) return
    FormInformation set \(\mathcal{I}_t\)Typical test (this chapter)
    WeakPast prices and returnsAutocorrelation, variance ratio; in the appendix: runs and long-memory tests
    Semi-strongAll public information (news, reports)Event studies
    StrongPublic and private (insider) informationReturns of insiders and fund managers
    • The strong form implies the semi-strong form, which implies the weak form
    • Risk-adjusted returns with factor models follow in Chapter 3

    The Joint-Hypothesis Problem

    Slide 7 in the PDF

    The "fair return" in the definition of efficiency requires a model of expected returns, for example the Capital Asset Pricing Model (CAPM) of Chapter 3. Every test of efficiency is therefore a joint test of efficiency and of the asset-pricing model (Fama, 1991). A rejection may mean that the market is inefficient, or that the model of risk is wrong. Predictable expected returns are allowed in an efficient market if they compensate a risk that varies over time.

    The problem is least severe at short horizons. At daily horizons expected returns are tiny, so the choice of model matters little, and weak-form tests on daily data are the cleanest tests of efficiency. The logic of testing also limits what can be concluded: efficiency can never be "proved", only not rejected by a given test on given data.

    In brief

    CAPM: Capital Asset Pricing Model.
    • “Fair return” requires a model of expected returns
      • e.g. CAPM, Chapter 3
    • Every test of efficiency is a joint test of efficiency and of the asset-pricing model Fama (1991)
      • A rejection may mean an inefficient market or a wrong model of risk
      • Predictable expected returns are allowed if they compensate time-varying risk
    • At daily horizons expected returns are tiny
      • so the choice of model matters little
      • weak-form tests are the cleanest
    • Efficiency can never be “proved”, only not rejected by a given test on given data

    Correlation and dependence in returns

    Slide 8 in the PDF

    "The past contains no information" can mean two different things: zero correlation or independence. The lag-\(k\) autocorrelation is the correlation between a return and the return \(k\) periods earlier. Zero correlation excludes a linear association between the two, but it may leave dependence in nonlinear functions of the returns, for example in their squares.

    A toy example shows the gap. Let \(X\) take the values \(-2, -1, 1, 2\) with equal probability and let \(Y = X^2\). Then \(\mathbb{E}[X] = 0\) and \(\mathbb{E}[XY] = (-8-1+1+8)/4 = 0\), so the covariance and the correlation are zero. Yet knowing \(X\) determines \(Y\) exactly: the two variables are as dependent as variables can be. For returns, the analogue is a series whose levels are uncorrelated while the sizes of the moves, measured by squared returns, are dependent.

    The Random Walk: the Model

    Slide 9 in the PDF

    Let \(P_t\) be the index level at the close of day \(t\) and \(p_t = \ln P_t\) the log price. The random walk with drift is

    \[p_t = \mu + p_{t-1} + \varepsilon_t,\]
    (2.1)

    where \(\mu\) is the drift, the mean growth per day, and \(\varepsilon_t\) is the day's shock, with mean zero and variance \(\sigma^2\). By repeated substitution, \(p_t = p_0 + \mu t + \sum_{s=1}^{t}\varepsilon_s\): the log price is a linear trend plus the sum of all past shocks. Equivalently, the log return is \(r_t = p_t - p_{t-1} = \mu + \varepsilon_t\).

    With the information set \(\mathcal{I}_t = \{p_t, p_{t-1}, \dots\}\), the best forecast of the future log price is \(\mathbb{E}[p_{t+h} \mid \mathcal{I}_t] = p_t + \mu h\), today's price plus the drift. This holds if the shocks have conditional mean zero, \(\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{t+1} \mid \mathcal{I}_t] = 0\). Zero autocorrelations alone (RW3) are not enough: under RW3 the past does not, in general, improve the best linear forecast, but a nonlinear function of the past may still help.

    If the shocks are uncorrelated with variance \(\sigma^2\), the variance of the \(h\)-day change is \(\mathrm{Var}(p_{t+h} - p_t) = h\,\sigma^2\): it grows linearly with the horizon. This property is the basis of the variance ratio in section 2. RW1, RW2 and RW3 differ only in what they assume about the shocks \(\varepsilon_t\) (Campbell, Lo & MacKinlay, 1997).

    In brief

    \[ p_t = \mu + p_{t-1} + \varepsilon_t, \qquad p_t = \ln P_t \]
    • \(P_t\): the index level at the close of day \(t\).
      • \(p_t\): the log price.
      • \(\mu\): the drift (mean growth per day).
      • \(\varepsilon_t\): the day's shock, with mean zero and variance \(\sigma^2\).
    • By repeated substitution: \(p_t = p_0 + \mu t + \sum_{s=1}^{t} \varepsilon_s\).
      • the log price is a linear trend plus the sum of all past shocks.
    • Equivalently, the log return \(r_t = p_t - p_{t-1} = \mu + \varepsilon_t\).
    • The best forecast: \(\E[p_{t+h} \mid \mathcal{I}_t] = p_t + \mu h\), with \(\mathcal{I}_t = \{p_t, p_{t-1}, \dots\}\).
      • it holds if the shocks have conditional mean zero, \(\E[\varepsilon_{t+1} \mid \mathcal{I}_t] = 0\): today's price plus the drift.
      • RW3 (zero autocorrelations only) is not enough.
      • Under RW3, the past does not, in general, improve the best linear forecast.
    • With uncorrelated shocks of variance \(\sigma^2\): \(\Var(p_{t+h} - p_t) = h\,\sigma^2\).
      • the variance grows linearly with the horizon.
      • the basis of the variance ratio in section 2.
    • RW1, RW2 and RW3 differ only in what they assume about the shocks \(\varepsilon_t\) Campbell, Lo & MacKinlay (1997).

    RW1, RW2, RW3: the restrictions on returns

    Slide 10 in the PDF

    The three versions of the random walk are distinguished by the condition imposed on the shocks:

    • Under RW1, the shocks are independent and identically distributed (i.i.d.), with mean 0 and variance \(\sigma^2\); an illustrative model is \(r_t = \mu + 0.01 z_t\), with \(z_t\) i.i.d. with the standard Normal distribution \(N(0,1)\).
    • Under RW2, the shocks are independent but their variance \(\sigma_t^2\) may vary over time; the illustration is \(r_t = \mu + \sigma_t z_t\), with \(\sigma_t\) equal to 1% and then 3%, fixed in advance.
    • Under RW3, only \(\mathrm{Cov}(\varepsilon_t, \varepsilon_{t-k}) = 0\) for every \(k \ge 1\) is required.

    The example of RW3 used throughout the chapter is the GARCH (Generalized AutoRegressive Conditional Heteroskedasticity) model,

    \[\varepsilon_t = \sigma_t z_t, \qquad \sigma_t^2 = \omega + \alpha\,\varepsilon_{t-1}^2 + \beta\,\sigma_{t-1}^2,\]
    (2.2)

    in its GARCH(1,1) form, with \(\omega > 0\) and \(\alpha, \beta \ge 0\). Today's variance depends on yesterday's squared shock and yesterday's variance.

    The versions are nested. RW1 implies RW2, because i.i.d. returns are in particular independent; RW2 implies RW3, because independent returns are in particular uncorrelated, provided their variance exists. A GARCH model keeps zero correlation but violates RW2: the size of today's move depends on past shocks. And RW3 does not make tomorrow's mean unpredictable, since a nonlinear function of the past can forecast it.

    In brief

    • i.i.d.: independent and identically distributed.
    • GARCH: Generalized AutoRegressive Conditional Heteroskedasticity.
    • \(z_t\): a shock with the standard Normal distribution \(N(0,1)\).
      • in GARCH(1,1), \(\omega > 0\) and \(\alpha, \beta \ge 0\).
    Condition on the shocksIllustrative return model
    RW1\(\varepsilon_t\) i.i.d., mean 0, variance \(\sigma^2\)\(r_t=\mu+0.01z_t\), \(z_t\) i.i.d. \(N(0,1)\)
    RW2\(\varepsilon_t\) independent; the variance \(\sigma_t^2\) may vary over time\(r_t=\mu+\sigma_t z_t\), \(\sigma_t=1\%\) then \(3\%\), fixed in advance
    RW3\(\operatorname{Cov}(\varepsilon_t, \varepsilon_{t-k}) = 0\) for every \(k \ge 1\)GARCH: \(\varepsilon_t = \sigma_t z_t\), \(\sigma_t^2 = \omega + \alpha\varepsilon_{t-1}^2 + \beta\sigma_{t-1}^2\)
    • RW1 implies RW2: i.i.d. returns are in particular independent.
    • RW2 implies RW3: independent returns are in particular uncorrelated, provided their variance exists.
    • A GARCH model keeps zero correlation but violates RW2.
      • the size of today's move depends on past shocks.
    • RW3 does not make tomorrow's mean unpredictable: a nonlinear function of the past can forecast it.

    Three models for returns and volatility

    Slide 11 in the PDF

    The chart shows three simulated series with mean zero, one for each version of the random walk: daily returns at the top and, below, the conditional standard deviation (SD) used in the simulation. The RW2 volatility is fixed in advance, 1% and then 3% from the middle of the sample, while the GARCH volatility responds to past shocks.

    In the interactive version, the sliders set the GARCH parameters \(\alpha\), the reaction to yesterday's shock, and \(\beta\), the persistence of the variance, as well as the RW2 volatility after day 450. The ▶ button changes the parameter step by step.

      Question for the room RW2 has independent returns, with standard deviation 1% up to day 450 and 3% after; GARCH has turbulent periods after large shocks. What is the kurtosis of RW2 in this simulation?
      Answer

      Clearly above 3: 4.8, against 3.0 for RW1 and 4.1 for GARCH. Mixing a calm period with a turbulent one gives fat tails without any dependence, so neither the chart of returns nor the kurtosis tells RW2 from GARCH. The difference lies elsewhere: in RW2 volatility rises at a date fixed in advance and the returns stay independent; in GARCH today's volatility depends on yesterday's shock (α = 0.08) and on yesterday's volatility (β = 0.90), so the returns are dependent.

      The GARCH series shows volatility clustering: large moves tend to be followed by large moves. The RW2 series also has a turbulent period, although its returns are independent. The chart alone therefore cannot tell a regime change fixed in advance from volatility clustering; the difference lies in whether volatility depends on past shocks.

      In brief

      SD: Standard Deviation.
      • Three simulated series with mean zero.
        • daily returns on top; below, the conditional standard deviation used in the simulation.
      • RW2 volatility is fixed in advance, while GARCH volatility responds to past shocks.
        • 1% then 3%, from the middle of the sample.
      • Volatility clustering: large moves tend to be followed by large moves.
      • The RW2 series also has a turbulent period although its returns are independent.
        • The chart alone cannot tell a regime change fixed in advance from volatility clustering.

      GARCH: returns versus squared returns

      Slide 12 in the PDF

      The chart compares two autocorrelation functions of the same simulated GARCH series. On the left are the autocorrelations of the returns, on the right those of the squared returns, at lags up to 20.

      In the interactive version, the sliders set \(\alpha\) and \(\beta\), and the menu sets the length \(T\) of the series, from 900 to 10,000 days.

        Question for the room Keep β = 0.90 and the 900-day series. As α rises from 0.08 to 0.095, how does the sample lag-1 autocorrelation of squared returns change?
        Answer

        It rises: in this simulation, the sample autocorrelation rises from 0.216 to 0.272. A long-run variance of 1 does not fix the dependence of squared returns. At α = 0.095, 3α² + 2αβ + β² equals 1.0081: the stationary model has no finite fourth moment, so the theoretical ACF of squared returns is undefined. The sample ACF of the finite series can still be calculated. At α = 0 the returns are independent: the sample autocorrelation of the squares is 0.049 at lag 1 and goes down to −0.050 over lags 1–20. For α > 0 the series satisfies RW3 but violates RW2: the size of today's move depends on past shocks.

        The autocorrelations of the returns are close to zero at all lags, as RW3 requires. The autocorrelations of the squared returns are positive up to lag 20: a day with a large move is followed by other days with large moves. This is the toy example of zero correlation with dependence, now in a model of returns.

        In brief

        • Left: the autocorrelations of simulated GARCH returns are close to zero at all lags.
        • Right: the autocorrelations of squared returns are positive up to lag 20.
          • a day with a large move is followed by other days with large moves.

        Check: returns and information

        Slide 13 in the PDF

        The three questions test the distinctions of the section: between RW1 and RW2, between zero autocorrelation and independence, and between rejecting a test and proving inefficiency.

        In brief

        • Can returns remain independent if their standard deviation moves from 1% to 3% on a date fixed in advance?
        • Does zero autocorrelation establish independence?
        • Why does rejecting an efficiency test not, on its own, prove that the market is inefficient?

        Answers: returns and information

        Slide 14 in the PDF

        Returns can remain independent when their standard deviation moves from 1% to 3% on a date fixed in advance: the series satisfies RW2 but violates RW1, because the distribution is no longer identical over time. Zero autocorrelation does not establish independence; in the GARCH example the returns are uncorrelated while their squares are dependent. Finally, a rejection does not on its own prove inefficiency, because every test of efficiency also tests the model of expected returns, and a wrong risk model can produce the rejection.

        In brief

        • Yes. The series satisfies RW2 but violates RW1, because the distribution is no longer identical.
        • No. In the GARCH example, return autocorrelation is zero, but squared returns are dependent.
        • Every efficiency test also tests the model of expected returns.
        • A rejection can come from a wrong risk model (the joint-hypothesis problem).

        Section summary

        Efficiency is a statement about an information set and about expected returns, and it can be tested only jointly with a model of risk. For the weak form, the three versions of the random walk separate zero correlation from independence: RW3, the weakest, allows the volatility clustering seen in returns. The next section tests RW3 with autocorrelations and variance ratios whose standard errors remain valid under such clustering.

        Autocorrelation and variance ratios

        The section tests the weak form on 16 daily series: 13 stock indices, Bitcoin, Ethereum and the EUR/RON reference rate. The first statistic is the lag-1 autocorrelation, the second the variance ratio, which accumulates the autocorrelations up to a horizon of \(q\) days. Both are tested with standard errors that remain valid when volatility clusters, and the multiplicity of horizons is handled by the Chow–Denning test.

        2. Autocorrelation and variance ratios

        Slide 15 in the PDF

        The question of the section is whether today's return predicts tomorrow's, in equity, crypto and currency markets. The lag-1 autocorrelation \(\rho_1\) measures the linear dependence between consecutive days, and the variance ratio accumulates the autocorrelations up to horizon \(q\).

        The markets are 13 stock indices, developed and emerging, including the BET, together with Bitcoin, Ethereum and the EUR/RON reference rate. The selection includes markets where predictability is more likely: where liquidity is thin, as for the BET and the crypto-assets, or where the exchange rate is managed, as for EUR/RON. All samples end on 18 September 2026. The equity series start in January 2000, EUR/RON in July 2005, Bitcoin in September 2014 and Ethereum in January 2016.

        In brief

        • The question: does today's return predict tomorrow's in equity, crypto and currency markets?
        • The lag-1 autocorrelation \(\rho_1\) measures linear dependence between consecutive days.
          • The variance ratio accumulates the autocorrelations up to horizon \(q\).
        • Markets: 13 stock indices, Bitcoin, Ethereum and the EUR/RON reference rate.
          • developed and emerging, including the BET.
        • Why this selection: predictability is more likely:
          • where liquidity is thin (BET, crypto).
          • or where the exchange rate is managed (EUR/RON).
        • Samples: all to 18 September 2026.
          • equities from January 2000, EUR/RON from July 2005.
          • Bitcoin from September 2014 and Ethereum from January 2016.

        Lag-1 Autocorrelation Across Markets (1/3)

        Slide 16 in the PDF

        The chart shows, for each of the 16 markets, the lag-1 autocorrelation \(\hat\rho_1\) of daily log returns over its full sample, with its 95% confidence interval. Around zero, two bands serve for testing: the robust test band and the narrower band implied by i.i.d. returns. The next slide explains how they are built and how to read them.

        In the interactive version, the slider changes the lag \(k\) from 1 to 10, and the ▶ button runs through the lags.

          Question for the room In a hypothetical example, the estimated autocorrelation is −0.040, the i.i.d. standard error is 0.012, and the robust standard error under H₀ is 0.027. Using the asymptotic two-sided cutoff of 1.96, which test rejects H₀: ρ₁ = 0?
          Answer

          Only the test using the i.i.d. standard error. With the i.i.d. standard error, |z| = 0.040/0.012 = 3.33; with the robust error, |z| = 0.040/0.027 = 1.48. The decision depends on the estimate relative to its standard error, not on the autocorrelation's magnitude alone. For the S&P 500, ρ̂₁ = −0.099 lies outside both test bands, with the same standard errors (0.027 robust, 0.012 i.i.d.); for the Euro Stoxx 50 or the Nikkei 225, ρ̂₁ falls outside the i.i.d. band but inside the robust one. The bars around the estimates are confidence intervals with the HAC standard error of the estimator (S&P 500: 0.030); they serve for comparisons, not for the test of ρ₁ = 0.

          The rule for reading the chart is to compare each dot with the robust band, not with the i.i.d. band and not with the confidence interval. The markets whose dots lie outside the robust band are listed on the third slide.

          In brief

          MFM_ch2_autocorrelation

          Lag-1 Autocorrelation Across Markets (2/3)

          Slide 17 in the PDF

          Each dot is the lag-1 autocorrelation \(\hat\rho_1\) of one market's daily log returns. The coloured bar is the 95% confidence interval \(\hat\rho_1 \pm 1.96\,\mathrm{SE}_{\mathrm{HAC}}\), where \(\mathrm{SE}_{\mathrm{HAC}}\) is the heteroskedasticity and autocorrelation consistent (HAC) standard error of the estimator, valid also when \(\rho_1 \neq 0\). The light band is the robust test band \(0 \pm 1.96\,\mathrm{SE}_0\), where \(\mathrm{SE}_0\) is derived under the null hypothesis \(H_0\) of an unpredictable mean. The ticks mark the i.i.d. band \(0 \pm 1.96/\sqrt{T}\), where \(T\) is the number of daily returns.

          The test and the interval answer different questions, and they can disagree. We reject \(\rho_1 = 0\) at 5% if the dot lies outside the robust test band. For the Nikkei 225 and the PX, the HAC interval excludes 0, but \(\hat\rho_1\) stays inside the test band, so we do not reject.

          The i.i.d. band is too narrow under volatility clustering. In all 16 series, \(\mathrm{SE}_0\) exceeds the i.i.d. value; for the S&P 500, with \(T = 6\,717\) daily returns, it is 0.027 against 0.012. The abbreviations BIST, WIG20, BUX, PX, IPC and BET denote the stock-exchange indices of Istanbul, Warsaw, Budapest, Prague, Mexico and Bucharest, and FX (foreign exchange) denotes the EUR/RON reference rate.

          SE: Standard Error; HAC: Heteroskedasticity and Autocorrelation Consistent; CI: Confidence Interval.

          Lag-1 Autocorrelation Across Markets (3/3)

          Slide 18 in the PDF

          Four series have positive and significant autocorrelations: the BET (\(\hat\rho_1 = 0.109\)), the IPC (0.071), the Nifty 50 (0.048) and EUR/RON (0.170). The S&P 500 has a negative and significant autocorrelation, \(\hat\rho_1 = -0.099\). For Bitcoin (\(-0.022\)) and Ethereum (\(-0.012\)) the estimates lie inside the robust test band, so we do not reject \(\rho_1 = 0\).

          The slide proposes a mechanism for each finding. For the BET, persistence can come from thin trading in some stocks, whose prices absorb news with a delay. For EUR/RON, it can come from the managed float, in which the central bank smooths exchange-rate moves. For the S&P 500, the reversal can come from short-term overreaction and liquidity provision. These mechanisms are not tested here: what the result supports is description.

          The effects are also small. The square \(\hat\rho_1^2\), the share of today's return variance explained by yesterday's return, is 0.98% for the S&P 500 and 1.19% for the BET. Both robust standard errors require a finite fourth moment of returns, an assumption that Chapter 1 questions.

          In brief

          • BET (\(\hat\rho_1 = 0.109\)), IPC (\(0.071\)), Nifty 50 (\(0.048\)) and EUR/RON (\(0.170\)): positive and significant autocorrelations.
          • S&P 500: \(\hat\rho_1 = -0.099\), negative and significant.
          • Bitcoin (\(\hat\rho_1 = -0.022\)) and Ethereum (\(-0.012\)): inside the robust test band, so we do not reject \(\rho_1 = 0\).
          • For the BET, persistence can come from thin trading in some stocks, whose prices absorb news with a delay.
          • For EUR/RON, it can come from the managed float, in which the central bank smooths exchange-rate moves.
          • For the S&P 500, the reversal can come from short-term overreaction and liquidity provision.

          The Variance Ratio: Why and What It Measures

          Slide 19 in the PDF

          The lag-1 autocorrelation looks at a single lag. Small autocorrelations at many lags can each pass the test and still accumulate over longer horizons. The variance ratio (VR) of Lo & MacKinlay (1988) measures this accumulation. Over a horizon of \(q\) days,

          \[\mathrm{VR}(q) = \frac{\mathrm{Var}(r_t + \dots + r_{t-q+1})}{q\,\mathrm{Var}(r_t)},\]
          (2.3)

          the variance of the \(q\)-day return divided by \(q\) times the variance of the daily return; \(q = 5\) is one trading week. Without autocorrelation, the variance grows linearly with the horizon, as in the random-walk model, and \(\mathrm{VR}(q) = 1\). The estimate \(\widehat{\mathrm{VR}}(q)\) tends to 1 even when volatility varies over time, as under RW2 and RW3.

          For a covariance-stationary series, the ratio is a weighted sum of autocorrelations,

          \[\mathrm{VR}(q) = 1 + 2\sum_{k=1}^{q-1}\Big(1 - \frac{k}{q}\Big)\rho_k,\]
          (2.4)

          with weights that fall with the lag \(k\). A value \(\mathrm{VR}(q) > 1\) signals persistence, a value below 1 reversal (mean reversion). For \(q = 2\), (2.4) gives \(\mathrm{VR}(2) = 1 + \rho_1\): on the S&P 500, \(1 - 0.099 = 0.901\), equal to \(\widehat{\mathrm{VR}}(2)\) estimated directly from (2.3).

          In brief

          VR: Variance Ratio.
          • Why another test: \(\hat\rho_1\) looks at a single lag.
            • small autocorrelations at many lags can each pass the test yet accumulate over longer horizons.
          • Variance ratio over a horizon of \(q\) days Lo & MacKinlay (1988): \(\text{VR}(q) = \dfrac{\Var(r_t + \dots + r_{t-q+1})}{q\,\Var(r_t)}\).
            • equal to 1 without autocorrelation.
            • \(q = 5\): one trading week.
          • Without autocorrelation, \(\widehat{\text{VR}}(q)\) tends to 1 even when volatility varies over time (RW2, RW3).
          • For a covariance-stationary series, \(\text{VR}(q) = 1 + 2 \sum_{k=1}^{q-1} \big(1 - \tfrac{k}{q}\big) \rho_k\).
            • a weighted sum of autocorrelations, with weights that fall with the lag \(k\).
          • \(\text{VR}(q) > 1\) signals persistence, \(\text{VR}(q) < 1\) reversal (mean reversion).
          • For \(q = 2\), \(\text{VR}(2) = 1 + \rho_1\): on the S&P 500, \(1 - 0.099 = 0.901\), equal to \(\widehat{\text{VR}}(2)\) estimated directly.

          The robust variance-ratio band

          Slide 20 in the PDF

          The test of the variance ratio uses a band centred at 1, the value under the null. With \(T\) daily returns, the robust 95% null band is

          \[1 \pm 1.96\sqrt{\hat\theta(q)/T},\]
          (2.5)

          where \(\hat\theta(q)\) is the estimated variance of \(\sqrt{T}\,(\widehat{\mathrm{VR}}(q) - 1)\), robust to heteroskedasticity. If \(\widehat{\mathrm{VR}}(q)\) falls outside the band, we reject the null at 5%.

          The band is not a confidence interval: a confidence interval would be centred at the estimate \(\widehat{\mathrm{VR}}(q)\), not at 1. Its width responds to two features of the data. When large moves follow large moves (volatility clustering), \(\hat\theta(q)\) grows and the band widens. \(\hat\theta(q)\) also grows with \(q\), so the band widens at long horizons, because a sample contains fewer independent \(q\)-day periods.

          In brief

          • The robust 95% band is centred at 1, the value under the null.
            • if \(\widehat{\text{VR}}(q)\) falls outside it, we reject the null at 5%.
          • The band is not a confidence interval.
            • a confidence interval would be centred at the estimate \(\widehat{\text{VR}}(q)\), not at 1.
          • The robust null band is \(1 \pm 1.96\sqrt{\hat\theta(q)/T}\) (test construction).
            • \(T\): the number of daily returns.
            • \(\hat\theta(q)\): the estimated variance of \(\sqrt{T}\,(\widehat{\text{VR}}(q) - 1)\), robust to heteroskedasticity.
          • When large moves follow large moves (volatility clustering), \(\hat\theta(q)\) grows and the band widens.
          • \(\hat\theta(q)\) also grows with \(q\), so the band widens at long horizons.
            • a sample contains fewer independent \(q\)-day periods.
          Further Topics: a numerical check

          Which variance-ratio horizons do we test?

          Slide 21 in the PDF

          Reversal or persistence can appear at one day, a week or a month, and we do not know in advance at which. The chapter tests four horizons, \(q = 2, 5, 10, 20\) trading days: two days, about one week, two weeks and one trading month. The choice follows Lo & MacKinlay (1988), who use \(q = 2, 4, 8, 16\) on weekly data; other horizons can give a different result.

          The robust null band (2.5) has level 5% at each horizon separately. With four tests, a false rejection occurs more often than 5% of the time. The Chow–Denning test (Chow & Denning, 1993) corrects this by using the largest robust statistic \(|z^*(q)|\) across the four horizons, with a critical value of 2.49 instead of 1.96.

          With this correction, the test rejects RW3 for 5 of the 16 series, including the BET and the S&P 500. For Bitcoin it does not reject RW3 (p-value 0.835). Like the autocorrelation test, \(z^*\) and the Chow–Denning test require a finite fourth moment, which Chapter 1 questions.

          In brief

          • Why several horizons: reversal or persistence can appear at one day, a week or a month.
            • we do not know in advance at which.
          • We test four horizons, \(q = 2, 5, 10, 20\) trading days.
            • two days, about one week, two weeks and one trading month.
          • The choice follows Lo & MacKinlay (1988), who use \(q = 2, 4, 8, 16\) on weekly data.
            • other horizons can give a different result.
          • The robust null band has level 5% at each horizon separately.
            • with four tests, a false rejection occurs more often than 5% of the time.
          • The Chow–Denning test Chow & Denning (1993) corrects this.
            • it uses the largest \(|z^*(q)|\) across the four horizons.
            • The critical value is \(2.49\) instead of \(1.96\).
          • The Chow–Denning test rejects RW3 for 5 of the 16 series, including the BET and the S&P 500.
          • For Bitcoin, the Chow–Denning test does not reject RW3 (p-value \(= 0.835\)).
          • \(z^*\) and the Chow–Denning test require a finite fourth moment, which Chapter 1 questions.

            Question for the room For the S&P 500, the 20-day variance ratio is 0.754. For this value, what is the standard-error boundary below which |(VR − 1)/SE| exceeds 1.96?
            Answer

            The boundary is |0.754 − 1|/1.96 = 0.1255. The value 0.2460 is the deviation from the null before division by the cutoff. The value 0.1328 is the actual robust standard error at 20 days; it exceeds the boundary, so the test does not reject. The null band widens with q: its lower edge is 0.817 at 10 days, where VR = 0.767 is rejected, and 0.740 at 20 days. The calculation uses overlapping aggregated returns and the robust Lo–MacKinlay standard error.

            A profile above the band signals persistence that accumulates with the horizon, a profile below it reversal. The band widens with \(q\), so a deviation of the same size is less significant at long horizons; the values for each market are on the next slide.

            Check: autocorrelation and variance ratios

            Slide 24 in the PDF

            The questions concern the standard error under volatility clustering, the gap between statistical and economic significance, the meaning of a variance ratio below one, and a transfer exercise in which the standard error of a variance-ratio estimate doubles.

            In brief

            • Why can an i.i.d. band be misleading under volatility clustering?
            • Does positive autocorrelation imply profit after costs?
            • What does a variance ratio below one suggest?
            • Transfer: a test statistic has magnitude 2.4.
              • If the VR estimate stays the same but its standard error doubles, what does the magnitude become?
            • Is the null still rejected at 5% (two-sided)?

            Answers: autocorrelation and variance ratios

            Slide 25 in the PDF

            Under volatility clustering, the i.i.d. standard error ignores the dependence in squared returns, so it is too small and the test rejects the null too often. A positive autocorrelation does not imply profit after costs: effect size, risk, implementation and costs still matter. A variance ratio below 1 suggests reversal over the chosen horizon, and calling the deviation significant requires a robust standard error. In the transfer exercise, a statistic of magnitude 2.4 halves to 1.2 when the standard error doubles; 1.2 does not cross 1.96, so the null is no longer rejected. The estimated effect is unchanged; only the evidence for it is weaker.

            In brief

            • The i.i.d. standard error ignores dependence in squared returns, so it is too small.
              • The test rejects the null too often.
            • No: effect size, risk, implementation and costs still matter.
            • A variance ratio below 1 suggests reversal over the chosen horizon.
            • To call the deviation significant, we need a robust standard error.
            • The statistic halves to 1.2.
            • No: 1.2 does not cross 1.96.
              • the estimated effect is unchanged, its evidence is weaker.

            Section summary

            Four of the 16 series have a significant positive lag-1 autocorrelation and the S&P 500 a significant negative one, while Bitcoin and Ethereum stay inside the robust band. The variance-ratio profiles confirm reversal on the S&P 500 and persistence on the BET over several horizons. The effects are small, and the proposed mechanisms are not tested. The next section asks a different question: whether a predictor forecasts returns on data not used to estimate it.

            Out-of-sample predictability

            The section moves from the dependence of returns on their own past to forecasting with another variable, the log dividend–price ratio. The test is out of sample: each forecast uses only the data available when it is made, and it is scored against the historical mean with the out-of-sample \(R^2\) and the Clark–West test.

            3. Out-of-sample predictability

            Slide 26 in the PDF

            The question is whether a variable known today forecasts next month's market return. The classic candidate is \(dp\), the log dividend–price ratio: dividends over the last 12 months divided by the average index level in that month.

            In sample, regressions of returns on such variables appear to work. On new data, most published predictors fail (Welch & Goyal, 2008). The proper test is therefore one in which every forecast uses only the data available when it is made.

            In brief

            • The question: does a variable known today forecast next month's market return?
            • The classic candidate is \(dp\), the log dividend–price ratio.
              • dividends over the last 12 months divided by the average index level in that month.
            • In sample, such regressions appear to work.
              • on new data, most published predictors fail Welch & Goyal (2008).
            • The proper test: every forecast uses only the data available when it is made.
            Further Topics: why the dividend–price ratio

            How we build the forecasts

            Slide 27 in the PDF

            Let \(r\) be the monthly S&P 500 log return, with dividends, minus the 3-month US Treasury bill rate. Each month \(t\), the predictive regression

            \[r_{s+1} = \alpha + \beta\, dp_s + u_{s+1}\]
            (2.6)

            is estimated on all months up to \(t\); at the next step the window grows by one month (an expanding window). The out-of-sample (OS) forecast for month \(t+1\) is \(\hat r_{t+1} = \hat\alpha + \hat\beta\, dp_t\).

            The competing forecast is the historical mean \(\bar r_{t+1}\), the mean of the returns up to month \(t\), which corresponds to no predictability. The first forecast is for January 1970, which gives 680 monthly forecasts.

            One qualification applies. Monthly dividends are interpolated from quarterly totals, so \(dp_t\) was not known exactly in month \(t\), and the exercise is pseudo-out-of-sample.

            In brief

            OS: Out-of-Sample.
            • \(r\): the monthly S&P 500 log return, with dividends, minus the 3-month US Treasury bill rate.
            • Each month \(t\) we estimate \(r_{s+1} = \alpha + \beta\, dp_s + u_{s+1}\) on all months up to \(t\).
              • at the next step the window grows by one month (expanding window).
            • The \(dp\) forecast for month \(t+1\): \(\hat r_{t+1} = \hat\alpha + \hat\beta\, dp_t\).
            • The competing forecast: the historical mean \(\bar r_{t+1}\).
              • The mean of returns up to month \(t\).
              • it corresponds to no predictability.
            • The first forecast is for January 1970, giving 680 monthly forecasts.
            • Monthly dividends are interpolated from quarterly totals.
              • so \(dp_t\) was not known exactly in month \(t\).
              • the exercise is pseudo-out-of-sample.

            How we score the result: \(R^2_{OS}\)

            Slide 28 in the PDF

            The forecasts are scored by the out-of-sample \(R^2\),

            \[R^2_{OS} = 1 - \frac{\sum_t (r_t - \hat r_t)^2}{\sum_t (r_t - \bar r_t)^2},\]
            (2.7)

            with the sums over the forecast months. A positive \(R^2_{OS}\) means that the \(dp\) forecast has smaller squared errors than the historical mean; a negative one, that the historical mean forecasts better. Unlike the in-sample \(R^2\), \(R^2_{OS}\) can be negative.

            The two measures differ because, in sample, \(\hat\beta\) also fits the noise of the months it explains. A positive \(R^2_{OS}\) can also arise by chance, so its significance is tested separately, with the Clark–West test reported in the results.

            In brief

            • \(R^2_{OS} = 1 - \sum_t (r_t - \hat r_t)^2 \big/ \sum_t (r_t - \bar r_t)^2\), with the sums over the forecast months.
            • \(R^2_{OS} > 0\): the \(dp\) forecast has smaller squared errors than the historical mean.
            • \(R^2_{OS} < 0\): the historical mean forecasts better.
              • unlike the in-sample \(R^2\), \(R^2_{OS}\) can be negative.
            • Why the two differ: in sample, \(\hat\beta\) also fits the noise of the months it explains.
            • A positive \(R^2_{OS}\) can also arise by chance.
              • we test its significance separately (the Clark–West test, in the results).

            Reading the cumulative squared-error chart

            Slide 29 in the PDF

            The chart of the next slide accumulates the difference between the two forecasts month by month. Each month from 1970 adds \((r_t - \bar r_t)^2 - (r_t - \hat r_t)^2\), the squared error of the historical mean minus that of the \(dp\) forecast.

            The curve therefore rises in months in which \(dp\) forecasts better and falls in months in which it forecasts worse, and its final value has the sign of \(R^2_{OS}\) in (2.7). On the vertical axis is the running sum, with returns as fractions (0.01 = 1%), squared.

            In brief

            • Each month from 1970 adds \((r_t - \bar r_t)^2 - (r_t - \hat r_t)^2\).
              • the squared error of the historical mean minus that of the \(dp\) forecast.
            • The curve rises in months in which \(dp\) forecasts better and falls in months in which it forecasts worse.
            • The final value of the curve has the sign of \(R^2_{OS}\).
            • Vertical axis: the running sum, with returns as fractions (0.01 = 1%), squared.

            The Dividend–Price Ratio: Out-of-Sample Forecasts

            Slide 30 in the PDF

            The chart shows the cumulative difference of squared errors between the historical mean and the \(dp\) forecast for the S&P 500, month by month from 1970.

            In the interactive version, the slider sets the year in which the evaluation starts, from 1970 to 2015, and the menu switches between the unrestricted \(dp\) forecast and the forecast with the Campbell–Thompson restrictions.

              Question for the room Over 680 monthly forecasts from 1970, R²OS is −0.51%. Does it turn positive if the evaluation starts later?
              Answer

              No. For every start between 1970 and 2015, R²OS stays negative: −1.37% from 1990, −0.01% from 2000, −2.75% from 2010. The curve rises until 1990, and the 1990s boom wipes out the advantage. The Clark–West test over 1970–2026 gives t = 0.42 (p = 0.34), so the absence of predictability is not rejected.

              The value at the right edge of the curve has the sign of \(R^2_{OS}\), and the slopes of the curve show in which periods \(dp\) helped or hurt. The results for the full sample are on the next slide.

              In brief

              MFM_ch2_predictability

              The Dividend–Price Ratio: Results for the S&P 500 (1/2)

              Slide 31 in the PDF

              The curve ends below zero: over the 680 monthly forecasts, \(R^2_{OS} = -0.51\%\). The historical mean forecast better than the \(dp\) regression over the whole evaluation period.

              The Clark–West test (Clark & West, 2007) compares the squared errors and corrects a handicap of the \(dp\) model: even if \(\beta = 0\), estimating \(\beta\) adds noise to the forecast. On the S&P 500 the test gives \(t = 0.42\), with a one-sided p-value of 0.34, so we do not reject the absence of predictability. The test on squared errors requires a finite fourth moment, which Chapter 1 questions.

              The path of the curve shows when the relation worked. Until 1990 the curve rises: \(dp\) forecast better than the historical mean. In the 1990s boom, \(dp\) fell to record lows while prices kept rising, and the advantage was lost. In the 2000s it partly returned, and after 2010 it was lost again. What the result supports is a comparison of procedures.

              In brief

              • The curve ends below zero: over 680 monthly forecasts, \(R^2_{OS} = -0.51\%\).
              • The Clark–West test Clark & West (2007) compares the squared errors and corrects a handicap of the \(dp\) model.
                • even if \(\beta = 0\), estimating \(\beta\) adds noise to the forecast.
              • On the S&P 500 the test gives \(t = 0.42\) (one-sided \(p = 0.34\)).
                • we do not reject the absence of predictability.
                • the test on squared errors requires a finite fourth moment, which Chapter 1 questions.
              • Until 1990 the curve rises: \(dp\) forecast better than the historical mean.
              • In the 1990s boom, \(dp\) fell to record lows while prices kept rising: the advantage was lost.
              • In the 2000s the advantage partly returned, and after 2010 it was lost again.
              • What the result supports: comparison of procedures.

              The Dividend–Price Ratio: Results for the S&P 500 (2/2)

              Slide 32 in the PDF

              A small \(R^2_{OS}\) can still matter economically. Campbell & Thompson (2008) show that even 0.5% is a lot for an investor: a mean–variance investor who raises equity exposure when the forecast is high and cuts it when it is low would raise the expected return by about 20%. The calculation uses the monthly market Sharpe ratio, 0.160; the formula is in the appendix.

              Campbell and Thompson also impose two restrictions on the forecast: \(\hat\beta\) must have the sign that theory predicts, and the forecast of the equity premium must be non-negative. On the S&P 500 the restrictions bind in 5.7% of months and leave \(R^2_{OS}\) at \(-0.51\%\), so the economic gain is absent.

              In brief

              • Does a small \(R^2_{OS}\) matter economically? Campbell & Thompson (2008) show that it does: even 0.5% is a lot for an investor.
              • A mean–variance investor would raise the expected return by about 20%.
                • who raises equity exposure when the forecast is high and cuts it when it is low.
                • formula: Appendix.
              • The calculation uses the monthly market Sharpe ratio, \(0.160\).
              • Campbell and Thompson also impose two restrictions.
                • \(\hat\beta\) with the sign theory predicts.
                • a non-negative equity-premium forecast.
              • On the S&P 500 the restrictions bind in \(5.7\%\) of months and leave \(R^2_{OS}\) at \(-0.51\%\).
                • the economic gain is absent.

              Check: out-of-sample predictability

              Slide 33 in the PDF

              The questions ask what a negative \(R^2_{OS}\) means, why a regression with a good in-sample fit can fail on new data, whether a profitable strategy can have a negative \(R^2_{OS}\), and what the loss of the \(dp\) advantage in the 1990s says about the stability of the relation.

              In brief

              • What does a negative \(R^2_{OS}\) mean?
              • Why can a regression with a good in-sample \(R^2\) fail on new data?
              • Can a profitable strategy have a negative \(R^2_{OS}\)?
              • What does the loss of the \(dp\) advantage in the 1990s say about the stability of the relation?

              Answers: out-of-sample predictability

              Slide 34 in the PDF

              A negative \(R^2_{OS}\) means that the historical mean forecast better than the model over the months evaluated. A regression with a good in-sample \(R^2\) can fail on new data because, in sample, \(\hat\beta\) also fits the noise, and on new data the noise does not repeat. A profitable strategy can have a negative \(R^2_{OS}\): its profit can come from constant market exposure or from a few correct sign calls, not from small squared errors. The loss of the advantage in the 1990s shows that the relation is not stable: a predictor that was useful until 1990 lost all its advantage when \(dp\) fell to record lows.

              In brief

              • The historical mean forecast better than the model over the months evaluated.
              • In sample, \(\hat\beta\) also fits the noise.
                • on new data the noise does not repeat.
              • Yes: profit can come from constant market exposure or from a few correct sign calls.
                • not from small squared errors.
              • The relation is not stable.
                • a predictor that was useful until 1990 loses all its advantage when \(dp\) falls to record lows.

              Section summary

              On 680 monthly forecasts since 1970, the dividend–price ratio does not beat the historical mean for the S&P 500: \(R^2_{OS}\) is negative and the Clark–West test does not reject the absence of predictability. The relation worked until 1990 and was unstable afterwards. The next section turns to the semi-strong form and asks how prices react to a specific public announcement.

              Event studies

              The autocorrelation and predictability tests of the previous sections use the history of prices. An event study turns to semi-strong efficiency: it asks how a price reacts to a single, precisely dated piece of public news. The method separates the part of a return explained by the market from the abnormal part and sums the abnormal part over a window around the announcement. The section's example is the tax on bank assets announced in Romania in December 2018.

              4. Event studies

              Slide 35 in the PDF

              The question of the section is how fast and how fully a price absorbs a precisely dated piece of public news. Answering it requires a counterfactual: the return the stock would have earned had the news not arrived. The difference between the observed return and this expected return is the abnormal return (AR). An event study measures abnormal returns around the announcement and is therefore a test of semi-strong efficiency, the form that concerns all public information (Fama, Fisher, Jensen & Roll, 1969).

              The section's example is the tax on bank assets announced in December 2018. It is a useful test case because the news was not anticipated and hit the listed banks directly, so the reaction of their shares can be dated to a single day.

              In brief

              • The question: how fast and how fully does the price absorb a precisely dated piece of public news?
              • To answer it, we need the return the stock would have earned without the news.
              • The difference between the observed return and this expected return is the abnormal return (AR).
              • An event study measures AR around the news and so tests semi-strong efficiency Fama, Fisher, Jensen & Roll (1969).
              • The section's example: the bank tax announced in December 2018.
                • unanticipated news that hit the listed banks directly.

              The Event-Study Method (1/2)

              Slide 36 in the PDF

              The expected return is given by the market model, a regression of the firm's daily return on the return of the market index:

              \[r_{i,t} = a_i + b_i\, r_{m,t} + \varepsilon_{i,t},\]
              (2.8)

              where \(a_i\) is the intercept, \(b_i\) the market beta of firm \(i\) and \(r_{m,t}\) the return of the market index on day \(t\). The model removes from the firm's return the part explained by the market move of the same day. With daily data, models with more factors reduce the variance of the abnormal return only slightly (MacKinlay, 1997), so the one-factor model is the usual choice.

              The timeline separates two windows. The estimation window, days \(-250\) to \(-11\) relative to the event, covers about one trading year and serves to estimate \(a_i\) and \(b_i\). It stops ten days before the event so that any leak of information before the announcement does not enter the estimates. The event window, days \(0\) to \(+5\), contains the announcement day and five more trading days, which shows whether the reaction continues or reverses.

              The Event-Study Method (2/2)

              Slide 37 in the PDF

              With the coefficients estimated in the estimation window, the abnormal return of firm \(i\) on day \(t\) is the residual of the market model,

              \[AR_{i,t} = r_{i,t} - \hat a_i - \hat b_i\, r_{m,t},\]
              (2.9)

              and the cumulative abnormal return (CAR) over the days \(\tau_1\) to \(\tau_2\) is their sum,

              \[CAR_i[\tau_1, \tau_2] = \sum_{t=\tau_1}^{\tau_2} AR_{i,t}.\]
              (2.10)

              A first-pass test divides the CAR by its standard deviation under the null of no effect, \(t = CAR_i/(\hat\sigma_\varepsilon\sqrt{L})\) (MacKinlay, 1997), where \(\hat\sigma_\varepsilon\) is the standard deviation of the residuals in the estimation window and \(L\) the number of days summed. The formula treats \(\hat a_i\) and \(\hat b_i\) as known and so ignores their estimation error; the worked example shows when the correction matters.

              For unanticipated news, efficiency predicts a jump on day 0 and neither a predictable drift nor a reversal afterwards. Daily data can show only whether the news is absorbed within the day; speed at the scale of microseconds requires intraday data, treated in Chapter 10. The literature on the method is listed in the Appendix, and an application to the price response of Ethereum to network development milestones is on Quantinar.

              In brief

              • Abnormal return: \(AR_{i,t} = r_{i,t} - \hat a_i - \hat b_i r_{m,t}\), with \(\hat a_i\), \(\hat b_i\) estimated in the estimation window.
              • Cumulative abnormal return (CAR): \(CAR_i[\tau_1, \tau_2] = \sum_{t=\tau_1}^{\tau_2} AR_{i,t}\), where \([\tau_1, \tau_2]\) are the days summed.
              • First-pass test: \(t = CAR_i / (\hat\sigma_{\varepsilon} \sqrt{L})\) MacKinlay (1997).
                • \(\hat\sigma_\varepsilon\): the standard deviation of the residuals in the estimation window.
                • \(L\): the number of days summed.
              • The formula ignores the uncertainty in \(\hat a_i\) and \(\hat b_i\).
                • its correction appears in the worked example.
              • For unanticipated news, efficiency predicts a jump on day 0 and no predictable drift or reversal afterwards
              • Daily data can only show absorption within a day.
                • speed at the microsecond scale needs intraday data (Chapter 10).
                • literature: Appendix.
              • Application: the price response of Ethereum to network development milestones
                • Quantinar: Market Responses to ETH Development Milestones

              The Bank Tax: the Data and How to Read the Chart

              Slide 38 in the PDF

              The government announced the tax on bank assets on the evening of 18 December 2018, so event day 0 is 19 December. The example studies Banca Transilvania (TLV), BRD and their equal-weighted portfolio, with the return of the BET index as the market return \(r_{m,t}\).

              The chart has two panels. The left panel shows the daily abnormal returns, in percent, from day \(-10\) to day \(+5\). The light-blue band marks \(\pm 1.96\) standard deviations of the portfolio's prediction error, so a portfolio bar outside the band is significant at 5%. The right panel shows the CAR from day 0 up to the day on the horizontal axis, in percent. A curve that moves back towards zero after day 0 indicates that the initial reaction was reversed.

              In brief

              • The government announced the tax on bank assets on the evening of 18 December 2018.
                • event day 0 is 19 December.
              • We study Banca Transilvania (TLV), BRD and their equal-weighted portfolio.
                • \(r_{m,t}\) is the return of the BET index.
              • Left panel: daily \(AR\), in percent, from day \(-10\) to day \(+5\).
              • Light-blue band: \(\pm 1.96\) standard deviations of the portfolio's prediction error.
                • a portfolio bar outside it is significant at 5%.
              • Right panel: \(CAR\) from day 0 up to the day on the horizontal axis, in percent.
              • A curve that returns towards zero after day 0 indicates a reversed reaction.

              Worked Example: the Romanian Bank Tax of December 2018

              Slide 39 in the PDF

              The interactive chart reproduces the two panels. The first slider changes the length of the estimation window, between 60 and 240 days; the other two set the first day \(\tau_1\) and the last day \(\tau_2\) of the CAR. The ▶ button extends the CAR window day by day up to day \(+5\).

                Question for the room TLV's CAR[0,5] is −1.1%, and its estimated beta is 1.18. Keep TLV returns, alpha and beta unchanged, but increase the sum of BET log returns over the window by 1 percentage point. What is TLV's new CAR?
                Answer

                CAR falls by 1.18 × 1 percentage point because the benchmark return is subtracted after multiplication by beta: −1.1% − 1.18% = −2.28%. The +0.08% option reverses the adjustment's sign. The −1.1% option ignores the benchmark. CAR can change without any change in TLV prices. In the data, AR₀ = −8.3% and the CAR over days +1 to +5 is +7.2% (t = 3.82), although over days 0 to +5 the log return is −15.6% for TLV and −12.7% for the BET. The test for CAR[0,5] gives t = −0.48 with the prediction error and t = −0.54 with σ̂√L. A single event cannot separate overreaction, later news and a benchmark effect.

                On day 0 every bar lies far outside the band, and the CAR curve of TLV moves back towards zero over the following five sessions. The numbers behind the chart, and the three explanations of the reversal, are on the next two frames.

                In brief

                MFM_ch2_seminar

                The Bank Tax of December 2018: Results (1/2)

                Slide 40 in the PDF

                For TLV, the estimated market beta is \(1.18\), and the residual standard deviation in the estimation window is \(0.83\%\) per day. On day 0 the abnormal return of TLV is \(AR_0 = -8.3\%\), with \(t = -10.0\) by the first-pass formula. The abnormal returns of the same day are \(-5.6\%\) for BRD and \(-6.9\%\) for the equal-weighted portfolio.

                The market itself fell sharply that day: the log return of the BET was \(-11.9\%\), a fall in the index of \(11.2\%\). With such an extreme market return, the uncertainty in \(\hat b\) matters, because the error of the predicted return grows with the distance of \(r_{m,0}\) from its mean in the estimation window. For TLV, the standard deviation of the prediction error rises from \(0.83\%\) to \(1.21\%\), and the \(t\) statistic falls to \(-6.85\). The effect remains strongly significant.

                One further caveat concerns the benchmark. TLV and BRD carry large weights in the BET, so the market return of day 0 already contains part of the shock to the banks. Measured against this index, \(AR_0\) understates the fall.

                In brief

                • For TLV, the estimated market beta is \(1.18\).
                  • the residual standard deviation in the estimation window is \(0.83\%\) per day.
                • On day 0, the abnormal return of TLV is \(AR_0 = -8.3\%\), with \(t = -10.0\) by the first-pass formula.
                • Also on day 0: \(AR_0 = -5.6\%\) for BRD and \(-6.9\%\) for the equal-weighted portfolio.
                • That day the log return of the BET was \(-11.9\%\), a fall in the index of \(11.2\%\).
                • With such an extreme market return the uncertainty in \(\hat b\) matters.
                  • the prediction-error standard deviation for TLV rises from \(0.83\%\) to \(1.21\%\).
                • With this prediction error, \(t = -6.85\).
                  • the effect remains strongly significant.
                • TLV and BRD carry large weights in the BET.
                  • so \(r_{m,0}\) already contains part of the shock.
                  • \(AR_0\) understates the fall.

                The Bank Tax of December 2018: Results (2/2)

                Slide 41 in the PDF

                Over days \(+1\) to \(+5\), the CAR of TLV is \(+7.2\%\) (\(t = 3.82\)), and over days \(0\) to \(+5\) it is only \(-1.1\%\) (\(t = -0.54\)). The abnormal component of the fall, measured against the BET, is therefore almost offset in the next five sessions. This does not mean that the share price recovered: over days 0 to \(+5\) the log return is \(-15.6\%\) for TLV and \(-12.7\%\) for the BET.

                Three explanations are consistent with the offset:

                1. An overreaction on day 0, which would contradict semi-strong efficiency.
                2. Later news about the final form of the ordinance.
                3. The benchmark moved with the event: the BET fell by \(11.9\%\) (log) on day 0, so the abnormal returns measure TLV against an index that already contains the shock; the BET did not rebound, with a log return of \(-0.8\%\) over days \(+1\) to \(+5\).

                A single event cannot separate the three explanations. What the result supports is a causal identification of the day-0 reaction only, provided no other news arrived that day; the later offset remains unexplained.

                In brief

                • Over days \(+1, \dots, +5\), the cumulative abnormal return of TLV is \(+7.2\%\) (\(t = 3.82\)).
                • Over days \(0, \dots, +5\), the cumulative abnormal return is only \(-1.1\%\) (\(t = -0.54\)).
                  • the abnormal component of TLV's fall, measured against the BET, is almost offset over the next five sessions.
                  • this does not show that the share price recovered its loss: over days 0 to +5 the log return is \(-15.6\%\) for TLV and \(-12.7\%\) for the BET benchmark.
                • Explanation 1: an overreaction on day 0, against semi-strong efficiency.
                • Explanation 2: later news about the final form of the ordinance.
                • Explanation 3: the benchmark moved with the event.
                  • TLV and BRD carry large weights in the BET, which fell by \(11.9\%\) (log) on day 0.
                  • so \(AR\) and \(CAR\) measure TLV against an index that already contains the shock.
                  • the BET did not rebound: over days \(+1\) to \(+5\) its log return is \(-0.8\%\).
                • A single event does not let us choose between the three explanations.
                • What the result supports: causal identification, of the day-0 reaction only.
                  • If no other news arrived that day; the later offset stays unexplained.

                Check: event studies

                The two questions test the design of an event study: why the event window must be fixed in advance, and whether another announcement could explain the same price movement.

                In brief

                • Why must you specify the event window?
                • Could another announcement explain the same movement?

                Answers: event studies

                Slide 43 in the PDF

                The CAR sums abnormal returns over the days of the window only, so the window determines what is measured. A window chosen after seeing the data can create an effect or hide one. Overlapping news is an alternative explanation of any reaction, and for this reason the reaction alone is not causal proof.

                In brief

                • CAR adds abnormal returns over the days of the window only.
                • A window chosen after seeing the data can create or hide an effect.
                • Yes: overlapping news is an alternative explanation, so the reaction alone is not causal proof.

                Section summary

                An event study compares the observed return with the return predicted by the market model (2.8) and sums the abnormal returns (2.9) over a window fixed in advance (2.10). On the bank tax of December 2018, the abnormal return of TLV on day 0 is \(-8.3\%\), still significant (\(t = -6.85\)) after correcting for the uncertainty in \(\hat b\). Over days 0 to \(+5\) the CAR is only \(-1.1\%\), and a single event cannot tell an overreaction from later news or from a benchmark that moved with the event.

                Multiple testing and anomalies

                A calendar anomaly is a pattern in mean returns tied to the calendar. Many such patterns have been tested on the same data, so a small \(p\)-value has to be read against the number of tests performed. The section first measures how many false anomalies pure chance produces, then corrects fifteen real tests on five equity markets with the Bonferroni and Holm procedures.

                5. Multiple testing and anomalies

                Slide 44 in the PDF

                The question of the section is whether a calendar anomaly remains significant once the number of patterns searched is taken into account. The literature has tested thousands of patterns on the same data, and some pass a test by chance alone (Harvey, Liu & Zhu, 2016). A small \(p\)-value therefore means something different after fifteen tests than after one.

                The section proceeds in two steps. A simulation first measures how many false anomalies chance produces when every null hypothesis is true. The real tests on calendar effects are then corrected with the Bonferroni and Holm procedures.

                In brief

                • The question: does a calendar anomaly remain significant?
                  • once we account for how many patterns were searched
                • The literature has tested thousands of patterns on the same data, and some pass the test by chance alone Harvey, Liu & Zhu (2016).
                • A small p-value means something different after 15 tests than after one.
                • First we measure how many false anomalies chance produces.
                  • then we correct the real tests with Bonferroni and Holm.

                Calendar effects and data snooping

                Slide 45 in the PDF

                A calendar effect is a difference in mean returns associated with a day or a month. Three effects are tested in the chapter. The day-of-the-week effect is a difference in mean returns across Monday to Friday (French, 1980). The January effect is a difference between the mean January return and that of the other months (Rozeff & Kinney, 1976). The turn-of-the-month (TOM) effect concerns the last trading day of a month and the first three of the next (Ariel, 1987).

                Each of these patterns was found by looking at data. Data snooping is the selection of a pattern after many alternatives have been searched on the same data; the selected pattern then looks significant even if no effect exists. The rest of the section quantifies this risk and corrects for it.

                In brief

                • A calendar effect is a difference in mean returns associated with a day or a month.
                • Day-of-the-week effect: mean returns differ across Monday, Tuesday, …, Friday French (1980).
                • January effect: the mean January return differs from that of the other months Rozeff & Kinney (1976).
                • Turn of the month (TOM): the last trading day of a month and the first three of the next Ariel (1987).
                • Data snooping: selecting a pattern after searching many alternatives on the same data.

                How Many “Anomalies” Does Pure Chance Produce? (1/3)

                Slide 46 in the PDF

                A simulation is useful here because every null hypothesis in it is true, so every rejection is a false one. The experiment uses 2,000 trading rules with no economic content, applied to daily S&P 500 returns. Each rule picks 20% of the days at random and calls them "good days", and a \(t\) test compares the mean return on these days with the mean on the other days.

                The chart shows the histogram of the 2,000 \(t\) statistics over the standard Normal density. Two pairs of vertical lines mark the thresholds \(|t| = 1.96\), the usual 5% level, and \(|t| = 3.0\), the stricter bar proposed by Harvey, Liu & Zhu (2016).

                In brief

                • Why we simulate: here every null hypothesis is true, so every rejection is false.
                • Experiment: 2 000 trading rules with no economic content, on daily S&P 500 returns.
                • Each rule picks 20% of the days at random and calls them “good days”.
                • For each rule, a \(t\) test compares the mean return on “good days” with the mean on the other days.
                • The chart: the histogram of the 2 000 \(t\) statistics, over the standard Normal density.
                • The vertical lines: the thresholds \(|t| = 1.96\) (5%) and \(|t| = 3.0\), the bar proposed by Harvey, Liu & Zhu (2016).

                How Many “Anomalies” Does Pure Chance Produce? (2/3)

                Slide 47 in the PDF

                In the interactive version, the first slider sets the number of rules tried, from 20 to 2,000, and the menu sets the share of "good days", from 5% to 50%. The ▶ button adds rules step by step, and "New rules" draws a new set of random rules.

                  Question for the room For 2,000 tests with valid p-values, which individual Bonferroni cutoff guarantees at most a 5% probability of any false rejection, regardless of dependence between tests?
                  Answer

                  The cutoff is 0.05/2,000 = 0.000025. The value 0.025 divides the level by two rather than by the number of tests; a two-sided p-value already includes both tails. The value 0.05 retains the individual level without controlling the family. Bonferroni's guarantee does not require independent tests. In the simulation, of 2,000 rules with no economic content, 104 (5.2%) have |t| > 1.96, six have |t| > 3.0, and the best has |t| = 3.30. Adding rules to the same family cannot lower the largest |t|.

                  The histogram follows the standard Normal density closely: under the null, the \(t\) statistics behave as the theory predicts. The rules beyond the vertical lines are those that a researcher who reports only the best result would call anomalies.

                  In brief

                  MFM_ch2_multiple_testing

                  How Many “Anomalies” Does Pure Chance Produce? (3/3)

                  Slide 48 in the PDF

                  The result matches the test level: \(5.2\%\) of the random rules are "significant" at 5%, with \(|t| > 1.96\). The most spectacular rule has \(|t| = 3.30\), although it is pure chance; reported alone, it would pass even the strict bar \(|t| > 3.0\). That bar is passed by \(0.30\%\) of the random rules, 6 rules out of 2,000.

                  The lesson is that the number of rules tried, the family of tests, matters as much as the threshold chosen. A strict threshold reduces the share of false discoveries but does not remove them when the search is large enough. Sullivan, Timmermann & White (1999) show how to correct for data snooping in trading rules.

                  In brief

                  • Result: \(5.2\%\) of the rules are “significant” at 5% (\(|t| > 1.96\)), as the test level implies.
                  • The most “spectacular” rule has \(|t| = 3.30\), although it is pure chance.
                    • reported alone, it would pass even the strict bar \(|t| > 3.0\).
                  • The bar \(|t| > 3.0\) is passed by \(0.30\%\) of the random rules, i.e. 6 rules out of 2 000.
                  • How many rules were tried (the family of tests) matters as much as the bar chosen.
                  • Sullivan, Timmermann & White (1999) show how to correct for data snooping in trading rules.
