                  Reading the calendar-effects table

                  Slide 49 in the PDF

                  The same logic applies to 15 real tests on daily data from 2000: five markets times three effects, the weekday, January and TOM effects. The markets compare two developed indices, the S&P 500 and the Euro Stoxx 50, with three emerging markets of the region, the BET (Romania), the WIG20 (Poland) and the BUX (Hungary).

                  The January extra return is the mean January return minus the mean of the other months, in percent per month. The TOM extra return is the mean return on TOM days minus the mean of the other days, in basis points per day (bp; 1 bp equals 0.01 percentage points).

                  The standard errors are heteroskedasticity and autocorrelation consistent (HAC), because returns show volatility clustering: HAC errors remain valid under it, while ordinary ones do not. In these tests the HAC errors are \(0.92\) to \(1.54\) times the ordinary ones, so the correction can make them smaller as well as larger.

                  In brief

                  HAC: Heteroskedasticity and Autocorrelation Consistent.
                  • We apply the same logic to 15 real tests, on daily data from 2000.
                    • five markets \(\times\) three effects (weekday, January, TOM).
                  • We compare developed markets (S&P 500, Euro Stoxx 50) with emerging markets of the region (BET, WIG20, BUX).
                  • January extra: the mean January return minus the mean of the other months, in percent per month.
                  • TOM extra: the mean return on TOM days minus the mean of the other days.
                    • in basis points per day (bp; 1 bp \(= 0.01\) percentage points).
                  • We use HAC errors because returns show volatility clustering.
                    • HAC errors remain valid under it; ordinary ones do not.
                    • In these tests the HAC errors are \(0.92\)–\(1.54\) times the ordinary ones: smaller as well as larger.

                  January and Turn-of-the-Month Effects

                  Slide 50 in the PDF

                  The strongest results concern the turn of the month in the two smaller markets. The TOM extra return is \(+17.3\) bp per day for the BET and \(+15.1\) bp for the BUX, both with \(|t| > 3.5\), above the bar of \(3.0\) of Harvey, Liu & Zhu (2016). For the S&P 500 the TOM extra return is only \(+3.2\) bp per day, and we do not reject the null (\(t = 0.92\)).

                  The January effect is weaker. For the BET, January exceeds the other months by \(3.48\) percentage points, but the estimate is imprecise (\(t = 1.49\)). The BUX, with \(t = 2.25\), is the only market with a January-effect \(p\)-value below \(0.05\). For the day-of-the-week effect, we do not reject the null in any market; the smallest \(p\)-value is \(0.2265\), for the WIG20.

                  Fifteen tests were run, and the next frame asks which of them survive a correction for multiple testing. Even for the effects that survive, what the result supports is an association between the calendar and returns, not a mechanism.

                  Multiple Testing: Bonferroni and Holm

                  Slide 51 in the PDF

                  The error rate controlled here is the family-wise error rate (FWER), the probability of at least one false rejection in the family of tests. It is the appropriate criterion when every anomaly reported should be real, not only most of them. With \(M\) independent tests at level \(\alpha\),

                  \[\text{FWER} = 1 - (1-\alpha)^M,\]
                  (2.11)

                  so for \(M = 15\) and \(\alpha = 5\%\) the FWER would be \(53.7\%\) if the tests were independent.

                  The Bonferroni procedure rejects a hypothesis only if \(p_i < \alpha/M\); it guarantees \(\text{FWER} \le \alpha\) under any dependence between the tests. The Holm procedure sorts the \(p\)-values, \(p_{(1)} \le \dots \le p_{(M)}\), and rejects step by step while

                  \[p_{(j)} < \frac{\alpha}{M - j + 1},\]
                  (2.12)

                  stopping at the first failure. Holm has the same guarantee as Bonferroni and rejects at least every hypothesis that Bonferroni rejects. Holm applied to the calendar tests is detailed in the Appendix, and bootstrap methods that exploit the correlation between tests, such as StepM, in a further appendix frame.

                  In brief

                  FWER: Family-Wise Error Rate — the probability of at least one false rejection in the family of tests.
                  • Why FWER: we want every anomaly we report to be real, not only most of them.
                  • With \(M\) independent tests at level \(\alpha\): \(\text{FWER} = 1 - (1 - \alpha)^M\).
                  • For \(M = 15\) and \(\alpha = 5\%\), FWER would be \(53.7\%\) if the tests were independent.
                  • Bonferroni: reject only if \(p_i < \alpha / M\).
                    • it guarantees \(\text{FWER} \leq \alpha\) under any dependence between the tests.
                  • Holm: sort \(p_{(1)} \leq \dots \leq p_{(M)}\) and reject while \(p_{(j)} < \alpha/(M - j + 1)\), stopping at the first failure.
                  • Holm has the same guarantee and rejects at least everything Bonferroni rejects.
                  • Bootstrap methods that use the correlation between tests (StepM): Appendix.
                  Further Topics: Holm on the calendar tests

                  Check: multiple testing and anomalies

                  Slide 52 in the PDF

                  The three questions concern the meaning of a small \(p\)-value after a search over many rules, the meaning of a non-rejection, and the kind of new evidence that would confirm an anomaly.

                  In brief

                  • Why does testing many rules change the meaning of a small p-value?
                  • Does non-rejection prove efficiency?
                  • Which new sample or robustness check would you request?

                  Answers: multiple testing and anomalies

                  Slide 53 in the PDF

                  Searching over many rules creates more opportunities for a false rejection, so the same \(p\)-value is weaker evidence. A non-rejection does not prove efficiency: a test with low power may fail to reject even when the market is inefficient, and the conclusion concerns only the alternative tested, not every form of predictability.

                  The natural robustness check is the same rule on data after publication or on another market, where an artefact of data snooping disappears. A real effect usually weakens after publication but does not vanish (McLean & Pontiff, 2016).

                  In brief

                  • Searching many rules creates more opportunities for false rejection.
                  • No. A test with low power may fail to reject even if the market is inefficient.
                  • The conclusion concerns only the alternative tested, not every form of predictability.
                  • Example: the same rule on post-publication data or on another market.
                    • a data-snooping artefact disappears out of sample.
                  • A real effect usually weakens after publication but does not vanish McLean & Pontiff (2016).

                  Main results of the chapter

                  Slide 54 in the PDF

                  The core of the chapter yields five results. The S&P 500 shows a reversal of returns at horizons of 2 to 10 days, with variance ratios below 1, while the BET shows persistence, with variance ratios above 1 at all four horizons tested. Out of sample, the dividend–price ratio does not beat the historical mean (\(R^2_{OS} < 0\)).

                  In the event study, the abnormal component of TLV's fall after the bank tax, measured against the BET, was almost offset over the next five sessions; a single event cannot separate an overreaction from later news. Among the calendar effects, only the TOM effects of the BET and the BUX remain significant after the Holm correction.

                  In brief

                  • S&P 500: return reversal at horizons of 2–10 days (variance ratio below 1).
                  • BET: persistence (variance ratio above 1) at all four tested horizons.
                  • The dividend–price ratio does not beat the historical mean out of sample (\(R^2_{OS} < 0\)).
                  • Bank tax: the abnormal component of TLV's fall, measured against the BET, was almost offset over the next five sessions.
                    • a single event cannot separate overreaction from later news.
                  • After the Holm correction only the TOM effects for the BET and the BUX remain significant.

                  Section summary

                  Pure chance makes \(5.2\%\) of 2,000 random rules significant at 5% and lets one of them reach \(|t| = 3.30\). The FWER of fifteen independent tests at 5% would be \(53.7\%\) (2.11), and the Holm procedure (2.12) keeps it below 5% under any dependence. On the fifteen calendar tests, only the turn-of-the-month effects of the BET and the BUX survive the correction; the January effect of the BUX (\(t = 2.25\)) does not.

                  Data and conventions

                  Data Used in This Chapter

                  Slide 56 in the PDF

                  The chapter uses 16 daily series up to 18 September 2026; the sample sizes are in the Appendix. Thirteen are equity indices from 2000: four developed markets (S&P 500, Euro Stoxx 50, Nikkei 225, Hang Seng) and nine emerging or frontier markets (BET, WIG20, BUX, PX, BIST 100, Bovespa, IPC, Nifty 50, Shanghai). Bitcoin enters the full-sample and rolling tests from 2014, while the yearly crypto analysis starts in 2011. Ethereum starts in 2016, and the EUR/RON exchange rate, the reference rate of the National Bank of Romania (BNR), in July 2005.

                  Returns are daily log returns, \(r_t = \ln P_t - \ln P_{t-1}\), where \(P_t\) is the closing price, index level or exchange rate on day \(t\), each series on its own calendar. Equity indices use trading days only. A repeated close is removed only if the day shows no trading (zero volume, or open = high = low = close) or if its whole row copies the previous day's: such a day is a holiday filled with the previous price. A genuine unchanged close, with volume and an intraday range, stays as a zero return; the S&P 500 has 3 such days in about 6,700. The BET file has closes only, so none of its days is removed.

                  The IPC has 46 such days (44 of them in 2023–2026) with near-identical volume, likely duplicates from the data provider; they are kept, and dropping them moves \(\hat\rho_1\) by \(0.001\), \(z^*(2)\) by \(0.04\), \(\hat d\) by \(0.014\) and \(AQ\) by \(0.29\). Crypto-assets trade seven days a week, with 365 observations per year. Analyses that combine several series, such as event studies, align prices on common trading days before computing returns.

                  In brief

                  BNR: Banca Națională a României — the National Bank of Romania.
                  • 16 daily series up to 18 September 2026
                    • sample sizes: Appendix
                    • 13 equity indices from 2000: 4 developed (S&P 500, Euro Stoxx 50, Nikkei 225, Hang Seng), 9 emerging or frontier (BET, WIG20, BUX, PX, BIST 100, Bovespa, IPC, Nifty 50, Shanghai)
                    • Bitcoin from 2014 in the full-sample and rolling tests; the yearly crypto analysis starts in 2011
                    • Ethereum from 2016 and EUR/RON, the BNR reference rate, from July 2005
                  • Daily log returns \(r_t = \ln P_t - \ln P_{t-1}\), each series on its own calendar
                    • \(P_t\): the closing price (index level, exchange rate) on day \(t\)
                  • Equity indices use trading days only
                    • a repeated close is removed only if the day shows no trading (zero volume, or open = high = low = close)
                    • or if its whole row copies the previous day's; such a day is a holiday filled with the previous price
                    • a genuine unchanged close, with volume and an intraday range, stays as a zero return (S&P 500: 3 of about 6 700 days)
                    • the BET file has closes only: none of its days is removed
                    • IPC: 46 such days (44 in 2023–2026) with near-identical volume, likely provider duplicates, are kept
                    • dropping them moves \(\hat\rho_1\) by \(0.001\), \(z^*(2)\) by \(0.04\), \(\hat d\) by \(0.014\), \(AQ\) by \(0.29\)
                  • Crypto-assets trade 7 days a week: 365 observations per year

                  Further Topics: data and markets

                  Fair games, pricing and rational bubbles

                  What restriction does efficiency impose on prices?

                  Slide 57 in the PDF

                  The standard answer to the question of what efficiency imposes on prices is the fair game: the return in excess of the required return cannot be forecast from the information set. The autocorrelation tests of the core check only one consequence of this condition. Because the required return comes from a pricing model, every test of the fair game is also a test of that model, the joint-hypothesis problem (Fama, 1991).

                  The condition leaves room for rational bubbles but rules out deviations from fundamental value that revert predictably, often called fads. Deviations do not always disappear, because arbitrage is risky when the convergence of prices is uncertain (Shleifer & Vishny, 1997); the limits of arbitrage are treated in Further Topics.

                  In brief

                  • The question: what exact restriction does efficiency impose on prices, and what does it leave free?
                  • The standard answer is the fair game.
                    • The return in excess of the required return cannot be forecast.
                  • The autocorrelation tests of section 2 check only one consequence of this condition.
                  • The required return comes from a pricing model, so every fair-game test is also a test of that model Fama (1991).
                  • A fair game allows rational bubbles.
                    • but rules out deviations that revert predictably to fundamental value (fads).
                  • Deviations do not always disappear Shleifer & Vishny (1997).
                    • arbitrage is risky when price convergence is uncertain (Further Topics).
                  Further Topics: fair games, rational bubbles

                  Test construction and alternatives

                  Moments, tails and asymptotics

                  Slide 58 in the PDF

                  An efficiency test is valid only under assumptions that the data may violate. The robust standard errors of autocorrelations, treated in Further Topics, require a finite fourth moment, \(\mathbb{E}[r^4] < \infty\). Heavy tails can break this condition: a Student \(t\) distribution with four degrees of freedom has a finite variance but an infinite fourth moment, and then the robust standard error no longer converges.

                  The critical values in use, \(\pm 1.96\) or the quantiles of the \(\chi^2\) distribution, are asymptotic approximations. They are correct as the sample grows but can be inaccurate in a short sample such as a window of 500 days. The limit theorems also need weak dependence, or mixing: observations far enough apart in time become nearly independent. Mixing allows volatility clustering and dependence between neighbouring days.

                  In brief

                  • The question: which assumptions make an efficiency test valid, and which alternatives can it detect?
                  • Robust standard errors of autocorrelations (Further Topics) need a finite fourth moment, \(\E[r^4]<\infty\)
                    • heavy tails: Student \(t\) with four degrees of freedom has finite variance but \(\E[r^4]=\infty\), and then the robust standard error no longer converges
                  • Critical values (\(\pm 1.96\), \(\chi^2\) quantiles) are asymptotic approximations
                    • correct as the sample grows
                    • possibly inaccurate in a short sample such as a 500-day window
                  • Limit theorems also need weak dependence (mixing)
                    • observations far enough apart in time become nearly independent
                    • mixing allows volatility clustering and dependence between neighbouring days
                  Further Topics: test construction

                  Predictive regressions and bias

                  Predetermined variables, bias and consistency

                  Slide 59 in the PDF

                  A persistent predictor distorts inference in a finite sample. A predictor is predetermined when it is known before the return shock it is meant to predict; strict exogeneity also rules out correlation with the shocks at every other date. The dividend–price ratio of today can forecast the return of next month, but next month's price shock also changes the ratio, and this feedback rules out strict exogeneity.

                  Consistency is not the same as unbiasedness. With a merely predetermined predictor, the ordinary least squares (OLS) estimator converges to the true parameter as the sample grows, but it is biased in a finite sample.

                  In brief

                  • Why does a persistent predictor distort inference in a finite sample?
                  • A predetermined predictor is known before the return shock it predicts
                    • strict exogeneity additionally rules out correlation with shocks at every date.
                  • Today's dividend–price ratio can forecast next month's return.
                    • yet next month's price shock also changes the ratio.
                    • this feedback obstructs strict exogeneity.
                  • Consistent is not unbiased.
                    • with a merely predetermined predictor, OLS converges to the true parameter.
                    • but is biased in a finite sample.
                  Further Topics: predictive regressions

                  Long memory and changing efficiency

                  Short memory, long memory and scaling

                  Slide 60 in the PDF

                  The question is whether dependence persists across horizons and market regimes. A process has short memory when its autocovariances are absolutely summable; an autoregressive model of order one, AR(1), with coefficient 0.5 has correlations \(0.5^k\) at lag \(k\). Long memory here means positive dependence that decays so slowly that the sum of the autocovariances diverges, which power-law decay can produce.

                  Scaling describes how a measure of fluctuations over \(n\) days, \(F(n)\), grows with the horizon, for example the standard deviation of a sum of \(n\) returns:

                  \[F(n) \propto n^H,\]
                  (2.13)

                  where \(H\) is the Hurst exponent, estimated in Further Topics. Doubling the horizon multiplies fluctuations by \(2^H\): by about 1.41 at \(H = 0.5\), without long memory, and by 1.68 at \(H = 0.75\). Anti-persistence describes increments that offset each other, as in fractional Gaussian noise (fGn) with \(H < 0.5\); fGn is a stationary Gaussian model of increments whose partial sums have variance proportional to \(n^{2H}\).

                  In brief

                  • Does dependence persist across horizons and market regimes?
                  AR: AutoRegressive model.
                  • Short memory has absolutely summable autocovariances
                    • an AR(1) with coefficient 0.5 has correlations \(0.5^k\) at lag \(k\).
                  • Long memory here means positive dependence decaying so slowly that the autocovariance sum diverges
                    • power-law decay can produce this.
                  • Scaling: a measure of fluctuations over \(n\) days, \(F(n)\), grows like \(n^H\)
                    • e.g. the standard deviation of a sum of \(n\) returns
                    • \(H\) is the Hurst exponent, estimated in Further Topics
                    • doubling the horizon multiplies fluctuations by \(2^H\): about 1.41 at \(H = 0.5\) (no long memory) and 1.68 at \(H = 0.75\)
                  • Anti-persistence describes offsetting increments, as in fractional Gaussian noise (fGn) with \(H<0.5\)
                    • fGn is a stationary Gaussian increment model with partial-sum variance proportional to \(n^{2H}\).
                  Further Topics: R/S analysis

                  Variance Ratio and Hurst Exponent: Long or Short Memory?

                  Slide 61 in the PDF

                  For fGn the variance of a sum of \(q\) returns is \(\mathrm{Var}(r_t + \dots + r_{t-q+1}) = q^{2H}\,\mathrm{Var}(r_t)\), so the variance ratio (VR) is \(\mathrm{VR}(q) = q^{2H-1}\). Inverting the relation, an observed variance ratio implies a Hurst exponent

                  \[H(q) = 0.5 + \frac{\ln \mathrm{VR}(q)}{2 \ln q}.\]
                  (2.14)

                  At \(q = 20\) days, the implied exponent is \(0.553\) for the BET and \(0.453\) for the S&P 500.

                  The difficulty is that a process without long memory can also give \(H(q) \neq 0.5\). An AR(1) with \(\rho_1 = -0.1\) has autocorrelations \(\rho_k = (-0.1)^k\), so only short memory, yet its \(\mathrm{VR}(20) = 0.826\) gives \(H(20) = 0.468\): the process looks anti-persistent without any long memory. At \(q = 250\) the same process gives \(H(250) = 0.482\); for short memory, \(H(q)\) approaches \(0.5\) as \(q\) grows.

                  An AR(1) with the BET's first-order autocorrelation, \(\hat\rho_1 = 0.109\), gives \(H(20) = 0.535\). Most of the gap between the BET's \(H(20) = 0.553\) and \(0.5\) is therefore short memory. Separating long from short memory requires a statistic built for the purpose, the modified rescaled-range (R/S) statistic of Lo (1991), treated in Further Topics.

                  In brief

                  • For fGn, the variance of a sum of \(q\) returns is \(\Var(r_t + \dots + r_{t-q+1}) = q^{2H} \Var(r_t)\).
                    • fGn: the exactly self-similar process defined above.
                  • Hence \(\text{VR}(q) = q^{2H-1}\), and an observed \(\text{VR}(q)\) implies a Hurst exponent \(H(q) = 0.5 + \ln \text{VR}(q) / (2 \ln q)\).
                  • At \(q = 20\) days, the implied exponent is \(0.553\) for the BET and \(0.453\) for the S&P 500.
                  • The problem: a process without long memory can give \(H(q) \neq 0.5\)
                    • Example: an AR(1) with \(\rho_1 = -0.1\) has autocorrelations \(\rho_k = (-0.1)^k\), so only short memory.
                    • For this AR(1), \(\text{VR}(20) = 0.826\), so \(H(20) = 0.468\): the process looks “anti-persistent” without any long memory.
                    • At \(q = 250\), the same process gives \(H(250) = 0.482\): for short memory, \(H(q)\) approaches \(0.5\) as \(q\) grows.
                  • An AR(1) with BET's autocorrelation, \(\hat\rho_1 = 0.109\), gives \(H(20) = 0.535\).
                  • So most of the gap between BET's \(H(20) = 0.553\) and \(0.5\) is short memory.
                  • Separating long from short memory needs a statistic built for it: Lo's modified R/S (Further Topics).
                  Further Topics: DFA, changing efficiency

                  Event-study inference

                  Event-study inference

                  Slide 62 in the PDF

                  The \(t\) statistics of the event-study section rest on three assumptions:

                  1. The coefficients \(\hat a_i\) and \(\hat b_i\) are known exactly;
                  2. Firms are independent;
                  3. The variance of abnormal returns does not rise on the event day.

                  Each of them can fail.

                  Estimation error arises because \(\hat a_i\) and \(\hat b_i\) come from a finite window, so the predicted normal return is uncertain as well. Cross-sectional dependence arises because two banks hit by the same news have abnormal returns that move together. Event-induced variance arises because the event itself raises the variance, which the calm days of the estimation window understate. The corrections are given in the Appendix.

                  In brief

                  • The question: how reliable are the \(t\) statistics of the Event studies section?
                    • when their assumptions fail
                  • There we assumed:
                    • \(\hat a_i\) and \(\hat b_i\) are known exactly.
                    • firms are independent.
                    • the abnormal-return variance does not rise on the event day.
                  • Estimation error: \(\hat a_i\) and \(\hat b_i\) come from a finite window.
                    • the predicted normal return is also uncertain.
                  • Cross-sectional dependence: two banks hit by the same news have abnormal returns that move together.
                  • Event-induced variance: the event itself raises the variance.
                    • the calm days before understate it.
                  Further Topics: event-study inference

                  Anomalies and behavioural explanations

                  Anomalies and behavioural explanations

                  Slide 63 in the PDF

                  The question is whether behavioural biases can explain the anomalies that survive correction. The frame begins with momentum, which comes in two forms. Cross-sectional momentum ranks assets against each other: if asset A gained 10% and asset B 2%, a relative strategy may buy A and sell B short. Time-series momentum uses the sign of each asset's own past return, so in the same example both A and B would receive positive signals. The behavioural explanations are in the Appendix.

                  In brief

                  • Can behavioural biases explain the anomalies that survive?
                  Momentum
                  • Cross-sectional momentum ranks assets.
                    • if A gained 10% and B gained 2%, a relative strategy may buy A and short B.
                  • Time-series momentum uses each asset's own past sign.
                    • both A and B would receive positive signals in that example.
                  Further Topics: anomalies and behaviour

                  Machine learning and research design

                  Machine learning and research design

                  Slide 64 in the PDF

                  The question is what an in-sample rejection means when efficiency is tested with hundreds of predictors. Anomalies are found by searching among many predictors, and Harvey, Liu & Zhu (2016) count more than 300 published factors. The section on multiple testing showed that 2,000 random rules produce in-sample "anomalies". The Further Topics frames show how to evaluate a flexible model properly and why an in-sample rejection can arise even with rational investors (Martin & Nagel, 2022).

                  In brief

                  • The question: what does an in-sample rejection mean?
                    • when we test efficiency with hundreds of predictors
                  • Anomalies are found by searching among many predictors: Harvey, Liu & Zhu (2016) count more than 300 published factors.
                  • The Multiple testing and anomalies section showed that 2 000 random rules produce in-sample “anomalies”.
                  • Here:
                    • how to evaluate a flexible model properly.
                    • why an in-sample rejection can arise even with rational investors Martin & Nagel (2022).
                  Further Topics: machine learning

                  Case study: time-series momentum in Bitcoin

                  The case study tests a published result on weak-form efficiency in a market that did not exist when the classical tests were designed. Liu and Tsyvinski (2021) find that Bitcoin's weekly return predicts the returns of the following weeks. The course data reproduce the result in the authors' sample and test it on the eight years that followed.

                  The Question: Do Past Returns Predict Bitcoin's Return?

                  Slide 65 in the PDF

                  The question is whether this week's Bitcoin return predicts the returns of the following weeks. If it does, past prices contain information that the market has not used, a departure from weak-form efficiency. Liu and Tsyvinski (2021), in the Review of Financial Studies, find that cryptocurrency returns have no exposure to the usual stock-market or macroeconomic factors. They are predicted instead by factors specific to crypto markets: time-series momentum (TSMOM) and investor attention.

                  TSMOM means that a week with a high return is followed, on average, by high returns in the following weeks. In the authors' working paper of the National Bureau of Economic Research (NBER w24877), on Bitcoin from January 2011 to May 2018, the slope of the regression of next week's return on this week's return is 0.19 (\(t = 3.73\)). One more standard deviation of this week's return (16.6%) adds 3.2% to next week's return. The quintile of weeks with the highest returns is followed by 11.2% a week on average, the quintile with the lowest by 2.6%.

                  The result matters for efficiency because an effect that investors exploit should weaken after publication (McLean & Pontiff, 2016). The eight years after the authors' sample are a natural out-of-sample test.

                  In brief

                  • Question: does this week's Bitcoin return predict the returns of the following weeks?
                    • if so, past prices contain unused information: a departure from weak-form efficiency
                  • Liu & Tsyvinski (2021), Review of Financial Studies
                    • cryptocurrency returns have no exposure to the usual stock market factors or to macroeconomic factors
                    • they are predicted by factors specific to crypto markets: time-series momentum (TSMOM) and investor attention
                  • TSMOM: a week with a high return is followed, on average, by high returns in the following weeks
                  • The authors' numbers (working paper of the National Bureau of Economic Research, NBER w24877; Bitcoin, January 2011 – May 2018)
                    • slope of the regression of next week's return on this week's return: 0.19 (\(t\) = 3.73)
                    • one more standard deviation today (16.6%) adds 3.2% to next week's return
                    • the quintile of weeks with the highest returns is followed by 11.2% a week on average, the one with the lowest by 2.6%
                  • Why it matters: an effect that investors exploit should weaken after publication McLean & Pontiff (2016)

                  Bitcoin: The Momentum Slope in the Paper's Sample and After

                  Slide 66 in the PDF

                  The left panel shows the slope \(b_h\) of the predictive regression

                  \[R_{t+h} = a + b_h R_t + e_{t+h}, \qquad h = 1, \dots, 4 \text{ weeks},\]
                  (2.15)

                  with 95% intervals based on Newey–West (NW) standard errors, in the paper's sample and after it; the diamonds are the authors' numbers. The right panel shows the slope \(b_1\) over rolling windows of 104 weeks (two years), each dated at the last week of its window.

                  In the interactive version, the menu switches between Bitcoin and Ethereum, the first slider moves the end of the first period, and the second sets the length of the rolling window, from 52 to 208 weeks. The ▶ button moves the end of the first period through time.

                    Question for the room After May 2018, Bitcoin's slope is b₁ = 0.115, with NW t = 2.61. Which conclusion does this regression support?
                    Answer

                    The regression supports a one-week predictive association. A one-standard-deviation increase in the current return corresponds to an estimated 0.96-percentage-point increase in next week's return, compared with 2.98 percentage points in the earlier period. Neither a trading rule after costs nor out-of-sample forecast errors are evaluated. Using a sample after the paper's sample period does not replace that evaluation. Over rolling two-year windows, b₁ is significant in 13% of the windows.

                    In the paper's sample the course estimate of \(b_1\) lies close to the authors' diamond. After the sample, only the one-week slope stays away from zero, and the rolling panel shows how rarely a two-year window yields a significant slope. The numbers are on the result frame.

                    In brief

                    • Left: the slope \(b_h\) in \(R_{t+h} = a + b_h R_t + e_{t+h}\), with 95% Newey–West (NW) intervals; diamonds: the authors' numbers
                    • Right: the slope \(b_1\) over rolling windows of 104 weeks (two years), dated at the last week of the window
                    MFM_ch2_crypto_momentum

                    The Method in Brief and How the Replication Differs

                    Slide 67 in the PDF

                    \(R_t\) is the simple return of Bitcoin in week \(t\), from one Sunday close to the next, computed from daily closing prices in Coordinated Universal Time (UTC), as in the paper. The predictive regression (2.15) is estimated for \(h = 1\) to 4 weeks in the paper's sample, 9 January 2011 to 27 May 2018 (386 weeks), and after it, 3 June 2018 to 13 September 2026 (433 weeks). The authors' robustness check, the same regression from 2013, is also reproduced. In the sort, the weeks are split into five quintiles by \(R_t\), and the mean of \(R_{t+1}\) is compared between the top and the bottom quintile.

                    The replication differs from the paper in three ways. The prices are those of the course data, from EODHD (EOD Historical Data), instead of CoinDesk prices. The standard errors are Newey–West with 5 lags instead of the authors' OLS and bootstrap standard errors. The attention factors (Google searches, Twitter) and Ripple are left out.

                    In brief

                    • \(R_t\): the simple return of Bitcoin in week \(t\), from the Sunday close to the next Sunday close
                      • daily closing prices in UTC (Coordinated Universal Time), as in the paper
                    • Predictive regression \(R_{t+h} = a + b_h R_t + e_{t+h}\), for \(h = 1, \dots, 4\) weeks
                      • the paper's sample: 9 Jan 2011 – 27 May 2018, 386 weeks
                      • after the sample: 3 Jun 2018 – 13 Sep 2026, 433 weeks
                      • the authors' robustness check: the same regression from 2013
                    • Sorting: the weeks are split into five quintiles by \(R_t\); we compare the mean of \(R_{t+1}\) in the top and the bottom quintile
                    • Differences from the paper
                      • EODHD (EOD Historical Data) prices from the course data instead of CoinDesk prices
                      • NW standard errors with 5 lags instead of the authors' OLS (ordinary least squares) and bootstrap standard errors
                      • no attention factors (Google searches, Twitter) and no Ripple

                    The Result: Only the One-Week Effect Remains, Three Times Smaller

                    Slide 68 in the PDF

                    In the paper's sample, the course data reproduce the result: \(b_1 = 0.168\) (OLS \(t = 3.33\), NW \(t = 2.75\)), against 0.19 in the paper. The top quintile is followed by 9.9% a week and the bottom quintile by 2.6%, with a \(t\) statistic of 2.18 for the difference.

                    After the sample, predictability remains only at the one-week horizon. The slope is \(b_1 = 0.115\) (NW \(t = 2.61\)), and it survives the Holm correction over the four horizons (\(p = 0.009 < 0.05/4\)). At \(h = 2\), 3 and 4 the slopes lie between \(-0.026\) and \(0.072\), all with \(|t| \le 1.48\). One more standard deviation of this week's return adds 0.96% to next week's return, against 2.98% in the paper's sample, and weekly volatility fell from 17.7% to 8.3%. For Ethereum after the sample, \(b_1 = 0.107\) (NW \(t = 2.45\)).

                    What the result supports is statistical predictability of the one-week return, not a profitable strategy: the regression includes no trading costs, no risk adjustment and no out-of-sample forecasts. Three limits remain:

                    1. From 2013, within the paper's sample, \(b_1\) is no longer significant (NW \(t = 1.06\)), so the extreme weeks of 2011–2012 weigh heavily.
                    2. The slope is significant in only 13% of the rolling two-year windows.
                    3. The NW \(t\) test requires a finite fourth moment, which Chapter 1 questions.

                    In brief

                    • In the paper's sample, the course data reproduce the result
                      • \(b_1\) = 0.168 (OLS \(t\) = 3.33, NW \(t\) = 2.75), against 0.19 in the paper
                      • the top quintile is followed by 9.9% a week, the bottom one by 2.6% (\(t\) of the difference = 2.18)
                    • After the sample, predictability remains only at the one-week horizon
                      • \(b_1\) = 0.115 (NW \(t\) = 2.61); it survives the Holm correction over the four horizons (\(p\) = 0.009 \(<\) 0.05/4)
                      • at \(h\) = 2, 3, 4: slopes between \(-\)0.026 and 0.072, all with \(|t| \le\) 1.48
                      • one more standard deviation today adds 0.96% to next week's return, against 2.98% in the paper's sample
                      • weekly volatility fell from 17.7% to 8.3%; Ethereum after the sample: \(b_1\) = 0.107 (NW \(t\) = 2.45)
                    • What the result supports: statistical predictability of the one-week return, not a profitable strategy
                      • no trading costs, no risk adjustment and no out-of-sample forecasts
                    • Limits
                      • from 2013, within the paper's sample, \(b_1\) is no longer significant (NW \(t\) = 1.06): the extreme weeks of 2011–2012 weigh heavily
                      • \(b_1\) is significant in only 13% of the rolling two-year windows
                      • the NW \(t\)-test requires a finite fourth moment, which Chapter 1 questions

                    Section summary

                    The course data reproduce the TSMOM slope of Liu and Tsyvinski in their sample (\(b_1 = 0.168\) against 0.19). After May 2018 only the one-week slope remains significant, \(b_1 = 0.115\), and the effect of one standard deviation falls from 2.98% to 0.96% a week. The result is predictability, not profit, and it depends on the extreme weeks of 2011–2012.

                    AI for scientific discovery

                    The last part of the core applies the chapter's tools to an open research question with the help of an artificial-intelligence (AI) assistant. The question is whether the entry of arbitrageurs through spot exchange-traded funds (ETFs) made Bitcoin more efficient. The frames show how a hypothesis proposed by the assistant is turned into a test with checks fixed before the results are seen.

                    An Open Question: Does a Market Become More Efficient When Arbitrageurs Enter?

                    Slide 69 in the PDF

                    The open question is whether Bitcoin's informational efficiency rises when new arbitrageurs enter through regulated instruments such as spot ETFs. Two strands of evidence bear on it. Tran & Leirvik (2020) find that the efficiency of crypto-assets varies over time and rises. Kia, Liu, Li, Song & Xu (2026) find that, after the launch of the spot ETFs, the spot market again became the dominant venue of price discovery.

                    Neither result says whether the change comes from the ETFs or from the maturing of the market. This is the question tested in the section; the ETF data and the placebo design are in the Appendix.

                    In brief

                    • The question: does Bitcoin's informational efficiency rise when new arbitrageurs enter?
                      • the new arbitrageurs come in through regulated instruments
                      • such as spot ETFs
                    • Tran & Leirvik (2020) find that the efficiency of crypto-assets varies over time and rises.
                    • Kia, Liu, Li, Song & Xu (2026) find that, after the spot ETFs, the spot market again became the dominant venue of price discovery.
                    • We do not know whether the change comes from the ETFs or from market maturity.
                      • this is the question tested in this section.
                    Further Topics: ETFs, placebo

                    Mini-Case: Did Spot ETFs Make Bitcoin More Efficient?

                    Slide 70 in the PDF

                    An AI assistant proposes a typical hypothesis: US spot Bitcoin ETFs, trading since 11 January 2024, brought arbitrageurs, so Bitcoin became more efficient. The discovery loop turns this claim into a test, with reusable prompts given in the Appendix. The literature step uses Kia, Liu, Li, Song & Xu (2026) and Tran & Leirvik (2020). The hypothesis is \(H_0: \rho_{1,\text{post}} = \rho_{1,\text{pre}}\), the equality of the first-order autocorrelation in two windows of equal length around the launch date, the first trading day. The data and code step uses the daily Bitcoin closing price in US dollars and first replicates the chapter's full-sample estimate, \(\hat\rho_1 = -0.022\).

                    The checks are fixed before any result is seen; the rationale for each is in the Appendix:

                    • The standard errors of the estimates are HAC, not \(1/\sqrt{T}\);
                    • The two windows have equal length;
                    • The test uses the launch date only, plus placebo dates;
                    • A control asset absorbs confounders such as the halving of April 2024, interest rates and fund inflows;
                    • Every statistic tried enters a correction for multiple testing (Holm or Chow–Denning), and the minimum detectable effect (MDE) is reported.

                    The results of the mini-case are in the Appendix.

                    In brief

                    • An artificial-intelligence (AI) assistant proposes a typical hypothesis:
                      • “US spot Bitcoin ETFs (trading since 11 January 2024) brought arbitrageurs, so Bitcoin became more efficient”
                    • The discovery loop
                      • reusable prompts: Appendix
                      • literature: Kia, Liu, Li, Song & Xu (2026) and Tran & Leirvik (2020) (the topic's opening slide)
                      • hypothesis: \(H_0: \rho_{1,\text{post}} = \rho_{1,\text{pre}}\), two windows of equal length around the launch date (first trading day)
                      • data and code: daily Bitcoin closing price (USD); first replicate the chapter's full-sample \(\hat\rho_1 = -0.022\)
                    • Checks fixed before seeing results
                      • rationale for each: Appendix
                      • HAC standard errors of the estimates (not \(1/\sqrt T\)); equal windows; the launch date only, plus placebo dates
                      • a control asset for confounders (the April 2024 halving, rates, inflows)
                      • a multiple-testing correction (Holm or Chow–Denning) for every statistic tried, and the minimum detectable effect (MDE)

                    Synthesis and technical reference

                    Synthesis, Check Yourself, and Next: Chapter 3

                    Slide 72 in the PDF

                    Every efficiency claim in the chapter can be checked in five steps:

                    1. The information set \(\mathcal{I}_t\) that prices are assumed to reflect, past prices or public news.
                    2. The null hypothesis, RW1, RW3 or the martingale difference, stated together with the model of risk.
                    3. The statistic, with a variance that remains valid under volatility clustering.
                    4. The uncertainty created by the horizons, markets and windows tried, and the out-of-sample evidence.
                    5. The economic step: a statistical rejection is not an exploitable profit after costs, and a profit does not prove that the market is irrational.

                    The questions for self-checking ask why RW3 can be rejected for the S&P 500 while the market remains efficient in the economic sense, why the BET's \(H(20) > 0.5\) does not prove long memory, and which of two results, the BET's persistence or the non-rejection for Bitcoin, would survive a change of test, window and inference. Chapter 3 continues with risk-adjusted returns in factor models and the factor zoo. The reference textbooks are Campbell, Lo & MacKinlay (1997), chapter 2, and Franke, Härdle & Hafner (2019); advanced courses are listed in the Appendix.

                    In brief

                    Five steps for every efficiency claim
                    • Information set: which information \(\mathcal{I}_t\) do we assume prices reflect (past prices, public news)?
                    • Null: RW1, RW3 or the martingale difference, stated together with the risk model
                    • Statistic: its variance valid under volatility clustering
                    • Uncertainty: horizons, markets and windows tried, and out-of-sample evidence
                    • Economics: a statistical rejection is not an exploitable profit after costs
                      • a profit does not prove an irrational market
                    Check yourself and next steps
                    • Why can RW3 be rejected for the S&P 500 while the market remains efficient in the economic sense?
                    • Why does BET's \(H(20) > 0.5\) not prove long memory?
                    • Which of the two results would survive a change of test, window and inference?
                      • BET's persistence or Bitcoin's non-rejection

                    The Practical Decision

                    Slide 73 in the PDF

                    The decision has three parts: what I choose, under which conditions the choice holds, and what result would make me drop it.

                    For weak-form tests I choose robust standard errors, not i.i.d. ones: for the S&P 500, the robust standard error of \(\hat\rho_1\) is \(0.027\), against \(0.012\) under i.i.d. assumptions. Across the four variance-ratio horizons I use the Chow–Denning test, and across the 15 calendar tests the Holm correction. A predictor is judged by \(R^2_{OS}\) and the Clark–West test against the historical mean, not by the in-sample \(R^2\).

                    The choice holds under conditions. The robust standard errors require a finite fourth moment, which Chapter 1 questions. A rejection also tests the model of expected returns and is not a profit after costs.

                    Three results would make me drop a conclusion. For a predictor, \(R^2_{OS} < 0\) with an insignificant Clark–West test, as for \(dp\) on the S&P 500 (\(-0.51\%\), \(t = 0.42\)). For an anomaly, a rejection that does not survive Holm, as the January effect of the BUX (\(t = 2.25\)). For the BET's persistence, a variance ratio that stays inside the robust band on new windows.

                    In brief

                    • What I choose
                      • weak-form tests with robust standard errors, not i.i.d. ones (S&P 500: robust SE of \(\hat\rho_1\) \(0.027\), against \(0.012\))
                      • Chow–Denning across the four variance-ratio horizons, Holm across the 15 calendar tests
                      • for a predictor: \(R^2_{OS}\) and the Clark–West test against the historical mean, not the in-sample \(R^2\)
                    • Under which conditions
                      • the robust standard errors require a finite fourth moment, which Chapter 1 questions
                      • a rejection also tests the model of expected returns and is not a profit after costs
                    • What result would make me drop it
                      • a predictor: \(R^2_{OS} < 0\) with an insignificant Clark–West test, as \(dp\) on the S&P 500 (\(-0.51\%\), \(t = 0.42\))
                      • an anomaly: a rejection that does not survive Holm, as the BUX January effect (\(t = 2.25\))
                      • the BET persistence: a variance ratio that stays inside the robust band on new windows

                    Section summary

                    Efficiency is a statement about an information set and a model of expected returns, and every test of it has to be read with a variance robust to volatility clustering, a correction for the family of tests tried and an out-of-sample check. On the chapter's data, the S&P 500 shows short-horizon reversal, the BET persistence that is mostly short memory, the dividend–price ratio fails out of sample, and only the turn-of-the-month effects of the BET and the BUX survive the Holm correction.

                    Why the robust band is wider: a numerical check

                    Slide 74 in the PDF

                    For a horizon of \(q = 2\) days the classical statistic is \(z = \sqrt{T}\,(\widehat{\text{VR}}(2) - 1)\), valid when returns are i.i.d. (RW1), while the robust statistic \(z^*\) divides the same numerator by \(\sqrt{\hat\theta(2)}\). Without volatility clustering \(\hat\theta(2) \approx 1\) and the two coincide; with clustering \(\hat\theta(2) > 1\) and \(|z^*| < |z|\). In a toy example with \(T = 400\) days and \(\widehat{\text{VR}}(2) = 0.90\), \(z = 20 \times (-0.10) = -2.0\) rejects at 5%, but \(\hat\theta(2) = 4\) gives \(z^* = -1.0\), which does not reject: the robust band around 1 is twice as wide. For the S&P 500, \(z = -8.15\) and \(z^* = -3.66\), about 2.2 times smaller, and the null is still rejected.

                    In brief

                    • For \(q = 2\), the classical statistic is \(z = \sqrt{T}\,(\widehat{\text{VR}}(2) - 1)\).
                      • it assumes i.i.d. returns (RW1).
                    • The robust statistic \(z^*\) divides the same numerator by \(\sqrt{\hat\theta(2)}\).
                    • Without volatility clustering, \(\hat\theta(2) \approx 1\) and \(z^* \approx z\).
                      • with volatility clustering, \(\hat\theta(2) > 1\) and \(|z^*| < |z|\).
                    • Toy example: \(T = 400\) days and \(\widehat{\text{VR}}(2) = 0.90\) give \(z = 20 \times (-0.10) = -2.0\): the null is rejected at 5%.
                    • If volatility clustering makes \(\hat\theta(2) = 4\), then \(z^* = -2.0/\sqrt{4} = -1.0\) and the null is no longer rejected.
                    • The robust band around 1 is then \(\sqrt{4} = 2\) times wider.
                    • For the S&P 500, \(z = -8.15\) and \(z^* = -3.66\).
                      • the robust statistic is about 2.2 times smaller.
                      • the null is still rejected.
                    ↩ Back

                    Why the dividend–price ratio?

                    Slide 75 in the PDF

                    The Campbell–Shiller identity, \(dp_t \approx c + \sum_{j \ge 0} \rho^j (r_{t+1+j} - \Delta d_{t+1+j})\) with \(\rho\) just below 1 and \(\Delta d\) the log dividend growth, follows from the definition of the return through a log-linear approximation, without any economic model. If the log dividend–price ratio \(dp\) varies, it must therefore forecast future returns, future dividend growth or both. In the data \(dp\) forecasts dividend growth poorly, so its variation should forecast returns, which is why it is the most studied market predictor. A successful forecast would still not prove inefficiency, because it can reflect a risk premium that varies over time.

                    In brief

                    • The Campbell & Shiller (1988) identity: \(dp_t \approx c + \sum_{j \ge 0} \rho^j \big(r_{t+1+j} - \Delta d_{t+1+j}\big)\).
                      • \(\rho\) just below 1.
                      • \(\Delta d\): log dividend growth.
                    • The identity follows from the definition of the return through a log-linear approximation.
                      • with no economic model.
                    • Consequence: if \(dp\) varies, it must forecast future returns, future dividend growth or both.
                    • In the data \(dp\) forecasts dividend growth poorly, so its variation should forecast returns Cochrane (2008).
                    • That is why \(dp\) is the most studied market predictor Fama & French (1988).
                    • A successful forecast would not prove inefficiency.
                      • it can reflect a risk premium that varies over time.
                    ↩ Back

                    Holm on the 15 calendar p-values

                    Slide 76 in the PDF

                    The table collects the two-sided \(p\)-values of the calendar tests on five indices: the HAC \(t\) statistic against the Normal distribution for January and the turn of the month (TOM), and the Wald \(\chi^2_4\) test that the five weekday means are equal. The Holm procedure orders the 15 \(p\)-values and compares the \(k\)-th smallest with \(0.05/(16-k)\). The two smallest, TOM for the BET and TOM for the BUX, both 0.0003, lie below 0.0033 and 0.0036 and are rejected. The third, January for the BUX at 0.0245, exceeds 0.0038, so the procedure stops; without the correction it would have been a third rejection.

                    In brief

                    • Two-sided \(p\)-values:
                      • the HAC \(t\) statistic against the Normal distribution (January, TOM).
                      • the Wald \(\chi^2_4\) test that the five daily means are equal (weekdays).
                    MarketWeekday, equal meansJanuaryTOM
                    S&P 500 (US)\(0.8263\)\(0.4257\)\(0.3585\)
                    BET (Romania)\(0.3052\)\(0.1368\)\(0.0003\)
                    Euro Stoxx 50\(0.6319\)\(0.7738\)\(0.2266\)
                    WIG20 (Poland)\(0.2265\)\(0.6865\)\(0.0853\)
                    BUX (Hungary)\(0.9327\)\(0.0245\)\(0.0003\)
                    • The two smallest \(p\)-values are TOM for the BET and TOM for the BUX, both \(0.0003\).
                    • They are below the Holm thresholds \(0.05/15 = 0.0033\) and \(0.05/14 = 0.0036\), so both nulls are rejected.
                    • The third \(p\)-value, January for the BUX (\(0.0245\)), exceeds the threshold \(0.05/13 = 0.0038\), so Holm stops here.
                    • Without correction, January for the BUX would have been the third rejection, since \(0.0245 < 0.05\).
