                    Fair games and martingales

                    Slide 78 in the PDF

                    A fair game for excess returns requires \(\mathbb{E}[r_{t+1} - \mu_t \mid \mathcal{I}_t] = 0\), where \(\mathcal{I}_t\) is the information available at the end of day \(t\) and \(\mu_t\) the required (equilibrium) return: the return beyond the compensation for risk cannot be forecast from \(\mathcal{I}_t\). A martingale is a process whose expected next value, given the past, equals its current value. With a constant required simple return \(m\), the value \(V_t\) of a position with dividends reinvested, discounted at \(m\), \(V_t/(1+m)^t\), is a martingale (Campbell, Lo & MacKinlay, 1997). Discounting at the risk-free rate gives a martingale only under the risk-neutral measure, and the price itself is a martingale only without dividends and with \(m = 0\).

                    In brief

                    • Fair game for excess returns: \(\E[r_{t+1} - \mu_t \mid \mathcal{I}_t] = 0\)
                      • \(\mathcal{I}_t\): the information available at the end of day \(t\); \(\E[\cdot \mid \mathcal{I}_t]\): the expectation given that information
                      • \(\mu_t\): the required (equilibrium) return, the compensation for risk
                      • in words: the return beyond \(\mu_t\) cannot be forecast from \(\mathcal{I}_t\)
                    • Martingale: a process whose expected next value, given the past, equals its current value
                      • with a constant required simple return \(m\), the value \(V_t\) of a position with dividends reinvested, discounted at \(m\), \(V_t/(1 + m)^t\), is a martingale Campbell, Lo & MacKinlay (1997)
                      • discounting at the risk-free rate gives a martingale only under the risk-neutral measure (slide on the risk-neutral measure)
                      • the price \(P_t\) itself is a martingale only if no dividends are paid and \(m = 0\); the expected log return is then negative (the Jensen term, next slide)
                    ↩ Back

                    The Jensen term: why averages differ

                    Slide 79 in the PDF

                    Because the logarithm is concave, the average of log outcomes is smaller than the log of their average unless the outcomes are constant. Equally likely simple returns of \(+10\%\) and \(-10\%\) have mean zero, but the mean log return is \(\tfrac12\ln(0.99) \approx -0.00503\). Along a path with one rise and one fall, wealth of 100 becomes \(100 \times 1.1 \times 0.9 = 99\): a zero arithmetic mean does not preserve compounded wealth.

                    In brief

                    • The logarithm is concave.
                      • averaging log outcomes gives less than taking the log of their average, unless outcomes are constant.
                    • Equally likely simple returns of \(+10\%\) and \(-10\%\) have mean zero.
                    • The mean log return is \(\tfrac12\ln(1.1)+\tfrac12\ln(0.9)=\tfrac12\ln(0.99)\approx-0.00503\).
                    • Along a path with one rise and one fall, wealth 100 becomes \(100(1.1)(0.9)=99\).
                      • a zero arithmetic mean does not preserve compounded wealth.
                    ↩ Back

                    Prices, log prices and information

                    Slide 80 in the PDF

                    A martingale in prices, \(\mathbb{E}[P_{t+1} \mid \mathcal{I}_t] = P_t\), and a constant mean log return, \(\mathbb{E}[\ln(P_{t+1}/P_t) \mid \mathcal{I}_t] = \mu\), are different restrictions; they differ by the Jensen term, \(\mathbb{E}[\ln(1+R)] \approx \mathbb{E}[R] - \operatorname{Var}(R)/2\). The fair game implies that the unexpected return \(\varepsilon_{t+1} = r_{t+1} - \mu_t\) is uncorrelated with any function \(g(\mathcal{I}_t)\) of past information. With a constant \(\mu_t = \mu\), all autocorrelations \(\rho_k\), \(k \ge 1\), are zero, the hypothesis tested in the section on autocorrelation and variance ratios. The converse fails: zero autocorrelations do not rule out a mean that is nonlinearly predictable, which leads to the martingale difference hypothesis.

                    In brief

                    • A martingale in prices and a constant mean log return are different restrictions
                      • a martingale in prices: \(\E[P_{t+1} \mid \mathcal{I}_t] = P_t\)
                      • a constant mean log return: \(\E[\ln(P_{t+1}/P_t) \mid \mathcal{I}_t] = \mu\)
                      • the difference is the Jensen term: \(\E[\ln(1 + R)] \approx \E[R] - \Var(R)/2\), with \(R = P_{t+1}/P_t - 1\) the simple return
                    • Consequence of the fair game: \(\text{corr}(\varepsilon_{t+1}, g(\mathcal{I}_t)) = 0\) for any function \(g\) of past information
                      • where \(\varepsilon_{t+1} = r_{t+1} - \mu_t\) is the unexpected part of the return
                    • If \(\mu_t = \mu\) is constant, all autocorrelations \(\rho_k = \text{corr}(r_t, r_{t-k})\), \(k \geq 1\), are zero
                      • this is the hypothesis tested in section 2
                    • The converse fails: zero autocorrelations do not rule out a nonlinearly predictable mean
                      • the MDH, next topic
                    ↩ Back

                    Discounting and the risk-neutral measure

                    Slide 81 in the PDF

                    The martingale above uses the actual probabilities and the required return \(m\); pricing theory uses, equivalently, the risk-free rate and risk-adjusted probabilities. A risk-neutral measure is a set of pricing probabilities, in an arbitrage-free model, under which discounted traded prices, dividends included, are martingales. In the example a stock without dividends costs 100 today and will be worth 120 or 80 in one year, with a risk-free rate of 5%; the pricing probability \(q = 0.625\) gives \(0.625 \times 120 + 0.375 \times 80 = 105\), and \(105/1.05 = 100\). This \(q\) need not equal the actual probability of a rise: pricing probabilities are not forecasts of frequencies.

                    In brief

                    • Why: the martingale above uses the actual probabilities and the required return \(m\).
                      • pricing theory uses, equivalently, the risk-free rate and risk-adjusted probabilities.
                    • A risk-neutral measure uses pricing probabilities, in an arbitrage-free model.
                      • under which discounted traded prices, including dividends, are martingales.
                    • Example: a stock without dividends costs 100 today.
                      • in one year it is worth 120 or 80.
                      • the risk-free rate is 5%.
                      • The pricing probability \(q=0.625\) gives \(q\,120+(1-q)80=105\), and \(105/1.05 = 100\), today's price.
                    • This \(q\) need not equal the actual probability of an increase.
                      • pricing probabilities are not forecasts of frequencies.
                    ↩ Back

                    Probabilities and conditional means

                    Slide 82 in the PDF

                    The chart sets the two constructions of the previous frames side by side. On the left, under the actual probabilities, the conditional mean of the next value stays at 100; on the right, the pricing probabilities give an expected value of 105, whose value discounted at the risk-free rate is again today's price of 100.

                    In brief

                    Left: the actual conditional mean stays at 100. Right: pricing probabilities give 105, whose discounted value is 100.
                    ↩ Back

                    Fundamental value, rational bubbles and fads

                    Slide 83 in the PDF

                    With a constant required return \(m\), the fair game reads \(\mathbb{E}[P_{t+1} + D_{t+1} \mid \mathcal{I}_t] = (1+m) P_t\), where \(D_{t+1}\) is the dividend paid in \(t+1\). The fundamental value \(P^*_t = \mathbb{E}[\sum_{j \ge 1} D_{t+j}/(1+m)^j \mid \mathcal{I}_t]\) satisfies the same equation, so the pricing error \(B_t = P_t - P^*_t\) satisfies \(\mathbb{E}[B_{t+1} \mid \mathcal{I}_t] = (1+m) B_t\). The fair game ties the price to fundamental value only up to a rational bubble, a gap sustained by the expectation of resale and admissible only if it is expected to grow at the rate \(m\) (Chapter 17). A mean-reverting gap, a fad, makes \(\varepsilon_{t+1}\) predictable and violates the fair game, although short-horizon tests have little power against it (Summers, 1986). The restriction concerns the conditional mean only; volatility may be predictable (Chapter 1).

                    In brief

                    • With a constant required simple return \(m\), the fair game is \(\E[P_{t+1} + D_{t+1} \mid \mathcal{I}_t] = (1 + m)\,P_t\)
                      • \(D_{t+1}\): the cash flow (dividend) paid in \(t + 1\)
                      • Fundamental value \(P^*_t = \E\big[\sum_{j \geq 1} D_{t+j}/(1 + m)^j \mid \mathcal{I}_t\big]\); pricing error \(B_t = P_t - P^*_t\)
                      • The fundamental value \(P^*_t\) satisfies the same fair-game equation as the price
                      • Subtracting the two equations, the gap \(B_t\) satisfies \(\E[B_{t+1} \mid \mathcal{I}_t] = (1 + m)\,B_t\)
                    • A fair game ties the price to the fundamental value only up to a rational bubble
                      • A gap \(B_t > 0\) sustained by the expectation of resale
                      • A gap \(B_t \neq 0\) is admissible only if it is expected to grow at the required rate \(m\) (Chapter 17)
                      • A mean-reverting gap (fad) makes \(\varepsilon_{t+1}\) predictable and violates the fair game, although short-horizon tests have little power against it Summers (1986)
                    • The restriction is on the conditional mean only
                      • The variance is not restricted
                      • volatility may be predictable (Chapter 1)
                    ↩ Back

                    Rational bubble: a numerical example

                    Slide 84 in the PDF

                    A share pays 10 RON at the end of every year, forever, starting one year from today, and the required return is \(m = 10\%\), so the fundamental value is \(10/0.10 = 100\) RON. If the price today is 120 RON, the bubble is \(B_t = 20\) RON. Without a crash it grows to \(1.1 \times 20 = 22\) RON; after next year's dividend the fundamental value is still 100 RON and the price is 122 RON. The total simple return, \((122 + 10 - 120)/120\), is exactly the required 10%.

                    In brief

                    • A share pays 10 RON at the end of every year, forever.
                      • The first dividend is paid one year from today.
                      • The constant required return is \(m=10\%\); fundamental value is \(P^*_t=10/0.10=100\) RON.
                    • Today \(P_t=120\) RON, so \(B_t=20\) RON.
                      • Without a crash, \(B_{t+1}=1.1\times20=22\) RON.
                      • After next year's dividend, fundamental value remains 100 RON; the price is therefore 122 RON.
                    • The total simple return is \((122+10-120)/120=10\%\).
                    ↩ Back

                    Rational bubble: what happens if it can burst?

                    Slide 85 in the PDF

                    Let the 20 RON bubble survive each year with probability \(\pi = 0.8\) and collapse to zero with probability 0.2, with both probabilities known to investors. The fair game for the bubble still requires an expected value of 22 RON, so conditional on survival the bubble must reach \(22/0.8 = 27.5\) RON, a conditional growth of 37.5%. The return is about 14.6% if the bubble survives (ex-dividend price 127.5 RON) and about \(-8.3\%\) if it bursts (price 100 RON); the expected return, \(0.8 \times 14.6\% + 0.2 \times (-8.3\%)\), is again 10%. The crash mechanism is that of Blanchard and Watson (1982): the expected return satisfies the fair game while either realised return can differ from it.

                    In brief

                    • Initial bubble \(B_t=20\) RON and required return \(m=10\%\).
                      • Survival probability \(\pi=0.8\); crash probability \(1-\pi=0.2\).
                      • A crash makes the bubble zero; investors know the probabilities.
                    • The fair game for the bubble, \(\E[B_{t+1}\mid\mathcal I_t]=(1+m)B_t\), requires an expected bubble of \((1+m)B_t=22\) RON.
                      • Conditional on survival: \(B_{t+1}=22/0.8=27.5\) RON.
                      • Conditional growth is \(27.5/20-1=37.5\%\).
                    • Crash mechanism: Blanchard & Watson (1982), Bubbles, Rational Expectations and Financial Markets.
                    • The purchase price is 120 RON.
                      • the dividend at year-end is 10 RON.
                    • Survival: the ex-dividend price is 127.5 RON.
                      • Simple return: \((127.5+10-120)/120\approx14.6\%\).
                    • Crash: the price falls to fundamental value, 100 RON.
                      • Simple return: \((100+10-120)/120\approx-8.3\%\).
                    • Expected return: \(0.8\times(127.5+10-120)/120+0.2\times(100+10-120)/120=10\%\).
                      • The expected return satisfies the fair-game condition; either realised return can differ from it.
                    ↩ Back

                    Rational bubble and the no-bubble condition

                    Slide 86 in the PDF

                    The fair game restricts only the expected return over the next period; it does not pin down the price level. The no-bubble (transversality) condition, \(\lim_{k\to\infty} \mathbb{E}[P_{t+k} \mid \mathcal{I}_t]/(1+m)^k = 0\), requires the discounted expected resale price to vanish as the resale horizon \(k\) grows, and under it the price equals the fundamental value, supported entirely by dividends. A rational bubble violates the condition: since \(\mathbb{E}[B_{t+k} \mid \mathcal{I}_t] = (1+m)^k B_t\), its discounted value stays \(B_t\) at every horizon, so the 20 RON bubble of the example is still worth 20 RON in today's money however distant the resale. Tirole (1985) shows that in overlapping-generations models a bubble can exist only if the interest rate is below the growth rate of the economy.

                    In brief

                    • The fair game restricts only the expected return over the next period.
                      • it does not pin down the price level.
                    • No-bubble condition: \(\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{\E[P_{t+k}\mid\mathcal I_t]}{(1+m)^k}=0\)
                      • also called the transversality condition
                      • \(k\): the resale horizon; the discounted expected resale price vanishes
                    • Under this condition the price equals the fundamental value \(P^*_t\).
                      • it is supported entirely by dividends.
                    • A rational bubble violates the condition.
                      • from \(\E[B_{t+k}\mid\mathcal I_t]=(1+m)^kB_t\), the discounted value of the bubble stays \(B_t\neq 0\) at every horizon.
                    • In the numerical example, the 20 RON bubble is still worth 20 RON in today's money, however distant the resale.
                    • Tirole (1985): in overlapping-generations models, a bubble can exist only if:
                      • the interest rate is below the growth rate of the economy.
                    ↩ Back

                    The Grossman–Stiglitz Paradox

                    Slide 87 in the PDF

                    Gathering information costs analysts, data and computing. If prices reflected all information, nobody would be paid for collecting it, nobody would collect it, and prices could not reflect it (Grossman & Stiglitz, 1980). The resolution is an equilibrium degree of inefficiency, just large enough to reward informed traders for their costs. Large, liquid markets with cheap information and low costs should therefore be closer to efficiency, a prediction for the chapter's comparison of developed, emerging and crypto markets. Efficiency does not rule out bubbles either; it says that they are hard to time (Chapter 17).

                    In brief

                    • Gathering information is costly (analysts, data, computing)
                    • If prices reflected all information, nobody would be paid for collecting it Grossman & Stiglitz (1980)
                      • Then nobody would collect it, and prices could not reflect it
                    • Resolution: an equilibrium degree of inefficiency
                      • just large enough to reward informed traders for their costs
                    • Markets with cheap information and low costs (large, liquid) should be closer to efficiency
                      • a prediction for our comparison of developed, emerging and crypto markets
                    • Efficiency does not rule out bubbles either
                      • it says they are hard to time (Chapter 17)
                    ↩ Back

                    Pure arbitrage and relative-value trading

                    Slide 88 in the PDF

                    A pure arbitrage requires no positive initial outlay, cannot lose and gains in at least one state. Relative-value trading is different: it bets on the convergence of two prices, and convergence is uncertain. A price gap of 20 points may widen to 30 before it closes, and the position then needs extra collateral (margin) and may be liquidated. When convergence is uncertain, arbitrageurs with limited capital cannot eliminate deviations from fundamental value (Shleifer & Vishny, 1997), which is the link between arbitrage and efficiency.

                    In brief

                    • Pure arbitrage is a strategy with:
                      • no positive initial outlay.
                      • no possible loss.
                      • a positive gain in at least one state.
                    • Relative-value trading is risky when price convergence is uncertain.
                    • Example: a price gap of 20 points may widen to 30 before it closes.
                      • the position then needs extra collateral (margin) and may be liquidated.
                    • The link with efficiency: arbitrageurs with limited capital cannot eliminate deviations from fundamental value Shleifer & Vishny (1997).
                      • when convergence is uncertain.
                    ↩ Back

                    Autocorrelation and Its Robust Standard Error

                    Slide 89 in the PDF

                    The sample autocorrelation at lag \(k\) is \(\hat\rho_k = \sum_{t=k+1}^T e_t e_{t-k} / \sum_{t=1}^T e_t^2\), with \(e_t = r_t - \bar r\) the deviations of the \(T\) daily returns from their mean; \(\hat\rho_k > 0\) indicates persistence and \(\hat\rho_k < 0\) reversal. Under i.i.d. returns (RW1), \(\text{SE}(\hat\rho_k) \approx 1/\sqrt{T}\) and the 95% band is \(\pm 1.96/\sqrt{T}\). If the mean is unpredictable but volatility clusters (with finite fourth moments), the standard error under the null is \(\sqrt{\hat\tau_k}\):

                    \[\hat\tau_k = \frac{\sum_t e_t^2 e_{t-k}^2}{\big(\sum_t e_t^2\big)^2}.\]
                    (2.16)

                    When large moves cluster, \(\hat\tau_k\) in (2.16) exceeds \(1/T\), so the i.i.d. band is too narrow and classical tests reject too often. The robust statistics \(\tilde Q\) (Ljung–Box) and \(z^*\) (variance ratio) use \(\hat\tau_k\) instead of \(1/T\).

                    In brief

                    • Sample autocorrelation: \(\hat\rho_k = \sum_{t=k+1}^T e_t e_{t-k} / \sum_{t=1}^T e_t^2\), with \(e_t = r_t - \bar r\)
                      • \(T\): number of daily returns; \(\bar r\): their mean; \(k\): the lag; \(\hat\rho_k > 0\): persistence, \(\hat\rho_k < 0\): reversal
                    • Under RW1 (i.i.d.): \(\text{SE}(\hat\rho_k) \approx 1/\sqrt{T}\); 95% band \(\pm 1.96/\sqrt{T}\)
                    • Unpredictable mean with volatility clustering (finite fourth moments): \(\text{SE}(\hat\rho_k) \approx \sqrt{\hat\tau_k}\)
                      • \(\hat\tau_k = \sum_t e_t^2 e_{t-k}^2 \,/\, \big(\sum_t e_t^2\big)^2\) (robust variance under \(H_0\); for test bands centred at 0)
                      • When large moves cluster, \(\hat\tau_k > 1/T\): the i.i.d. band is too narrow and classical tests reject too often; the robust statistics \(\tilde Q\) (Ljung–Box) and \(z^*\) (variance ratio) use \(\hat\tau_k\) instead of \(1/T\)
                    ↩ Back

                    Intervals for \(\rho_k\): the HAC Standard Error

                    Slide 90 in the PDF

                    The standard error \(\sqrt{\hat\tau_k}\) is derived under the null, so it suits a test band centred at 0 but not an interval around an estimate \(\hat\rho_k \neq 0\). Around the estimate the chapter uses the heteroskedasticity- and autocorrelation-consistent (HAC) variance of the estimator (Newey & West, 1987): each day contributes \(\hat\psi_t = (e_t e_{t-k} - \hat\rho_k e_t^2)/\hat\gamma_0\) to \(\hat\rho_k - \rho_k\), and the variance of \(\hat\rho_k\) is the Bartlett-weighted long-run variance of \(\hat\psi_t\) divided by \(T\), with bandwidth \(\ell = \lfloor 4(T/100)^{2/9} \rfloor\): 9–10 lags for the full samples, 4–5 for one calendar year and 6 for the ETF windows. These intervals serve to compare periods, such as crypto years or the windows before and after the ETFs. A moving-block bootstrap gives similar values (S&P 500: HAC 0.030, block bootstrap 0.031, against \(\text{SE}_0 = 0.027\)), and the HAC standard error is smaller than \(\text{SE}_0\) for 14 of the 16 series (BET: 0.023 against 0.028).

                    In brief

                    • \(\sqrt{\hat\tau_k}\) is derived under \(H_0\): valid for a test band centred at 0, not for an interval around \(\hat\rho_k \neq 0\).
                    • Around the estimate we use the HAC variance of the estimator Newey & West (1987):
                      • \(\hat\psi_t = (e_t e_{t-k} - \hat\rho_k e_t^2)/\hat\gamma_0\), with \(\hat\gamma_0 = \tfrac{1}{T}\sum_t e_t^2\): the contribution of day \(t\) to \(\hat\rho_k - \rho_k\).
                      • \(\widehat{\Var}(\hat\rho_k) = \tfrac{1}{T}\big[\hat c_0 + 2\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}\big(1 - \tfrac{j}{\ell+1}\big)\hat c_j\big]\), with \(\hat c_j\) the lag-\(j\) autocovariance of \(\hat\psi_t\).
                      • bandwidth \(\ell = \lfloor 4 (T/100)^{2/9} \rfloor\): 9–10 lags for the full samples, 4–5 for one calendar year, 6 for the ETF windows.
                    • Use: intervals around \(\hat\rho_k\) and comparisons between periods (crypto years, before/after the ETFs).
                    • Check: a moving-block bootstrap (blocks of \(T^{1/3}\) days) gives similar values.
                      • S&P 500: HAC \(0.030\), block bootstrap \(0.031\), \(\text{SE}_0 = 0.027\).
                    • The HAC SE is smaller than \(\text{SE}_0\) for 14 of the 16 series (BET: \(0.023\) against \(0.028\)).
                    ↩ Back

                    Joint tests and reference distributions

                    Slide 91 in the PDF

                    A portmanteau test combines several lag correlations into one statistic instead of selecting the most striking lag, and a Wald test does the same for restrictions on regression coefficients. Equal means on the five weekdays impose four restrictions, \(\beta_1 = \beta_2\), \(\beta_1 = \beta_3\), \(\beta_1 = \beta_4\), \(\beta_1 = \beta_5\), so the reference distribution is \(\chi^2_4\), with 5% critical value 9.49. Five restrictions, all \(\beta_d = 0\), would test a different null: that every weekday mean is zero.

                    In brief

                    • A portmanteau test combines several lag correlations into one statistic.
                      • instead of selecting the most striking lag.
                    • A Wald test similarly tests coefficient restrictions jointly
                      • five equal weekday means impose four restrictions, \(\beta_1=\beta_2\), \(\beta_1=\beta_3\), \(\beta_1=\beta_4\), \(\beta_1=\beta_5\), so the reference is \(\chi^2_4\), with 5% critical value 9.49.
                    • Five restrictions, all \(\beta_d=0\), would test a different null: every weekday mean equal to zero.
                    ↩ Back

                    Portmanteau Tests: Ljung–Box and Its Robust Version

                    Slide 92 in the PDF

                    The Ljung–Box statistic tests jointly \(H_0: \rho_1 = \dots = \rho_m = 0\) under i.i.d. returns (Ljung & Box, 1978); its robust version (Lobato, Nankervis & Savin, 2001) replaces the i.i.d. variance by \(\hat\tau_k\) from (2.16), giving

                    \[Q(m) = T(T+2)\sum_{k=1}^m \frac{\hat\rho_k^2}{T-k}, \qquad \tilde Q(m) = \sum_{k=1}^m \frac{\hat\rho_k^2}{\hat\tau_k},\]
                    (2.17)

                    both compared with \(\chi^2_m\). The robust limit holds under RW3 when the \(\hat\rho_k\) at different lags are asymptotically uncorrelated, as for a GARCH with symmetric innovations. At \(m = 10\) (critical value 18.31), the S&P 500 has \(Q = 97.1\) but \(\tilde Q = 20.0\) (\(p = 0.029\)), the BET \(Q = 108.0\) and \(\tilde Q = 24.4\) (\(p = 0.007\)), and Bitcoin \(Q = 20.2\) (\(p = 0.028\)) but \(\tilde Q = 11.9\) (\(p = 0.294\)). The robust test keeps the rejections for the S&P 500 and the BET but not for Bitcoin; \(\tilde Q\) requires a finite fourth moment, which Chapter 1 questions.

                    In brief

                    • Ljung & Box (1978): \(Q(m) = T(T+2) \sum_{k=1}^m \hat\rho_k^2/(T-k) \sim \chi^2_m\) under i.i.d. returns
                      • Tests jointly \(H_0\): \(\rho_1 = \dots = \rho_m = 0\)
                      • \(m\): the number of lags
                    • Robust version Lobato, Nankervis & Savin (2001): \(\tilde Q(m) = \sum_{k=1}^m \hat\rho_k^2/\hat\tau_k \to \chi^2_m\) under RW3
                      • if the \(\hat\rho_k\) at different lags are asymptotically uncorrelated
                      • the condition, \(\E[\varepsilon_t^2\varepsilon_{t-j}\varepsilon_{t-k}] = 0\) for \(j \neq k\), holds for a GARCH with symmetric innovations
                    • Three markets at \(m = 10\) (5% critical value \(18.31\))
                      • S&P 500: \(Q = 97.1\) but \(\tilde Q = 20.0\) (\(p = 0.029\))
                      • BET: \(Q = 108.0\), \(\tilde Q = 24.4\) (\(p = 0.007\))
                      • Bitcoin: \(Q = 20.2\) (\(p = 0.028\)) but \(\tilde Q = 11.9\) (\(p = 0.294\))
                    • The robust test keeps the rejections for S&P 500 and BET, but not for Bitcoin
                      • \(\tilde Q\) requires a finite fourth moment, which Chapter 1 questions
                    ↩ Back

                    The Runs Test

                    Slide 93 in the PDF

                    The runs test (Wald & Wolfowitz, 1940) uses only the signs of returns, with zero-return days dropped, so heavy tails do not affect it. A run is a maximal block of equal signs. With \(n_1\) positive and \(n_2\) negative days, \(n = n_1 + n_2\), independent signs give \(\mathbb{E}[R] = 2n_1 n_2/n + 1\) runs, and the standardised number of runs is approximately standard Normal, assuming a constant probability of a positive day. Too few runs (\(z < 0\)) mean persistent signs, too many (\(z > 0\)) alternating signs. The S&P 500 has \(z = 4.34\), alternation consistent with \(\hat\rho_1 < 0\); the BET has \(z = -6.05\), persistence consistent with \(\hat\rho_1 > 0\); Bitcoin has \(z = 2.68\), a rejection although \(\hat\rho_1\) is not significant: signs and magnitudes carry different information.

                    In brief

                    • The runs test Wald & Wolfowitz (1940) uses only the signs of returns (zero-return days dropped), so heavy tails do not affect it
                    • A run is a maximal block of equal signs: ++|-|+++ has 3 runs
                    • With \(n_1\) positive days, \(n_2\) negative days and \(n = n_1 + n_2\), if the signs are independent:
                      • \(\E[R] = \dfrac{2 n_1 n_2}{n} + 1\),   \(\Var[R] = \dfrac{2 n_1 n_2 (2 n_1 n_2 - n)}{n^2 (n-1)}\),   \(z = \dfrac{R - \E[R]}{\sqrt{\Var[R]}} \approx N(0,1)\)
                      • \(R\): the observed number of runs; \(|z| > 1.96\) rejects independence at 5%
                      • the test assumes a constant probability of a positive day
                    • Too few runs (\(z < 0\)) mean signs that persist
                      • too many (\(z > 0\)) mean signs that alternate
                    • S&P 500: \(z = 4.34\), signs alternate, as \(\hat\rho_1 < 0\)
                    • BET: \(z = -6.05\), signs persist, as \(\hat\rho_1 > 0\)
                    • Bitcoin: \(z = 2.68\) rejects independent signs although \(\hat\rho_1\) is not significant
                      • signs and magnitudes carry different information
                      • all markets: Appendix
                    ↩ Back

                    The Lo–MacKinlay Test

                    Slide 94 in the PDF

                    Lo & MacKinlay (1988) estimate the variance ratio \(\text{VR}(q)\) from overlapping sums of \(q\) daily returns: \(\hat\sigma_a^2\) is the variance of one-day returns, \(\hat\sigma_c^2(q)\) the variance of the overlapping \(q\)-day sums divided by \(q\), with a divisor \(m = q(T-q+1)(1-q/T)\) that removes the small-sample bias, and \(\widehat{\text{VR}}(q) = \hat\sigma_c^2(q)/\hat\sigma_a^2\). Under RW1 (homoskedastic returns) the test statistic is

                    \[z(q) = \frac{\widehat{\text{VR}}(q) - 1}{\sqrt{2(2q-1)(q-1)/(3qT)}} \approx N(0,1),\]
                    (2.18)

                    and \(|z| > 1.96\) rejects \(\text{VR}(q) = 1\) at 5%. The statistic (2.18) rejects too often under volatility clustering, which motivates the robust version.

                    In brief

                    • Lo & MacKinlay (1988) estimate \(\text{VR}(q)\) from overlapping sums of \(q\) daily returns and correct the small-sample bias
                      • \(\hat\sigma_a^2 = \frac{1}{T-1}\sum_t (r_t - \hat\mu)^2\),   \(\hat\sigma_c^2(q) = \frac{1}{m}\sum_{t=q}^{T} \big(\textstyle\sum_{j=0}^{q-1} r_{t-j} - q\hat\mu\big)^2\)
                      • \(m = q(T - q + 1)(1 - q/T)\);   \(\widehat{\text{VR}}(q) = \hat\sigma_c^2(q) / \hat\sigma_a^2\)
                      • \(\hat\mu\): mean daily return; \(\hat\sigma_a^2\): variance of one-day returns; \(\hat\sigma_c^2(q)\): variance of the overlapping \(q\)-day sums divided by \(q\); \(m\): a divisor that removes the small-sample bias
                    • Test statistic under RW1 (homoskedastic returns, i.e. with constant variance)
                      • \(z(q) = \dfrac{\widehat{\text{VR}}(q) - 1}{\sqrt{2(2q-1)(q-1)/(3qT)}} \approx N(0,1)\)
                      • the denominator is the standard error of \(\widehat{\text{VR}}(q)\) under RW1; \(|z| > 1.96\) rejects \(\text{VR}(q) = 1\) at 5%
                    • This \(z\) rejects too often under volatility clustering (next slide)
                    ↩ Back

                    The Heteroskedasticity-Robust Statistic \(z^*\)

                    Slide 95 in the PDF

                    Under RW3 plus the conditions of Lo & MacKinlay (1988) (finite moments, weak dependence and \(\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_t^2 \varepsilon_{t-j}\varepsilon_{t-k}] = 0\) for \(j \neq k\)), the \(\hat\rho_k\) are asymptotically uncorrelated with variances \(\hat\delta_k/T\), where \(\hat\delta_k = T\hat\tau_k\) equals 1 without volatility clustering. Since \(\widehat{\text{VR}}(q) - 1\) weights \(\hat\rho_k\) by \(2(q-k)/q\), the robust statistic is

                    \[\hat\theta(q) = \sum_{k=1}^{q-1}\Big(\frac{2(q-k)}{q}\Big)^2 \hat\delta_k, \qquad z^*(q) = \frac{\sqrt{T}\,(\widehat{\text{VR}}(q) - 1)}{\sqrt{\hat\theta(q)}} \approx N(0,1).\]
                    (2.19)

                    RW3 alone is not enough: the conditions hold for a GARCH with symmetric innovations but can fail with a leverage effect, in which case \(\hat\theta(q)\) needs the full covariance matrix of the \(\hat\rho_k\). At \(q = 5\) the S&P 500 has \(z^*(5) = -2.78\): the reversal stays significant with the robust standard error.

                    In brief

                    • Under RW3 plus the conditions of Lo & MacKinlay (1988), the \(\hat\rho_k\) are asymptotically uncorrelated with variances \(\hat\delta_k / T\)
                      • finite moments, weak dependence (mixing), \(\E[\varepsilon_t^2 \varepsilon_{t-j} \varepsilon_{t-k}] = 0\) for \(j \neq k\)
                      • \(\hat\delta_k = T\hat\tau_k\), with \(\hat\tau_k\) of the autocorrelation slide: the robust variance of \(\sqrt{T}\hat\rho_k\), equal to 1 without volatility clustering
                    • Asymptotic variance of \(\widehat{\text{VR}}(q)\) and the test statistic
                      • \(\hat\theta(q) = \sum_{k=1}^{q-1} \Big(\dfrac{2(q-k)}{q}\Big)^2 \hat\delta_k, \qquad z^*(q) = \dfrac{\sqrt{T}\,(\widehat{\text{VR}}(q) - 1)}{\sqrt{\hat\theta(q)}} \approx N(0,1)\)
                      • \(2(q-k)/q\): the weight of \(\hat\rho_k\) in \(\widehat{\text{VR}}(q)\); \(|z^*| > 1.96\) rejects \(\text{VR}(q) = 1\) at 5%
                    • RW3 alone is not enough
                      • the conditions hold for GARCH with symmetric innovations
                      • but can fail with a leverage effect
                      • Without the condition \(\E[\varepsilon_t^2 \varepsilon_{t-j} \varepsilon_{t-k}] = 0\), \(\hat\theta(q)\) needs the full covariance matrix of the \(\hat\rho_k\)
                    • At \(q = 5\), the S&P 500 has \(z^*(5) = -2.78\)
                      • the reversal stays significant with the robust standard error
                      • the comparison of \(z\) with \(z^*\) at \(q = 2\): section 2
                    ↩ Back

                    The chart shows, for each of the 16 series of the chapter, the robust statistic \(z^*(5)\) of (2.19), with dashed lines at \(\pm 1.96\). Bars above the upper line indicate persistence over five days, bars below the lower line reversal.

                    The 5-Day Variance Ratio and the Chow–Denning Test

                    Slide 97 in the PDF

                    At five days, \(|z^*(5)| > 1.96\) for the BET (2.98), the S&P 500 (\(-2.78\)), EUR/RON (2.19) and the Euro Stoxx 50 (\(-2.08\)). The joint test of Chow & Denning (1993), which takes the largest \(|z^*|\) over several horizons and compares it with the critical value 2.49, rejects RW3 for 5 of the 16 series: the BET, the S&P 500, EUR/RON, the IPC (at 2 days, \(z^* = 3.72\)) and Ethereum (at 20 days, \(z^* = 2.70\)). The Euro Stoxx 50 rejects at five days but not in the joint test, since its largest \(|z^*|\) is 2.24. The results give no ranking of the form "developed means efficient": the S&P 500 shows reversal, the BET and EUR/RON persistence.

                    In brief

                    • Each bar on the previous chart is the robust statistic \(z^*(5)\) of one of the 16 series.
                      • the dashed lines mark \(\pm 1.96\).
                    • At 5 days, \(|z^*(5)| > 1.96\) for:
                      • the BET (\(2.98\)), the S&P 500 (\(-2.78\)).
                      • EUR/RON (\(2.19\)) and the Euro Stoxx 50 (\(-2.08\)).
                    • The joint Chow & Denning (1993) test rejects RW3 for 5 of the 16 series.
                      • critical value \(2.49\) (section 2).
                      • BET, S&P 500, EUR/RON.
                      • IPC (at 2 days, \(z^* = 3.72\)) and Ethereum (at 20 days, \(z^* = 2.70\)).
                    • The Euro Stoxx 50 rejects at 5 days but not in the joint test: its largest \(|z^*|\) is \(2.24 < 2.49\).
                    • No ranking “developed = efficient”: the S&P 500 shows reversal, the BET and EUR/RON persistence.

                    Beyond Linear Predictability: the Martingale Difference Hypothesis

                    Slide 98 in the PDF

                    The martingale difference hypothesis (MDH), \(\mathbb{E}[r_t \mid \mathcal{I}_{t-1}] = \mu\), states that no function of the past, linear or not, predicts the mean. RW3, which only sets all \(\rho_k\) to zero, is weaker. In the counterexample \(r_t = \varepsilon_t + \theta(\varepsilon_{t-1}^2 - \sigma^2)\), with \(\varepsilon_t\) i.i.d. symmetric and \(\sigma^2 = \operatorname{Var}(\varepsilon_t)\), all autocorrelations are zero, yet \(\mathbb{E}[r_t \mid \varepsilon_{t-1}] = \theta(\varepsilon_{t-1}^2 - \sigma^2)\) is predictable. The BDS test (Brock–Dechert–Scheinkman) (Brock, Dechert, Scheinkman & LeBaron, 1996) compares the frequency of nearby return sequences with that implied by i.i.d. observations; it tests i.i.d., not the MDH, and GARCH alone makes it reject, so a BDS rejection is evidence against RW1, not against weak-form efficiency.

                    In brief

                    • MDH (Martingale Difference Hypothesis): \(\E[r_t \mid \mathcal{I}_{t-1}] = \mu\)
                      • no function of the past predicts the mean, linear or not
                    • RW3 (all \(\rho_k = 0\)) is weaker. Counterexample: \(r_t = \varepsilon_t + \theta(\varepsilon_{t-1}^2 - \sigma^2)\), \(\varepsilon_t\) i.i.d. symmetric
                      • \(\sigma^2 = \Var(\varepsilon_t)\), so the extra term has mean 0; \(\theta \neq 0\): how strongly yesterday's squared shock moves today's mean
                      • all \(\rho_k = 0\), yet \(\E[r_t \mid \varepsilon_{t-1}] = \theta(\varepsilon_{t-1}^2 - \sigma^2)\) is predictable: autocorrelation tests cannot see it
                      • The MDH tests on the next slide look for exactly this kind of dependence (results: Appendix)
                    • BDS (Brock–Dechert–Scheinkman) Brock, Dechert, Scheinkman & LeBaron (1996) compares the frequency of nearby return sequences
                      • with that implied by i.i.d. observations
                      • it tests i.i.d., not the MDH
                      • GARCH alone makes it reject
                      • A BDS rejection is evidence against RW1, not against weak-form efficiency
                    ↩ Back

                    What nonlinear tests examine

                    Slide 99 in the PDF

                    The generalised spectral test (Escanciano & Velasco, 2006) measures the covariance between \(r_t\) and nonlinear functions \(e^{\mathrm{i}x r_{t-j}}\) of past returns at all lags \(j\), so it detects the counterexample of the previous frame, which autocorrelations miss. The automatic portmanteau test (Escanciano & Lobato, 2009) is a heteroskedasticity-robust Ljung–Box with the number of lags chosen from the data by a penalty; because it tests autocorrelations only, it does not detect the nonlinear counterexample.

                    In brief

                    • Why: the counterexample on the previous slide shows that zero autocorrelations do not rule out a predictable mean
                    • The generalised spectral test Escanciano & Velasco (2006) measures the covariance between \(r_t\) and nonlinear functions of past returns
                      • \(e^{\mathrm{i}x r_{t-j}}\), at all lags \(j\)
                      • it detects the counterexample \(r_t = \varepsilon_t + \theta(\varepsilon_{t-1}^2 - \sigma^2)\), which autocorrelations miss
                    • The automatic portmanteau test Escanciano & Lobato (2009) is a heteroskedasticity-robust Ljung–Box
                      • with the number of lags chosen from the data by a penalty
                      • it tests autocorrelations only, so it does not detect the nonlinear counterexample
                    ↩ Back

                    HAC standard errors and bandwidth

                    Slide 100 in the PDF

                    In predictive regressions and on overlapping rolling windows the residuals are autocorrelated and heteroskedastic, and classical ordinary least squares (OLS) standard errors are not valid. In the dividend–price regression they are too small (OLS \(t = 1.16\), Newey–West \(t = 0.91\)), although HAC errors can also be smaller than OLS ones, as in the calendar-effects table. HAC standard errors estimate the long-run variance, the variance plus twice the sum of the autocovariances; Newey & West (1987) weight the autocovariance at lag \(j\) by \(1 - j/(\ell+1)\) so that the estimate is never negative. The bandwidth \(\ell\) is the number of lags included: with \(\ell = 5\) the weights of lags 1–5 are \(5/6, 4/6, \dots, 1/6\).

                    In brief

                    OLS: Ordinary Least Squares.
                    • Why: the residuals are autocorrelated and heteroskedastic
                      • in predictive regressions and on overlapping rolling windows
                      • and classical OLS standard errors are then not valid
                      • in the dividend–price regression they are too small (OLS \(t = 1.16\), Newey–West \(t = 0.91\))
                      • HAC errors can also be smaller than OLS ones (the calendar-effects table)
                    • HAC standard errors estimate the long-run variance
                      • the variance plus twice the sum of the autocovariances
                    • Newey & West (1987) weight the autocovariance at lag \(j\) by \(1 - j/(\ell + 1)\), so that the estimate is never negative
                    • The bandwidth \(\ell\) is the number of lags included
                      • with \(\ell = 5\) the weights of lags 1–5 are \(5/6, 4/6, \dots, 1/6\)
                    ↩ Back

                    Bootstrap: constructing repeated samples

                    Slide 101 in the PDF

                    Neither the Stambaugh bias nor the DFA null band has a known exact distribution, and the bootstrap approximates it by generating many artificial samples under the null and recomputing the statistic in each. The i.i.d. bootstrap draws observations with replacement and destroys all dependence, volatility clustering included. The block bootstrap (Künsch, 1989) draws consecutive blocks and keeps the dependence within each block. The wild bootstrap multiplies each demeaned return by a random sign: it keeps each day's volatility but destroys serial correlation, reproducing an RW3-type null with symmetric signs, and is the one used for DFA.

                    In brief

                    • Why: neither the Stambaugh bias nor the DFA null band has a known exact distribution
                      • the bootstrap approximates it
                    • The bootstrap generates many artificial samples under the null and recomputes the statistic in each
                    • The i.i.d. bootstrap draws observations with replacement
                      • and destroys all dependence, volatility clustering included
                    • The block bootstrap Künsch (1989) draws consecutive blocks and keeps the dependence within each block
                    • The wild bootstrap multiplies each demeaned return by a random sign
                      • it keeps each day's volatility but destroys serial correlation: it reproduces an RW3-type null with symmetric signs (used for DFA)
                    ↩ Back

                    What each bootstrap preserves

                    Slide 102 in the PDF

                    The diagram compares the artificial samples of the bootstrap schemes of the previous frame. Blocks preserve the local ordering of returns, while sign flips preserve their absolute magnitudes; each scheme therefore imposes a different assumption on the artificial samples, and the choice determines which null hypothesis the resulting band represents.

                    In brief

                    Blocks preserve local ordering; sign flips preserve absolute magnitudes. They impose different assumptions on the artificial samples.
                    ↩ Back

                    Predictive Regressions and the Stambaugh Bias (1/2)

                    Slide 103 in the PDF

                    The question is why the dividend–price ratio of the section on out-of-sample predictability can look like a good predictor even when its true slope is zero. The model has two equations, \(r_{t+1} = \alpha + \beta x_t + u_{t+1}\) and \(x_{t+1} = \theta + \rho x_t + v_{t+1}\), with \(\operatorname{Cov}(u, v) = \sigma_{uv}\). Here \(r\) is the excess return, \(x\) the log dividend–price ratio \(dp_t = \ln D_t - \ln P_t\), with \(D_t\) the dividends of the last year, and \(\rho \approx 1\) its persistence, the AR(1) coefficient of \(dp\), distinct from the return autocorrelations \(\rho_k\) and from the \(\rho\) of the Campbell–Shiller identity.

                    In brief

                    • Back to the regression of section 3: why can \(dp\) look like a good predictor even when \(\beta = 0\)?
                    • Model: \(r_{t+1} = \alpha + \beta x_t + u_{t+1}\),   \(x_{t+1} = \theta + \rho x_t + v_{t+1}\), with \(\text{Cov}(u, v) = \sigma_{uv}\)
                      • \(r\): excess return; \(x\): log dividend–price ratio \(dp_t = \ln D_t - \ln P_t\), persistent (\(\rho \approx 1\))
                      • \(D_t\): dividends paid over the last year; \(\beta\): the predictive slope, 0 if returns are unpredictable; \(\alpha\), \(\theta\): intercepts
                      • \(\rho\): the persistence of \(x\), the AR(1) coefficient of \(dp\) (not the return autocorrelation \(\rho_k\), nor the \(\rho\) of the Campbell–Shiller identity); \(u\), \(v\): the shocks of the two equations; \(\sigma_{uv}\): their covariance; \(\sigma_v^2 = \Var(v)\); \(T\): number of periods
                    ↩ Back

                    Predictive Regressions and the Stambaugh Bias (2/2)

                    Slide 104 in the PDF

                    The predictor is predetermined but not strictly exogenous, so OLS is consistent but biased (Stambaugh, 1999). Projecting \(u\) on \(v\) gives \(\hat\beta - \beta = (\sigma_{uv}/\sigma_v^2)(\hat\rho - \rho)\) plus noise with mean zero, for instance when \((u, v)\) are i.i.d. with a bivariate Normal distribution. Since \(\hat\rho\) is biased downward in small samples (Kendall, 1954), \(\mathbb{E}[\hat\rho - \rho] \approx -(1+3\rho)/T\), the error passes into the slope:

                    \[\mathbb{E}[\hat\beta - \beta] \approx -\frac{\sigma_{uv}}{\sigma_v^2}\,\frac{1+3\rho}{T}.\]
                    (2.20)

                    A price rise lowers \(dp\), so \(\sigma_{uv} < 0\) and the bias in (2.20) is upward: predictability looks stronger than it is. Near a unit root the \(t\)-statistic is not standard Normal either, and valid inference uses the Bonferroni \(Q\)-test of Campbell & Yogo (2006).

                    In brief

                    • \(x_t\) is predetermined, not strictly exogenous: OLS is consistent but biased Stambaugh (1999)
                      • Project \(u\) on \(v\): \(u = (\sigma_{uv}/\sigma_v^2)\, v + e\), so \(\hat\beta - \beta = (\sigma_{uv}/\sigma_v^2)(\hat\rho - \rho) + \text{noise}\); \(e\): the part of \(u\) unrelated to \(v\)
                      • Both regressions use the same months and the same \(x_t\), so the estimation errors \(\hat\beta - \beta\) and \(\hat\rho - \rho\) are linked through the correlation of the shocks \(u\) and \(v\); the noise term has mean zero when \(e\) is independent of the whole path of \(x\), e.g. \((u, v)\) i.i.d. with a bivariate Normal distribution
                      • \(\hat\rho\) is biased downward in small samples Kendall (1954), and this error passes into \(\hat\beta\)
                      • With \(\E[\hat\rho - \rho] \approx -(1 + 3\rho)/T\):   \(\E[\hat\beta - \beta] \approx -\dfrac{\sigma_{uv}}{\sigma_v^2}\,\dfrac{1 + 3\rho}{T}\)
                    • Price up \(\Rightarrow\) \(dp\) down: \(\sigma_{uv} < 0\), so the bias is upward: predictability looks stronger than it is
                    • Near a unit root the \(t\)-statistic is not \(N(0,1)\) either
                      • valid inference by a Bonferroni \(Q\)-test over a confidence interval for \(\rho\) Campbell & Yogo (2006)
                    ↩ Back

                    Bonferroni Q: allowing for uncertain persistence

                    Slide 105 in the PDF

                    The inference of Campbell & Yogo (2006) allows for uncertainty about the persistence of the predictor instead of treating its estimate as known: it combines a confidence set for \(\rho\) with tests of the predictive slope over that set, allocating the error probability across the steps. For the S&P 500, \(\hat\rho = 0.9988\) and \(T(1-\hat\rho) = 1.4\), so a unit-root test cannot reject \(\rho = 1\), which is where the \(t\)-statistic of \(\hat\beta\) departs most from the standard Normal. This \(Q\)-test concerns predictive regressions and is not the Ljung–Box \(Q\) statistic.

                    In brief

                    • Campbell–Yogo inference allows for uncertainty about predictor persistence. Campbell & Yogo (2006)
                      • instead of treating its estimate as known.
                    • It combines a confidence set for persistence with tests of the predictive slope over that set.
                      • allocating the error probability across the steps.
                    • For the S&P 500, \(\hat\rho = 0.9988\) and \(T(1 - \hat\rho) = 1.4\).
                      • a unit-root test cannot reject \(\rho = 1\).
                      • there the \(t\)-statistic of \(\hat\beta\) departs most from \(N(0,1)\).
                    • This Q-test concerns predictive regressions
                      • it is not the Ljung–Box Q statistic for autocorrelations.
                    ↩ Back

                    Worked Example: the Stambaugh Bias for the S&P 500

                    Slide 106 in the PDF

                    The regression uses the monthly log excess return of the S&P 500 on the lagged \(dp_t\) over 1934–2026, \(T = 1\,111\) months. OLS gives \(\hat\beta = 0.267\) (percent per month per unit of \(dp\)) with Newey–West \(t = 0.91\), not significant even before any correction. With \(\hat\rho = 0.9988\), \(\operatorname{corr}(\hat u, \hat v) = -0.97\) and \(\hat\phi = \hat\sigma_{uv}/\hat\sigma_v^2 = -0.925\), formula (2.20) gives a bias of 0.333, so the corrected slope is \(0.267 - 0.333 = -0.066\): the estimated first-order correction exceeds the estimated slope. The correction is itself estimated, and Kendall's formula is least accurate when \(T(1-\hat\rho)\) is small, here 1.4, so \(-0.066\) indicates sign and size, not a precise slope. In a simulation with the same \(\rho\) and \(\operatorname{corr}(u, v)\), \(\beta = 0\) and 360 months, the nominal 5% \(t\)-test rejects in 26.5% of samples, more than five times too often.

                    In brief

                    • Monthly log excess return regressed on the lagged \(dp_t\), 1934–2026, \(T = 1\,111\) months
                      • data construction: Appendix
                    • OLS: \(\hat\beta = 0.267\), Newey–West \(t = 0.91\): not significant even before any correction
                      • in % per month per unit of \(dp\)
                    • Inputs: \(\hat\rho = 0.9988\), \(\text{corr}(\hat u, \hat v) = -0.97\), \(\hat\phi = \hat\sigma_{uv}/\hat\sigma_v^2 = -0.925\) (as fractions, not in %)
                    • Substitution: bias \(= -100\,\hat\phi\,(1 + 3\hat\rho)/T = 0.333\) (in %, like \(\hat\beta\))
                      • corrected slope \(= 0.267 - 0.333 = -0.066\): the estimated first-order bias correction exceeds the estimated slope
                      • the correction is itself estimated: formal inference by Campbell & Yogo (2006) or a bootstrap
                      • Kendall's formula is first order in \(1/T\) and least accurate when \(T(1 - \hat\rho)\) is small, here \(1.4\): read \(-0.066\) as indicative of the sign and size, not as a precise slope
                    • Simulation: the nominal 5% \(t\)-test rejects \(\beta = 0\) in \(26.5\%\) of samples
                      • with the same \(\rho\) and \(\text{corr}(u, v)\), \(\beta = 0\) and a 30-year sample (\(T = 360\) months)
                      • more than five times too often
                    ↩ Back

                    Comparing Forecasts: Diebold–Mariano and Clark–West

                    Slide 107 in the PDF

                    The section on out-of-sample predictability defined the out-of-sample \(R^2_{OS}\) and applied the Clark–West test; this frame explains its form. Most published predictors have \(R^2_{OS} < 0\) (Welch & Goyal, 2008), which the update of Goyal, Welch & Zafirov (2024) confirms. The test of Diebold & Mariano (1995) compares the mean difference between the squared errors of two forecasts. The \(dp\) model nests the historical mean: under the null it estimates a zero \(\beta\), the noise of that estimate inflates its mean squared prediction error (MSPE), and the Diebold–Mariano test rejects less often than its nominal level. Clark & West (2007) add this noise back:

                    \[f_t = (r_t - \bar r_t)^2 - \big[(r_t - \hat r_t)^2 - (\bar r_t - \hat r_t)^2\big],\]
                    (2.21)

                    where \((\bar r_t - \hat r_t)^2\) is the part of the model's error due to estimating \(\beta\); the \(t\) test on the mean of \(f_t\) in (2.21) is one-sided, since only a positive gain is evidence for the predictor.

                    In brief

                    • Section 3 defined \(R^2_{OS}\) and applied the Clark–West test to the S&P 500
                      • here we show why the test has this form
                    • Most published predictors have \(R^2_{OS} < 0\) Welch & Goyal (2008)
                      • the update Goyal, Welch & Zafirov (2024) confirms it
                    • The Diebold & Mariano (1995) test compares the mean difference between the squared errors of the two forecasts
                    • The \(dp\) model contains the historical mean (nested models)
                      • under \(H_0\) it estimates a zero \(\beta\)
                      • the noise of that estimate inflates its mean squared prediction error (MSPE)
                    • So the Diebold–Mariano test rejects less often than its nominal level
                    • Clark & West (2007) add this noise back: \(f_t = (r_t - \bar r_t)^2 - \big[(r_t - \hat r_t)^2 - (\bar r_t - \hat r_t)^2\big]\)
                      • \((\bar r_t - \hat r_t)^2\): the part of the model's error due to estimating \(\beta\)
                      • the \(t\) test on the mean of \(f_t\) is one-sided: only a positive gain is evidence for the predictor
                    ↩ Back

                    Dependence across lags and horizons

                    Slide 108 in the PDF

                    The diagram contrasts short and long memory. The autocorrelations of an AR(1) process fade geometrically, while those of the fractional Gaussian noise (fGn) example fade slowly; the right panel compares how fluctuations grow with the horizon in the two cases, which is what the R/S and DFA statistics below measure.

                    In brief

                    AR(1) correlations fade geometrically; the fGn example fades slowly. The right panel compares fluctuation growth across horizons.

                    The Rescaled Range (R/S) Statistic

                    Slide 109 in the PDF

                    Using the rescaled range, Hurst (1951) found that years of high Nile floods followed other high-flood years more often than chance allows. For a window of \(n\) returns with mean \(\bar r_n\) and standard deviation \(s_n\), the cumulative deviations are \(Y_k = \sum_{i=1}^k (r_i - \bar r_n)\), the range is \(R_n = \max_k Y_k - \min_k Y_k\), and the statistic is \(R_n/s_n\). The scaling law

                    \[\mathbb{E}[R_n/s_n] \approx c\,n^H\]
                    (2.22)

                    defines the Hurst exponent \(H\) as the slope of \(\ln(R/S)\) on \(\ln n\), with \(R/S\) averaged over non-overlapping windows. \(H = 0.5\) means no long memory (short-run dependence is allowed), \(H > 0.5\) persistence with \(\rho_k \sim k^{2H-2}\), and \(H < 0.5\) anti-persistence.

                    In brief

                    • Using the rescaled range, Hurst (1951) found that years of high Nile floods followed other high-flood years
                      • more often than chance allows
                      • history: Appendix
                    • Construction for a window of \(n\) returns with mean \(\bar r_n\) and standard deviation \(s_n\)
                      • Cumulative deviations \(Y_k = \sum_{i=1}^k (r_i - \bar r_n)\), \(k = 1, \dots, n\)
                      • Range \(R_n = \max_k Y_k - \min_k Y_k\); rescaled range \(R_n / s_n\)
                    • Scaling law: \(\E[R_n/s_n] \approx c\, n^H\), where \(H\) is the Hurst exponent
                      • \(c\): a constant; taking logs, \(\ln(R/S) \approx \ln c + H \ln n\), so \(H\) is a slope
                      • Estimate: slope of \(\ln(R/S)\) on \(\ln n\), averaging \(R/S\) over non-overlapping windows
                    • \(H = 0.5\): no long memory (short-run dependence allowed)
                      • \(H > 0.5\): persistence, long memory with \(\rho_k \sim k^{2H-2}\)
                      • \(H < 0.5\): anti-persistence
                    ↩ Back

                    R/S Analysis: S&P 500 and BET (2/2)

                    Slide 111 in the PDF

                    The solid line on the chart is the Anis & Lloyd (1976) expectation for i.i.d. returns from the Normal distribution. In small samples the raw slope exceeds 0.5 even for i.i.d. returns, and the Anis–Lloyd correction removes this bias: \(H_{AL}\) is 0.5 plus the slope of \(\ln(R/S) - \ln\mathbb{E}[R_n/s_n]\) on \(\ln n\). For the S&P 500 the raw exponent is 0.530 and the corrected one 0.485, so the apparent persistence came from small-sample bias. For the BET the raw exponent is 0.578 and the corrected one 0.533, so persistence remains. Whether this reflects long memory or only the lag-1 autocorrelation \(\hat\rho_1 = 0.109\) is an open question that the classical R/S statistic cannot settle.

                    In brief

                    • On the previous chart, the dots show the average \(R/S\) for each window length \(n\).
                    • The solid line is the Anis & Lloyd (1976) expectation for i.i.d. returns from the Normal distribution.
                      • formula: Appendix.
                    • The Anis–Lloyd correction removes the bias of the raw slope.
                      • The raw slope exceeds \(0.5\) in small samples even for i.i.d. returns.
                      • \(H_{AL} = 0.5 +\) the slope of the regression of \(\ln(R/S) - \ln \E[R_n/s_n]\) on \(\ln n\).
                    • For the S&P 500, the raw exponent is \(0.530\) and the corrected one \(0.485\).
                      • the apparent “persistence” came from small-sample bias.
                    • For the BET, the raw exponent is \(0.578\) and the corrected one \(0.533\).
                      • persistence remains after the correction.
                    • Open question: is BET's \(H > 0.5\) long memory, or just its lag-1 autocorrelation \(\hat\rho_1 = 0.109\)?
                    • The classical R/S statistic cannot tell the two apart.

                    Lo's Modified R/S Test

                    Slide 112 in the PDF

                    Lo (1991) shows that short-run autocorrelation inflates R/S and mimics long memory. His modification replaces \(s_n\) by a long-run (HAC) standard deviation \(\hat\sigma_\ell\), with \(\hat\sigma_\ell^2 = \hat\gamma_0 + 2\sum_{j=1}^\ell (1 - j/(\ell+1))\hat\gamma_j\), and uses the full sample: \(V = (\max_k Y_k - \min_k Y_k)/(\hat\sigma_\ell\sqrt{T})\), with bandwidth chosen as in Andrews (1991). Under short memory \(V\) lies in \([0.809, 1.862]\) with probability 95%. Short memory is not rejected for the S&P 500 (\(V = 1.560\)), Bitcoin (1.547), the PX (1.749) and EUR/RON (1.429); for the BET (\(V = 1.961\), \(\ell = 7\)) and Ethereum (1.980), \(V\) is just above the upper limit, weak evidence of long memory.

                    In brief

                    • Lo (1991): short-run autocorrelation inflates R/S and mimics long memory
                    • Modification: \(s_n\) is replaced by a long-run (HAC) standard deviation \(\hat\sigma_\ell\) with \(\ell\) lags
                      • \(\hat\sigma_\ell^2 = \hat\gamma_0 + 2 \sum_{j=1}^{\ell} \big(1 - \tfrac{j}{\ell + 1}\big) \hat\gamma_j\) , \(\hat\gamma_j\): autocovariance at lag \(j\) (\(\hat\gamma_0\): the variance), with declining weights
                      • \(V = \dfrac{\max_k Y_k - \min_k Y_k}{\hat\sigma_\ell \sqrt{T}}\) ; \(Y_k\): cumulative deviations over the full sample of \(T\) returns; bandwidth \(\ell\) chosen as in Andrews (1991)
                    • Under short memory, \(V\) lies in \([0.809, 1.862]\) with probability \(95\%\)
                    • \(V\) is inside the interval: short memory is not rejected
                      • for the S&P 500 (\(V = 1.560\)), Bitcoin (\(1.547\)), PX (\(1.749\)) and EUR/RON (\(1.429\))
                    • For the BET (\(V = 1.961\), with \(\ell = 7\) lags) and Ethereum (\(V = 1.980\)), \(V\) is just above \(1.862\)
                    • This is weak evidence of long memory
                    ↩ Back

                    Detrended Fluctuation Analysis (DFA) (1/2)

                    Slide 113 in the PDF

                    Detrended fluctuation analysis (DFA) (Peng et al., 1994) measures how the fluctuation of cumulative returns grows with the window length. The profile \(y_k = \sum_{t \le k}(r_t - \bar r)\) is split into boxes of length \(n\); in each box a linear trend is fitted, and \(F(n)\) is the root mean square of all residuals, computed for many \(n\). Removing a linear trend from the profile removes a constant mean of returns in each box; if the mean itself trends, higher-order DFA is needed. The scaling \(F(n) \propto n^{\alpha_{DFA}}\) defines the exponent \(\alpha_{DFA}\), which plays the role of \(H\), with \(\alpha_{DFA} = 0.5\) meaning no long memory.

                    In brief

                    • Peng et al. (1994): DFA measures how the fluctuation of cumulative returns grows with the window length.
                    • Removing a linear trend from the profile removes a constant mean of returns in each box.
                    • If the mean of returns itself trends, higher-order DFA is needed.
                    • The algorithm
                      • The profile \(y_k = \sum_{t \leq k} (r_t - \bar r)\) is the running sum of deviations from the mean, over the full sample.
                      • The profile is split into boxes of length \(n\).
                      • In each box a linear trend is fitted and the residuals are kept.
                      • \(F(n)\) is the root mean square of all residuals; the calculation is repeated for many \(n\).
                    • \(F(n) \propto n^{\alpha_{DFA}}\): the DFA exponent \(\alpha_{DFA}\) plays the role of \(H\) (\(\alpha_{DFA} = 0.5\): no long memory).
                    ↩ Back

                    Detrended Fluctuation Analysis (DFA) (2/2)

                    Slide 114 in the PDF

                    The null band for \(\alpha_{DFA}\) comes from a wild bootstrap with 199 replications, in which each residual \(e_t = r_t - \bar r\) is multiplied by a random sign \(\eta_t = \pm 1\) with probability 1/2. The sign flip keeps the volatility \(|e_t|\), allowed under RW3, and destroys only serial correlation, whereas a shuffle would destroy both. The implicit null is stricter than RW3, since it requires signs that are symmetric and independent given volatility, so the band is a benchmark, neither an exact test of RW3 nor a confidence interval for \(\alpha_{DFA}\).

                    In brief

                    • The null band comes from a wild bootstrap (199 replications).
                      • each residual \(e_t = r_t - \bar r\) is multiplied by a random sign \(\eta_t = \pm 1\), with probability \(1/2\).
                    • The sign flip keeps the volatility \(|e_t|\), allowed under RW3, and destroys only serial correlation.
                      • a shuffle would destroy both.
                    • The implicit null is stricter than RW3: signs symmetric and independent, given volatility.
                    • The band is therefore a benchmark, not an exact test of RW3 and not a confidence interval for \(\alpha_{DFA}\).
                    ↩ Back

                    DFA Exponent Across Markets (2/2)

                    Slide 116 in the PDF

                    The S&P 500 (0.494), the Euro Stoxx 50 (0.488) and the Nikkei (0.503) are close to 0.5 and inside their bands. Three of the 16 series exceed the band: Ethereum (0.581), the BET (0.580, against an upper limit of 0.561) and, marginally, Bitcoin (0.576 against 0.575). The PX (0.553) and Shanghai (0.554) exceed only a band built by shuffling, which also destroys volatility clustering, so their apparent memory comes from volatility clustering. The local Whittle estimator (Robinson, 1995) estimates \(d = H - 0.5\) from the lowest frequencies; for the BET, \(\hat d = 0.118\) with 95% interval \([0.062, 0.174]\), but the interval assumes conditionally homoskedastic innovations, which volatility clustering violates. The evidence of long memory in returns is weak and depends on the method, while memory in volatility (Chapter 1) is strong.

                    In brief

                    • Each dot on the previous chart is the exponent \(\alpha_{DFA}\) of one series.
                      • the bar is the 95% wild-bootstrap null band.
                    • S&P 500 (\(0.494\)), Euro Stoxx 50 (\(0.488\)) and Nikkei (\(0.503\)) are close to \(0.5\), inside the null band.
                    • Three of the 16 series exceed the null band.
                      • Ethereum (\(0.581\)).
                      • BET (\(0.580\), against an upper limit of \(0.561\)).
                      • and, marginally, Bitcoin (\(0.576\) against \(0.575\)).
                    • PX (\(0.553\)) and Shanghai (\(0.554\)) exceed only a null band built by shuffling.
                      • Shuffling also destroys volatility clustering.
                      • their apparent memory comes from volatility clustering.
                    • The local Whittle estimator Robinson (1995) estimates \(d = H - 0.5\) from the lowest frequencies.
                      • for the BET, \(\hat d = 0.118\), 95% interval \([0.062, 0.174]\) (Appendix).
                    • The interval assumes conditionally homoskedastic innovations.
                      • volatility clustering violates this assumption.
                      • so it is not decisive evidence.
                    • Conclusion: the evidence of long memory in returns is weak and depends on the method.
                      • memory in volatility (Chapter 1) is strong.
                    lll MFM_ch2_hurst
                    ↩ Back

                    Adaptive Markets Hypothesis (AMH) (1/2)

                    Slide 117 in the PDF

                    Full-sample tests give weak and unstable evidence, and one possible reason is that efficiency varies over time. Andrew Lo proposed the Adaptive Markets Hypothesis (AMH) in Lo (2004) and developed it in the book Lo (2017). Markets are described as an ecology of competing species, traders, funds and algorithms, that adapt by trial and error rather than by perfect optimisation; efficiency is a matter of degree and depends on the number of competitors, the profit opportunities and the environment, and anomalies appear, weaken and may reappear. Time-varying statistics alone do not identify the AMH, because an Efficient Market Hypothesis (EMH) with time-varying risk premia also allows them.

                    In brief

                    Andrew W. Lo, MIT Photo: David Shankbone (2012); CC BY 3.0; Wikimedia Commons
                    • Full-sample tests give weak and unstable evidence
                      • one possible reason: efficiency varies over time
                    • Andrew Lo proposed the Adaptive Markets Hypothesis (AMH) in Lo (2004) and developed it in the book Lo (2017)
                    • Markets are an ecology of competing species that adapt by trial and error
                      • traders, funds, algorithms
                      • not by perfect optimisation
                      • efficiency is a matter of degree and depends on the number of competitors, the profit opportunities and the environment
                    • Anomalies appear, weaken and may reappear
                    • Time-varying statistics alone do not identify the AMH
                      • since an Efficient Market Hypothesis (EMH) with time-varying risk premia also allows them
                      • comparison table: Appendix
                    ↩ Back

                    Adaptive Markets Hypothesis (AMH) (2/2)

                    Slide 118 in the PDF

                    Two strands of evidence support time variation. The survey of Lim & Brooks (2011) finds that stock-market efficiency varies over time, especially around crises. Urquhart & McGroarty (2016) find on four major indices (US, UK, Japan, euro area) that serial dependence appears and disappears across subperiods with market conditions.

                    In brief

                    • Lim & Brooks (2011), a survey: stock-market efficiency varies over time, especially around crises
                    • Urquhart & McGroarty (2016): serial dependence appears and disappears across subperiods with market conditions
                      • on four major indices (US, UK, Japan, euro area)

                    Measuring Time-Varying Efficiency

                    Slide 119 in the PDF

                    Time-varying efficiency is measured by computing \(z^*(5)\) and the DFA exponent on rolling windows of 500 trading days, about two years, moved forward by 21 days, one month. The DFA band is computed by random sign flips, as before, but separately in each window, so that it follows the window's volatility. For comparison, a fixed band \([0.380, 0.615]\) is simulated from i.i.d. Student-\(t\) returns with 4 degrees of freedom: heavy tails without volatility clustering. Because clustering, allowed under RW3, raises \(\alpha_{DFA}\), the fixed band flags too many windows. Two cautions apply:

                    • Overlapping windows are strongly correlated, so runs of rejections are not independent evidence;
                    • About 5% of windows reject even under the null, so a share near 5% is a description, not a calibrated test of stability.

                    In brief

                    • Compute a statistic on a rolling window
                      • 500 trading days (about two years)
                      • moved forward by 21 days (one month)
                      • Statistics: \(z^*(5)\) of the variance-ratio test and the DFA exponent
                    • The DFA band is computed as before (random sign flips), but separately in each window
                      • so it follows the window's volatility
                    • For comparison, a fixed null band is \([0.380, 0.615]\)
                      • simulated from i.i.d. Student-\(t\) returns with 4 degrees of freedom
                      • heavy tails, no volatility clustering
                    • The fixed band ignores volatility clustering, allowed under RW3
                      • volatility clustering raises \(\alpha_{DFA}\)
                      • so the fixed band flags too many windows
                    • Two cautions for reading the results
                      • Overlapping windows are strongly correlated: runs of rejections are not independent evidence
                      • Even under the null about 5% of windows reject; a share near 5% is a description, not a calibrated test of stability

                    The AMH, Tested

                    Slide 123 in the PDF

                    Kim, Shamsuddin & Lim (2011) show, over a century of US data, that predictability varies with market conditions such as crises and bubbles rather than along a declining trend, and make the AMH testable by linking it to state variables. The chapter's version regresses the rolling \(z^*(5)\) on the window's annualised volatility (in percent) and a crisis indicator equal to 1 if the window overlaps September 2008–June 2009 or February–June 2020, with Newey–West errors of 24 lags because consecutive windows share 95% of their days. For the S&P 500 the crisis coefficient is \(-0.71\) (\(t = -2.53\)) and the volatility coefficient 0.008 (\(t = 0.39\)), \(R^2 = 0.21\); for the BET they are \(-0.82\) (\(t = -2.77\)) and \(-0.008\) (\(t = -0.36\)), \(R^2 = 0.15\). In crisis windows \(z^*(5)\) is lower, closer to reversal, even at equal volatility. The result supports an association conditional on volatility, not a causal effect; it is consistent with the AMH but does not identify it, because a change in \(z^*(5)\) can come from \(\widehat{\text{VR}}(5) - 1\) or from its standard error.

                    In brief

                    • Kim, Shamsuddin & Lim (2011) show that predictability varies with market conditions (crises, bubbles).
                      • rather than along a declining trend.
                      • over a century of US data.
                      • and make the AMH testable by linking it to state variables.
                    • Our version: regress the rolling \(z^*(5)\) on the window's annualised volatility (%) and a crisis indicator
                      • The crisis indicator is 1 if the window overlaps Sep 2008–Jun 2009 or Feb–Jun 2020.
                      • Newey–West errors with 24 lags, because consecutive windows share 95% of their days.
                      • Volatility is the control variable: windows with similar volatility are compared.
                    • S&P 500: the crisis coefficient is \(-0.71\) (\(t = -2.53\)).
                      • the volatility coefficient \(0.008\) (\(t = 0.39\)).
                      • \(R^2 = 0.21\).
                    • BET: the crisis coefficient is \(-0.82\) (\(t = -2.77\)).
                      • the volatility coefficient \(-0.008\) (\(t = -0.36\)).
                      • \(R^2 = 0.15\).
                    • In crisis windows \(z^*(5)\) is lower, i.e. closer to reversal, even at equal volatility.
                    • What the result supports: association.
                      • conditional on volatility, not a causal effect.
                    • State dependence is consistent with the AMH but does not identify it.
                      • a change in \(z^*(5)\) can come from \(\widehat{\text{VR}}(5) - 1\) or from its standard error.
                    ↩ Back

                    Crypto Efficiency Year by Year (2/2)

                    Slide 125 in the PDF

                    Only one of the 27 yearly autocorrelations, 16 for Bitcoin and 11 for Ethereum, is significant at 5%: Ethereum in 2023, \(\hat\rho_1 = -0.107\), against about 1.35 expected by chance alone. Urquhart (2016) rejects efficiency in the early years with other tests, another price source and classical standard errors. Power is limited: one year of 365 returns gives a robust standard error of about 0.05–0.13, so small effects are invisible, and the robust error itself requires a finite fourth moment, which Chapter 1 questions.
