                    Event Studies: the Prediction Error

                    Slide 126 in the PDF

                    The abnormal return on an event day is a prediction error of the market model, and its variance (MacKinlay, 1997) is

                    \[\operatorname{Var}(AR_\tau) = \sigma_\varepsilon^2\Big[1 + \frac{1}{T_0} + \frac{(r_{m,\tau} - \hat\mu_m)^2}{(T_0 - 1)\hat\sigma_m^2}\Big],\]
                    (2.23)

                    where \(T_0\) is the number of estimation days, \(\sigma_\varepsilon^2\) the residual variance, \(r_{m,\tau}\) the market return on the event day and \(\hat\mu_m\), \(\hat\sigma_m^2\) the mean and variance of the market return in the estimation window. The bracket in (2.23) grows when the event-day market move is unusual: for the bank tax of the section on event studies, the extreme BET move makes it 2.12, hence \(t = -6.85\) instead of \(-10.0\) for TLV, and \(t = -4.03\) for BRD. Boehmer, Musumeci & Poulsen (1991) standardise each abnormal return by its own prediction error and estimate the variance from the firms' standardised values on the event day, so the test stays valid if the event raises the variance.

                    In brief

                    • Prediction-error variance MacKinlay (1997): \(\Var(AR_\tau) = \sigma_\varepsilon^2\Big[1 + \dfrac{1}{T_0} + \dfrac{(r_{m,\tau} - \hat\mu_m)^2}{(T_0 - 1)\hat\sigma_m^2}\Big]\), \(T_0\): estimation days
                      • \(\sigma_\varepsilon^2\): the residual variance of the market model; \(r_{m,\tau}\): the market return on event day \(\tau\).
                      • \(\hat\mu_m\), \(\hat\sigma_m^2\): mean and variance of the market return in the estimation window.
                      • The bracket grows when the event-day market move is unusual.
                    • For the bank tax (section 4), the extreme BET move makes the bracket \(2.12\).
                      • hence \(t = -6.85\) instead of \(-10.0\) for TLV.
                    • With the same correction, BRD has \(t = -4.03\).
                    • Boehmer, Musumeci & Poulsen (1991) standardise each \(AR\) by its own prediction error.
                      • estimate the variance from the firms' standardised values on the event day, not from calm days.
                      • the test stays valid if the event raises the variance.
                    ↩ Back

                    Event Studies: Correlation Across Firms

                    Slide 127 in the PDF

                    When all \(N\) firms have the event on the same day, their abnormal returns are correlated, with average correlation \(\bar\varrho\) estimated in the estimation window; for TLV and BRD, \(\bar\varrho = 0.153\). A test that assumes independence ignores the covariances, and the true variance of the mean is \(1 + (N-1)\bar\varrho\) times larger. For TLV and BRD the independence-based \(t\) of \(-7.55\), divided by \(\sqrt{1 + \bar\varrho} = 1.074\), becomes \(-7.03\), the \(t\) of the equal-weighted portfolio, which allows for the correlation automatically. A cross-sectional variance estimate, as in Boehmer, Musumeci & Poulsen (1991), further understates the variance by the factor \(1 - \bar\varrho\), which is why Kolari & Pynnönen (2010) multiply the statistic by \(\sqrt{(1-\bar\varrho)/(1+(N-1)\bar\varrho)}\). With ten firms and the same correlation, the understatement is a factor of 2.38 for the independence-based test and 2.81 for the cross-sectional one.

                    In brief

                    • The question: what happens to the test of \(\overline{AR}_0\) when all firms have the event on the same day?
                    • Let \(N\) be the number of firms and \(\bar\varrho\) the average correlation of their abnormal returns.
                      • estimated in the estimation window.
                      • for TLV and BRD, \(\bar\varrho = 0.153\).
                    • The test that assumes independence ignores the covariances.
                      • with \(\bar\varrho > 0\) the true variance of the mean is \(1 + (N-1)\bar\varrho\) times larger.
                    • For TLV and BRD, the \(t\) computed under independence is \(-7.55\).
                      • divided by \(\sqrt{1 + \bar\varrho} = 1.074\) it becomes \(-7.03\).
                    • That is the \(t\) of the equal-weighted portfolio, which allows for the correlation automatically.
                    • If the variance is estimated cross-sectionally, as in Boehmer, Musumeci & Poulsen (1991), it further understates the true variance.
                      • by the factor \(1 - \bar\varrho\).
                      • this is why Kolari & Pynnönen (2010) multiply the statistic by \(\sqrt{(1 - \bar\varrho)/(1 + (N - 1)\bar\varrho)}\).
                    • With ten firms and the same correlation, the independence-based test understates the variance.
                      • By a factor of \(1 + 9\bar\varrho = 2.38\).
                      • the cross-sectional test by \((1 + 9\bar\varrho)/(1 - \bar\varrho) = 2.81\).
                    ↩ Back

                    Anomalies and Calendar Effects

                    Slide 128 in the PDF

                    An anomaly is a return pattern that the asset-pricing model does not explain, and it has three possible explanations: an omitted risk factor, mispricing due to a behavioural bias, or data snooping. Anomalies come in families:

                    • Calendar effects, such as the turn of the month (Lakonishok & Smidt, 1988) and the first half of the month (Ariel, 1987);
                    • Time-series effects, such as time-series momentum;
                    • Cross-sectional effects, such as size, value and momentum (Chapter 3).

                    Returns on published anomalies fall by 26% after the end of the sample studied and by 58% after publication (McLean & Pontiff, 2016). The day-of-week effect is estimated from \(r_t = \sum_{d=1}^5 \beta_d D_{d,t} + u_t\), where \(D_{d,t}\) marks weekday \(d\) and \(\beta_d\) is its mean return; the Wald test of \(\beta_1 = \dots = \beta_5\) (\(\chi^2_4\)) uses HAC errors (Newey & West, 1987) with 5 lags, because \(u_t\) is heteroskedastic and autocorrelated.

                    In brief

                    • The question: which patterns survive the correction for multiple testing, and what explains them?
                    • An anomaly is a return pattern the asset-pricing model does not explain.
                    • Three possible explanations.
                      • An omitted risk factor.
                      • Mispricing (a behavioural bias).
                      • Data snooping.
                    • Families:
                      • calendar (section 5): TOM Lakonishok & Smidt (1988), first half of the month Ariel (1987).
                      • time-series (time-series momentum).
                      • cross-sectional (size, value, momentum: Chapter 3).
                    • Returns on published anomalies fall McLean & Pontiff (2016):
                      • by 26% after the end of the sample studied.
                      • by 58% after publication.
                    • Day of week: \(r_t = \sum_{d=1}^{5} \beta_d D_{d,t} + u_t\).
                      • \(D_{d,t} = 1\) if day \(t\) is weekday \(d\) (Monday, …, Friday), else 0.
                      • \(\beta_d\) is the mean return on weekday \(d\).
                    • The Wald test of \(\beta_1 = \dots = \beta_5\) (\(\chi^2_4\)) uses HAC errors Newey & West (1987) with 5 lags.
                      • because \(u_t\) is heteroskedastic and autocorrelated.
                    ↩ Back

                    Day-of-the-Week Effect: S&P 500 and BET

                    Slide 129 in the PDF

                    The chart shows the mean daily log return by weekday over 2000–2026, in basis points (bp, 1 bp = 0.01%), with HAC 95% intervals. For the S&P 500 there is no Monday effect since 2000, unlike in French (1980): the means range from 0.0 bp on Friday to 5.3 bp on Tuesday, and the test of equal means has \(p = 0.83\). For the BET, Thursday has 11.2 bp (\(t = 3.09\)), yet the joint test gives \(p = 0.31\). The two results answer different nulls: that a day's mean is zero, which mixes the day effect with the positive average return, and that all five means are equal, which is the day effect itself.

                    In brief

                    • Mean daily log return by weekday, in basis points (bp), 2000–2026, HAC 95% intervals
                      • 1 bp = 0.01%
                    • S&P 500: we find no Monday effect since 2000, unlike French (1980)
                      • from \(0.0\) bp (Friday) to \(5.3\) bp (Tuesday)
                      • equal-means \(p = 0.83\)
                    • BET: Thursday \(11.2\) bp (\(t = 3.09\)), yet the joint test gives \(p = 0.31\)
                    • Two different nulls:
                      • a day's mean equal to zero (mixes the day effect with the positive average return)
                      • all five means equal (the day effect itself)

                    Time-Series Momentum on Indices (1/3)

                    Slide 130 in the PDF

                    Time-series momentum (TSMOM) (Moskowitz, Ooi & Pedersen, 2012) goes long an index when the sum of its past 12 monthly log returns is a gain and short otherwise: the signal is \(s_t = \text{sign}(\sum_{j=1}^{12} r_{t-j})\) and the strategy return is \(s_t R_t\), with \(R_t\) the index return of month \(t\). On the next chart, each bar is the HAC \(t\) statistic (6 lags) of the strategy's mean monthly return in one market.

                    In brief

                    • Time-series momentum (TSMOM) Moskowitz, Ooi & Pedersen (2012): signal \(s_t = \text{sign}\big(\sum_{j=1}^{12} r_{t-j}\big)\)
                      • \(+1\) (long) if the past 12 monthly log returns sum to a gain, \(-1\) (short) otherwise
                      • The strategy return is \(s_t R_t\), \(R_t\) the index return of month \(t\)
                    • On the next chart, each bar is the HAC \(t\) (6 lags) of the strategy's mean monthly return in one market.

                    Time-Series Momentum on Indices (3/3)

                    Slide 132 in the PDF

                    Taken alone, the strategy has a mean return significant at 5% for the BET (1.40% a month, \(t = 2.68\)) and the S&P 500 (\(t = 2.22\)), while Bitcoin has \(t = 1.94\). With 15 markets tested, the first Holm threshold is \(0.05/15 = 0.0033\), and the smallest \(p\)-value, that of the BET, is 0.007, so no market remains significant after the correction. The annualised TSMOM Sharpe ratio, computed on price indices without dividends or a risk-free rate, is below buy-and-hold: 0.65 against 0.75 for the BET and 0.48 against 0.52 for the S&P 500. Part of the TSMOM return may be the equity premium earned in the months spent long. The result supports a comparison of procedures.

                    In brief

                    • Taken alone, the strategy has a mean return significant at 5%:
                      • for the BET (\(1.40\%\) a month, \(t = 2.68\)).
                      • the S&P 500 (\(t = 2.22\)).
                    • For Bitcoin, \(t = 1.94\), below \(1.96\).
                    • But we tested 15 markets.
                      • the first Holm threshold is \(0.05/15 = 0.0033\).
                      • the smallest p-value, the BET's, is \(0.007\).
                    • After the multiple-testing correction, no market remains significant.
                    • The TSMOM Sharpe ratio is below buy-and-hold: \(0.65\) vs \(0.75\) for the BET, \(0.48\) vs \(0.52\) for the S&P 500.
                    • What the result supports: comparison of procedures.
                    • Part of the TSMOM return may be the equity premium, earned in the months spent long.
                      • the mean stock return over the risk-free rate.
                    • The Sharpe ratio is annualised (full table: Appendix).
                      • \(\sqrt{12}\times\) monthly mean over monthly SD.
                      • on price indices without dividends or a risk-free rate.

                    Behavioural concepts

                    Slide 133 in the PDF

                    The behavioural explanations of anomalies rest on a few concepts:

                    • In prospect theory gains and losses are judged relative to a reference point, losses weigh more than equal gains (loss aversion) and small probabilities are overweighted.
                    • The disposition effect is the tendency to sell winners and keep losers.
                    • Anchoring is excess weight on an initial number, such as the purchase price.
                    • Overconfidence overstates the precision of one's beliefs and leads to too much trading.
                    • Limited attention leaves relevant information unprocessed.
                    • Herding follows others' actions without an independent assessment.
                    • Underreaction and overreaction describe a price that adjusts too little or too much to news at first, the second followed by reversal.

                    In brief

                    • Prospect theory: gains and losses are judged relative to a reference point.
                      • losses weigh more than equal gains (loss aversion).
                      • small probabilities are overweighted.
                    • Disposition effect: the tendency to sell winners and keep losers.
                    • Anchoring: excess weight on an initial number, such as the purchase price.
                    • Overconfidence: overstating the precision of one's beliefs, which leads to too much trading.
                    • Limited attention: relevant information left unprocessed.
                    • Herding: following others' actions without an independent assessment.
                    • Underreaction and overreaction: a price adjusts too little or too much to news at first.
                      • the second is followed by reversal.
                    ↩ Back

                    Psychology in Prices, from the Floor to the Feed

                    Slide 134 in the PDF

                    Shiller (1981) documents excess volatility: prices move far more than the dividends that should justify them. De Bondt & Thaler (1985) use overreaction to explain return reversals over 3–5 years, and prospect theory (Kahneman & Tversky, 1979) explains the disposition effect. In an illiquid market such as the Bucharest Stock Exchange, herding produces log-periodic bubbles, with prices rising super-exponentially and ever faster oscillations before the crash (Pele, Mazurencu-Marinescu & Nijkamp, 2013); today its channels are order books, algorithms and social media (Chapter 10). Biases alone do not break efficiency, because rational arbitrageurs could trade against them; the next frame asks whether they can.

                    In brief

                    New York Stock Exchange trading floor, 1963 Photo: Thomas J. O'Halloran (1963); public domain; Wikimedia Commons
                    • Excess volatility Shiller (1981): prices move far more than the dividends that should justify them
                    • Overreaction: De Bondt & Thaler (1985) use it to explain 3–5-year return reversals
                      • portraits: Appendix
                    • Prospect theory Kahneman & Tversky (1979) explains the disposition effect
                    • Herding in an illiquid market (BVB): herding produces log-periodic bubbles Pele, Mazurencu-Marinescu & Nijkamp (2013)
                      • log-periodic bubble: prices rising super-exponentially, with ever faster oscillations before the crash
                      • today its channels are order books, algorithms and social media (Chapter 10)
                    • Biases alone do not break efficiency.
                      • rational arbitrageurs could trade against them.
                      • Can they?
                    ↩ Back

                    Limits to Arbitrage

                    Slide 135 in the PDF

                    Real arbitrage is risky and needs capital (Shleifer & Vishny, 1997). Noise-trader risk means that mispricing can get worse before it corrects (De Long, Shleifer, Summers & Waldmann, 1990), where noise traders trade on signals without information content, such as sentiment or rumours. Funding constraints force managers who lose clients after losses to close positions at the worst time, and short-selling limits, margins, trading costs and the lack of perfect substitutes add costs. Anomalies can therefore persist where arbitrage is hard: in the small, illiquid Romanian market the BET shows the strongest persistence among the stock indices. The AMH reconciles both views, with rational and biased behaviour coexisting and evolving.

                    In brief

                    Andrei Shleifer, Harvard Photo: Manuel Castells (2018); CC BY-SA 2.0; Wikimedia Commons
                    • Shleifer & Vishny (1997): real arbitrage is risky and needs capital
                    • Noise-trader risk De Long, Shleifer, Summers & Waldmann (1990): mispricing can get worse before it corrects
                      • Noise traders: investors who trade on signals without information content (sentiment, rumours)
                    • Funding constraints: managers lose clients after losses and must close positions at the worst time
                    • Costs: short-selling limits, margins, trading costs, no perfect substitutes
                    • Anomalies can persist where arbitrage is hard
                      • in small, illiquid markets such as the Romanian one (BET) we find the strongest persistence among the stock indices
                    • The AMH reconciles both views: rational and biased behaviour coexist and evolve
                    ↩ Back

                    Can Machine Learning Beat Efficient Markets?

                    Slide 136 in the PDF

                    Machine learning (ML) learns a prediction rule from data (Chapter 13) and can capture nonlinear patterns that autocorrelation tests miss. In the US cross-section, Gu, Kelly & Xiu (2020) find that trees and neural networks give large economic gains over linear regressions, although their monthly out-of-sample \(R^2\) is below 1%. Large language models read news and filings, and Tak & Pele (2025) forecast stock trends with LLaMA.

                    In brief

                    • ML (machine learning) learns a prediction rule from data (Chapter 13)
                      • captures nonlinear patterns that autocorrelation tests miss
                    • Gu, Kelly & Xiu (2020): trees and neural networks give large economic gains over linear regressions
                      • in the US cross-section
                      • yet their monthly out-of-sample \(R^2\) is below \(1\%\)
                    • Large language models read news and filings: Tak & Pele (2025) forecast stock trends with LLaMA
                    ↩ Back

                    Validation, leakage and shrinkage

                    Slide 137 in the PDF

                    Flexible models such as trees and neural networks can also fit accidental sample patterns (overfitting). Walk-forward validation estimates the model only on data before the test period, and leakage occurs when a forecast uses information unavailable at its date, such as an accounting figure revised later. Ridge regression,

                    \[\hat g = \arg\min_g \sum_t (r_t - x_t^\prime g)^2 + \lambda\sum_j g_j^2 = (X^\prime X + \lambda I)^{-1}X^\prime r,\]
                    (2.24)

                    pulls the coefficients towards zero for \(\lambda > 0\) (shrinkage) and reduces estimation variance. With \(N(0, \sigma^2)\) errors and a prior \(g \sim N(0, \tau^2 I)\), ridge with \(\lambda = \sigma^2/\tau^2\) is the posterior mean of the coefficients, a form of Bayesian learning. Cross-validation chooses \(\lambda\) by leaving out one block of data at a time, such as a year, estimating on the rest and picking the \(\lambda\) with the smallest error on the blocks left out.

                    In brief

                    • Flexible models, such as trees and neural networks, can also fit accidental sample patterns (overfitting).
                    • Walk-forward validation: the model is estimated only on data before the test period.
                    • Leakage: a forecast uses information unavailable at its date.
                      • such as an accounting figure revised later.
                    • Ridge: \(\hat g = \arg\min_g \sum_t (r_t - x_t^\prime g)^2 + \lambda \sum_j g_j^2 = (X^\prime X + \lambda I)^{-1} X^\prime r\).
                      • \(\lambda > 0\) pulls the coefficients towards zero (shrinkage).
                      • reduces estimation variance.
                    • With \(N(0, \sigma^2)\) errors and a prior \(g \sim N(0, \tau^2 I)\), ridge with \(\lambda = \sigma^2/\tau^2\) is the posterior mean of the coefficients.
                      • Bayesian learning.
                    • Cross-validation: leave out one block of data at a time (e.g. a year).
                      • estimate on the rest.
                      • pick the \(\lambda\) with the smallest error on the blocks left out.
                    ↩ Back

                    Case Study: Rational Learning with Many Predictors (1/2)

                    Slide 138 in the PDF

                    In the model of Martin & Nagel (2022), risk-neutral investors price \(N\) assets whose cash flows depend on \(J\) characteristics with unknown coefficients, which they learn optimally (by Bayes' rule) from past data; the posterior mean is the ridge estimator (2.24), and risk neutrality rules out a risk premium. In sample, the test of no predictability rejects with probability tending to 1 when \(J/N\) tends to a constant \(\psi > 0\) (their Proposition 4). The price embeds the shrunk coefficients and so underreacts to the characteristics, which therefore predict returns ex post even though investors are rational. A rejection is not evidence of irrationality: the rational-expectations null is the wrong benchmark when investors must learn the parameters.

                    In brief

                    • Martin & Nagel (2022): investors price \(N\) assets whose cash flows depend on \(J\) characteristics with unknown coefficients
                      • learn them optimally (Bayes) from past data
                      • research question: Appendix
                      • The posterior mean of the coefficients is the ridge estimator of the previous slide.
                      • Investors are risk-neutral, so predictability cannot be a risk premium.
                    • In sample: the test of no predictability rejects with probability tending to 1 (Martin & Nagel (2022), Prop. 4).
                      • if the number of predictors per asset, \(J/N\), tends to a constant \(\psi > 0\).
                    • The mechanism: the price embeds the shrunk coefficients, so it underreacts to \(X\).
                      • ex post, \(X\) predicts returns even though investors are rational.
                    • A rejection is not evidence of irrationality (formal results: Appendix).
                      • the rational-expectations null is the wrong benchmark when investors must learn the parameters.
                    ↩ Back

                    Case Study: Rational Learning with Many Predictors (2/2)

                    Slide 139 in the PDF

                    Out of sample the picture changes. A portfolio built with coefficients estimated in other periods has mean return \(\mathbb{E}[r_{\text{OOS}}] = 0\) (Proposition 6 of Martin & Nagel (2022)), so out-of-sample tests keep their economic meaning: in this model they test efficiency. For US stocks the in-sample \(R^2\) is about 1%, and the out-of-sample return is much lower than the in-sample one, within two standard errors of zero after 2014.

                    In brief

                    • \(r_{\text{OOS}}\): the return of a portfolio built from forecasts.
                      • in a period not used to estimate its coefficients.
                    • Out of sample: mean return zero (Martin & Nagel (2022), Prop. 6).
                      • For a portfolio built with coefficients from other periods.
                      • \(\E[r_{\text{OOS}}] = 0\).
                      • model and empirical design: Appendix.
                    • So out-of-sample tests keep their economic meaning: in this model, they test efficiency.
                    • For US stocks, the in-sample \(R^2\) is about 1%.
                    • The out-of-sample return \(r_{\text{OOS}}\) is much lower than the in-sample one (Martin & Nagel (2022), Fig. 8).
                      • after 2014, within two standard errors of zero.

                    Replication on Course Data: In Sample vs Out of Sample (2/2)

                    Slide 141 in the PDF

                    The data are 40–49 industry portfolios from the Kenneth French Data Library, July 1926 – August 2026, with 238 predictors (lagged returns and their squares, lags 2–120) and rolling 20-year OLS, as in Section 7 of Martin & Nagel (2022). Each month every industry receives a weight proportional to its forecast return. \(r_{\text{IS}}\), with coefficients from the same window, lies between 5.90 and 15.14 and is positive by construction. \(r_{\text{OOS}}\), with coefficients from the previous window, has a mean of \(1.05 \times 10^{-5}\) over 842 months from July 1956, about 11% of \(r_{\text{IS}}\); it is 4.16 standard errors above zero in the first 30 out-of-sample years, to June 1986, and only 0.62 afterwards. With \(J/N \approx 5\) the in-sample fit is inflated, an \(R^2\) of 5.4% against about 1% in the paper. The result supports a comparison of procedures.

                    In brief

                    • Data: 40–49 industry portfolios (Kenneth French Data Library), July 1926 – August 2026.
                      • 49 in recent years.
                      • 238 predictors (lagged returns and their squares, lags 2–120).

                    • The portfolio: each month, every industry gets a weight proportional to its forecast return.
                    • Vertical axis: the portfolio's monthly return, in units of \(10^{-5}\).
                      • one series is \(r_{\text{IS}}\) (coefficients from the same window).
                      • the other the 10-year moving average of \(r_{\text{OOS}}\) (coefficients from the previous window), with a descriptive \(\pm 2\) standard-error band.
                    • \(r_{\text{IS}}\) is between \(5.90\) and \(15.14\), positive by construction.

                    • With \(J/N \approx 5\), the in-sample fit is inflated: an \(R^2\) of \(5.4\%\) against about 1% in Martin & Nagel (2022) (Appendix).

                    Replication on Course Data: Ridge Coefficients by Lag (2/2)

                    Slide 143 in the PDF

                    The ridge regression covers January 1971 – August 2026, with the penalty \(\lambda\) chosen by leave-one-year-out cross-validation over 56 years. For lags 2–11, 8 of 10 coefficients are positive, matching momentum (Jegadeesh & Titman, 1993); for multiples of 12, 9 of 10 are positive, matching seasonality (Heston & Sadka, 2008); for the other lags above 12, 67% are negative, matching long-run reversal (De Bondt & Thaler, 1985), while the coefficients on squared returns have no dominant sign. The \(R^2\) on the years left out, \(1 - \sum(r - \hat r)^2/\sum r^2\), is 0.013% for ridge and \(-2.26\%\) for OLS. Ridge recovers the known anomalies, yet they predict almost nothing in the years left out.

                    In brief

                    • Full-sample ridge, January 1971 – August 2026.
                      • the penalty \(\lambda\) is chosen by leave-one-year-out cross-validation (56 years).
                    • The previous chart: the ridge coefficient of each standardised predictor, by lag in months.
                      • compare Martin & Nagel (2022), Fig. 7.
                    • For lags 2–11, 8 of 10 coefficients are positive, matching momentum Jegadeesh & Titman (1993).
                    • For multiples of 12, 9 of 10 coefficients are positive, matching seasonality Heston & Sadka (2008).
                    • For the other lags above 12, \(67\%\) of the coefficients are negative, matching long-run reversal De Bondt & Thaler (1985).
                      • the coefficients on squared returns have no dominant sign.
                    • \(R^2\) on the years left out, \(1 - \sum (r - \hat r)^2 / \sum r^2\): ridge \(0.013\%\), OLS \(-2.26\%\).
                    • Ridge recovers the known anomalies (limits and extensions: Appendix).
                      • yet they predict almost nothing in the years left out.

                    ETFs, placebo and price discovery

                    Slide 144 in the PDF

                    Three notions are needed for the spot Bitcoin ETF case. A spot exchange-traded fund (ETF) holds the underlying asset, here Bitcoin, not futures contracts like the Bitcoin ETFs launched in 2021, so arbitrage between the fund and the spot market becomes direct. A placebo repeats the analysis at a date without the event: a similar apparent change in 2019 would weaken a claim that the 2024 launch caused the change. Price discovery is the incorporation of information into prices; if exchange A reacts to news first and B follows, A may contribute more to price discovery.

                    In brief

                    ETF: Exchange-Traded Fund.
                    • A spot ETF holds the underlying asset (here Bitcoin).
                      • not futures contracts like the Bitcoin ETFs launched in 2021.
                      • arbitrage between the fund and the spot market becomes direct.
                    • A placebo repeats the analysis at a date without the event.
                      • a similar apparent change in 2019 weakens a claim that the 2024 launch caused the change.
                    • Price discovery is the incorporation of information into prices.
                      • if exchange A reacts to news first and B follows, A may contribute more to price discovery.
                    ↩ Back

                    Estimation and Power (1/3)

                    Slide 145 in the PDF

                    The test compares two non-overlapping windows of 982 days, before and after 11 January 2024, through \(z = \Delta\hat\rho_1/\sqrt{s_{\text{pre}}^2 + s_{\text{post}}^2}\), where \(\Delta\hat\rho_1 = \hat\rho_{1,\text{post}} - \hat\rho_{1,\text{pre}}\) and \(s\) are the HAC standard errors, valid also when \(\rho_1 \neq 0\). The minimum detectable effect (MDE) is the smallest true change detected with 80% power at the 5% level:

                    \[\text{MDE} = (z_{0.975} + z_{0.80})\,\operatorname{SE}(\Delta\hat\rho_1) \approx 2.8\,\operatorname{SE}(\Delta\hat\rho_1),\]
                    (2.25)

                    with \(z_p\) the \(p\)-quantile of the standard Normal distribution, \(z_{0.975} = 1.96\) and \(z_{0.80} = 0.84\). Placebo dates are 11 January of each year 2016–2021, with windows of 982 returns.

                    In brief

                    • The test: two non-overlapping windows of 982 days, before and after 11 January 2024.
                    • \(z = \Delta\hat\rho_1 / \sqrt{s_{\text{pre}}^2 + s_{\text{post}}^2}\), with \(\Delta\hat\rho_1 = \hat\rho_{1,\text{post}} - \hat\rho_{1,\text{pre}}\) and the HAC standard errors \(s\) of the two estimates (valid also when \(\rho_1 \neq 0\)).
                    • Minimum detectable effect (MDE): the smallest true change detected with 80% power at the 5% level.
                    • \(\text{MDE} = (z_{0.975} + z_{0.80}) \times \operatorname{SE}(\Delta\hat\rho_1) \approx 2.8 \times \operatorname{SE}(\Delta\hat\rho_1)\)
                      • \(z_p\): the \(p\)-quantile of the standard Normal distribution; \(z_{0.975} = 1.96\) and \(z_{0.80} = 0.84\)
                    • Placebo dates: 11 January of each year 2016–2021, with windows of 982 returns.
                    ↩ Back

                    Estimation and Power (2/3)

                    Slide 147 in the PDF

                    Before the launch (May 2021 – January 2024), \(\hat\rho_1 = -0.036\) (\(s = 0.042\)) and VR(5) \(= 1.000\) (\(z^* = 0.00\)); after it (January 2024 – September 2026), \(\hat\rho_1 = -0.050\) (\(s = 0.044\)) and VR(5) \(= 0.939\) (\(z^* = -0.64\)). The difference \(\Delta\hat\rho_1 = -0.014\) gives \(z = -0.23\), so we do not reject an unchanged \(\rho_1\); the HAC standard errors require a finite fourth moment, which Chapter 1 questions. None of the six placebo dates gives a significant change at 5%, the largest being \(|z| = 1.85\). The result is a description: a before/after comparison without a control group.

                    In brief

                    • The previous chart, left: \(\hat\rho_1\) before and after the launch, with HAC 95% intervals.
                      • the third point is the difference, with the \(\pm\)MDE bar.
                    • Right: the \(z\) statistic for the ETF date, among those of the six placebo dates.
                    • Before (May 2021 – Jan 2024): \(\hat\rho_1 = -0.036\) (\(s = 0.042\)), VR(5) \(= 1.000\) (\(z^* = 0.00\)).
                    • After (Jan 2024 – Sep 2026): \(\hat\rho_1 = -0.050\) (\(s = 0.044\)), VR(5) \(= 0.939\) (\(z^* = -0.64\)).
                    • \(\Delta\hat\rho_1 = -0.014\), \(z = -0.23\): an unchanged \(\rho_1\) is not rejected.
                      • The HAC standard errors \(s\) require a finite fourth moment, which Chapter 1 questions.
                    • None of the six placebo dates gives a significant change in \(\rho_1\) at 5%.
                      • the largest statistic is \(|z| = 1.85\).
                    • What the result supports: description.
                      • A before/after comparison without a control group (difference-in-differences).
                    ↩ Back

                    Estimation and Power (3/3)

                    Slide 148 in the PDF

                    Here \(\operatorname{SE}(\Delta\hat\rho_1) = \sqrt{0.042^2 + 0.044^2} \approx 0.060\), so by (2.25) the MDE is about 0.17, roughly 12 times the estimated change: "not significant" is not "no effect". Before 2024, \(\hat\rho_1 = -0.036\), so even a full move to \(\rho_1 = 0\) would be a change almost five times smaller than the MDE, which this design would detect with probability well below 80%. A better outcome variable would be intraday price discovery across venues rather than the daily \(\rho_1\).

                    In brief

                    • Here \(\operatorname{SE}(\Delta\hat\rho_1) = \sqrt{0.042^2 + 0.044^2} \approx 0.060\), so the MDE is about \(0.17\).
                    • “Not significant” is not “no effect”: the MDE is about 12 times the estimated change.
                    • Before 2024, \(\hat\rho_1 = -0.036\).
                      • even a full move to \(\rho_1 = 0\) would be a change almost five times smaller than the MDE.
                      • this design would detect it with probability well below 80%.
                    • A better outcome variable: intraday price discovery across venues, not daily \(\rho_1\).

                    Difference-in-differences: example and assumptions

                    Slide 149 in the PDF

                    The before/after test cannot separate the effect of the ETFs from other changes in 2024; a causal conclusion needs a control group, for example Ethereum before its own ETFs of July 2024. Difference-in-differences (DiD) subtracts the control group's before–after change from that of the treated group. It rests on:

                    • Parallel trends, that without treatment both groups would have changed in the same way, which similar pre-treatment trends support but cannot prove;
                    • No anticipation, that the treated group does not react before treatment;
                    • No spillover, that treatment does not affect the control group, so a Bitcoin ETF must not change Ethereum trading.

                    In brief

                    • The before/after test cannot separate the effect of the ETFs from other changes in 2024.
                    • A causal conclusion needs a control group, for example Ethereum before its own ETFs (July 2024).
                    • Difference-in-differences (DiD) compares two before–after changes.
                      • It subtracts the control group's change from the treated group's change.
                    • Parallel trends:
                      • without treatment, the treated and control groups would have changed in the same way.
                    • Similar pre-treatment trends support this assumption but cannot prove it.
                    • No anticipation: the treated group does not react before treatment.
                    • No spillover: treatment does not affect the control group.
                      • for example, a Bitcoin ETF must not change Ethereum trading.
                    ↩ Back

                    Appendix: Pioneers, Bachelier and Samuelson

                    Slide 150 in the PDF

                    Bachelier (1900) modelled the prices of French government bonds as a random walk and stated that "the mathematical expectation of the speculator is zero". Samuelson (1965) gave the economic argument: if prices are properly anticipated, their changes are unpredictable. Both results are the ancestors of the Efficient Market Hypothesis (EMH) studied in the chapter.

                    In brief

                    Louis Bachelier (1870–1946) Photo: unknown author; public domain; Wikimedia Commons
                    Paul A. Samuelson (1915–2009) Photo: Bernard Gotfryd (1970–1975); public domain; Wikimedia Commons
                    • Bachelier (1900): prices of French government bonds move like a random walk
                      • “The mathematical expectation of the speculator is zero”
                    • Samuelson (1965): if prices are properly anticipated, their changes are unpredictable
                    • Both results are the ancestors of the Efficient Market Hypothesis
                    ↩ Back

                    Appendix: Eugene Fama

                    Slide 151 in the PDF

                    Fama (1970) gave the definition of efficiency used in this chapter and the three forms of efficiency, weak, semi-strong and strong. Fama (1991) updated and surveyed the evidence, and Malkiel (2003) gave an accessible defence of the EMH against its critics. Fama received the Nobel Prize in 2013, shared with Lars Peter Hansen and with Robert Shiller, a sceptic of efficiency.

                    In brief

                    Eugene F. Fama, Nobel Prize 2013 Photo: Bengt Nyman (2013); CC BY 2.0; Wikimedia Commons
                    • Fama (1970) gave the definition used in this chapter and the three forms of efficiency
                    • Nobel Prize 2013, shared with Robert Shiller (a sceptic of efficiency) and Lars Peter Hansen
                    • Update and survey: Fama (1991)
                      • an accessible defence of the EMH against its critics: Malkiel (2003)

                    Appendix: Runs Test on All Markets, Other VR Tests

                    Slide 153 in the PDF

                    The runs statistics of the previous table add information that the autocorrelation does not always carry. Nifty 50, with \(z = -5.46\), and IPC, with \(z = -3.27\), show persistence in emerging markets, while Ethereum has \(z = 3.91\), significant although its \(\hat\rho_1\) is not. Two extensions of the variance-ratio (VR) test complete the toolkit: the automatic VR test of Choi (1999), which chooses the horizon from the data through a spectral estimate at frequency zero, and the wild-bootstrap versions for small samples of Kim (2006), applied to Asian markets by Kim & Shamsuddin (2008).

                    In brief

                    • Runs \(z\) (table on the previous slide):
                      • Nifty 50 \(-5.46\), IPC \(-3.27\) (persistence in emerging markets)
                      • Ethereum \(3.91\), significant although \(\hat\rho_1\) is not
                    • Choi (1999): an automatic VR test that chooses the horizon from the data
                      • spectral estimate at frequency zero
                    • Wild-bootstrap versions for small samples: Kim (2006)
                      • applications to Asian markets: Kim & Shamsuddin (2008)

                    Appendix: Automatic Portmanteau Test of the MDH

                    Slide 154 in the PDF

                    Escanciano & Lobato (2009) propose a portmanteau test that is valid under the martingale difference hypothesis (MDH) with conditional heteroskedasticity and that chooses the number of lags from the data. With \(e_t = r_t - \bar r\), the autocovariance at lag \(j\) is \(\hat\gamma_j = \tfrac{1}{T-j}\sum_t e_t e_{t-j}\) and its robust variance \(\hat\kappa_j = \tfrac{1}{T-j}\sum_t e_t^2 e_{t-j}^2\); the statistic is

                    \[AQ = T\sum_{j \le \tilde p} \tilde\rho_j^2, \qquad \tilde\rho_j = \frac{\hat\gamma_j}{\sqrt{\hat\kappa_j}},\]
                    (2.26)

                    where \(\tilde p \le 10\) maximises \(T\sum_{j \le p} \tilde\rho_j^2\) minus a penalty, the BIC penalty \(p \ln T\), replaced by the AIC penalty \(2p\) when \(\max_j \sqrt T|\tilde\rho_j|\) exceeds \(\sqrt{2.4 \ln T}\). Under the MDH, \(\tilde p \to 1\) with probability 1, so (2.26) has a \(\chi^2_1\) limit and a large \(AQ\) rejects. On the course data \(AQ\) is 13.23 for the S&P 500 (\(p = 0.0003\)), 14.92 for BET (\(p = 0.0001\)) and 0.75 for Bitcoin (\(p = 0.385\)); the MDH is rejected at 5% for 5 of the 16 series.

                    In brief

                    • What we derive: a portmanteau test valid under the MDH with conditional heteroskedasticity Escanciano & Lobato (2009).
                      • the number of lags chosen from the data.
                    • \(AQ = T\sum_{j \le \tilde p} \tilde\rho_j^2\), with \(\tilde\rho_j = \hat\gamma_j/\sqrt{\hat\kappa_j}\), \(\hat\gamma_j = \tfrac{1}{T-j}\sum_t e_t e_{t-j}\) and \(\hat\kappa_j = \tfrac{1}{T-j}\sum_t e_t^2 e_{t-j}^2\)
                      • \(e_t = r_t - \bar r\); \(\hat\gamma_j\): the autocovariance at lag \(j\); \(\hat\kappa_j\): its robust variance
                      • \(\tilde p \le 10\) maximises \(T\sum_{j \le p} \tilde\rho_j^2\) minus a penalty: BIC (\(p \ln T\)), but AIC (\(2p\)) when \(\max_j \sqrt T|\tilde\rho_j|\) exceeds \(\sqrt{2.4 \ln T}\)
                    • Under the MDH, \(\tilde p \to 1\) with probability 1, hence the \(\chi^2_1\) limit.
                      • a large \(AQ\) rejects the MDH.
                    • \(AQ\): S&P 500 \(13.23\) (\(p = 0.0003\)), BET \(14.92\) (\(p = 0.0001\)), Bitcoin \(0.75\) (\(p = 0.385\)).
                      • the MDH is rejected at 5% for 5 of the 16 series.
                    ↩ Back

                    Appendix: Predictive Regression, Data and Full Output

                    Slide 155 in the PDF

                    The predictive regression of the chapter regresses the monthly log excess return of the S&P 500, dividends included and net of the 3-month Treasury bill (T-bill) rate, on the lagged log dividend–price ratio \(dp_t\). The data are the S&P Composite series of Shiller (2026), to June 2026, extended with monthly averages of the course's daily S&P 500 closes and the dividends of the SPY ETF, and the 3-month T-bill yield from FRED (Federal Reserve Economic Data, series TB3MS), converted to a monthly log rate \(\ln(1 + \text{TB3MS}/1200)\) and lagged one month. The ordinary least squares (OLS) slope is \(\hat\beta = 0.267\) with a Newey–West SE of 0.293 (12 lags), so \(t = 0.91\) (OLS \(t = 1.16\)) and \(R^2 = 0.12\%\); the bias-corrected slope divided by the unadjusted HAC SE gives \(-0.22\), an illustrative figure only, since it ignores the uncertainty of the correction. The economic value of Campbell & Thompson (2008) is the proportional rise in the expected return of a market timer, \(\tfrac{R^2}{1-R^2}\cdot\tfrac{1+S^2}{S^2}\) with \(S\) the monthly Sharpe ratio. The out-of-sample comparison with the historical mean gives \(t = -0.57\) in the test of Diebold & Mariano (1995), which favours the small model when the models are nested; the correction of Clark & West (2007) gives \(t = 0.42\).

                    In brief

                    FRED: Federal Reserve Economic Data.
                    • Monthly log excess return regressed on the lagged \(dp_t\)
                      • dividends included, minus the 3-month T-bill rate
                      • Data: Shiller (2026) (S&P Composite, to June 2026), extended with monthly averages of our daily S&P 500 closes (scaled over the last 12 common months) and the dividends of the SPDR S&P 500 ETF (SPY); 3-month US Treasury bill yield (FRED, series TB3MS; annual %), monthly log rate \(\ln(1 + \text{TB3MS}/1200)\) lagged one month
                    • OLS: \(\hat\beta = 0.267\), Newey–West SE \(0.293\) (12 lags), \(t = 0.91\), OLS \(t = 1.16\), \(R^2 = 0.12\%\)
                    • Corrected slope / unadjusted HAC SE \(= -0.22\): only illustrative
                      • it ignores the uncertainty of the correction
                    • Economic value Campbell & Thompson (2008): proportional rise in the expected return of the market timer
                      • number: slide The Dividend–Price Ratio: Results for the S&P 500
                      • \(\dfrac{R^2}{1 - R^2}\cdot\dfrac{1 + S^2}{S^2}\) , \(S\) the monthly Sharpe ratio
                    • The Diebold & Mariano (1995) test gives \(t = -0.57\)
                      • but it favours the small model when the models are nested
                      • Clark & West (2007) correct for this and give \(t = 0.42\)

                    Appendix: Harold Hurst and the Nile

                    Slide 157 in the PDF

                    Hurst (1951) designed reservoirs on the Nile and found that the range of cumulative Nile flows grows faster than \(\sqrt n\) with the horizon \(n\): years of high floods tend to follow other years of high floods. This observation is the origin of the rescaled range (R/S) statistic used in the chapter to measure long memory.

                    In brief

                    Harold Edwin Hurst (1880–1978) Photo: Elliott & Fry (1953); public domain; Wikimedia Commons
                    Nilometer on Elephantine Island, Aswan Photo: Olaf Tausch (2011); CC BY 3.0; Wikimedia Commons
                    • Hurst (1951) designed Nile reservoirs
                    • He found that the range of cumulative Nile flows grows faster than \(\sqrt n\) with the horizon
                      • years of high floods follow other years of high floods, hence the rescaled range statistic (R/S)
                    ↩ Back

                    Appendix: The Anis–Lloyd Expectation

                    Slide 158 in the PDF

                    Anis & Lloyd (1976) derived the exact expectation of the rescaled range \(R_n/s_n\) when returns are i.i.d. draws from the Normal distribution,

                    \[\mathbb{E}\!\left[\frac{R_n}{s_n}\right] = \frac{\Gamma((n-1)/2)}{\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma(n/2)} \sum_{i=1}^{n-1} \sqrt{\frac{n-i}{i}},\]
                    (2.27)

                    where \(\Gamma\) is the gamma function. The corrected Hurst exponent \(H_{AL}\) of the chapter subtracts this reference value from the observed R/S. Without the correction, the estimates overstate memory by about 0.04–0.05 with daily data.

                    In brief

                    • Anis & Lloyd (1976): exact \(\E[R_n/s_n]\) under i.i.d. returns from the Normal distribution
                      • \(\E[R_n/s_n] = \dfrac{\Gamma((n-1)/2)}{\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma(n/2)} \sum_{i=1}^{n-1} \sqrt{\dfrac{n-i}{i}}\)
                      • \(\Gamma\): the gamma function
                    • This \(\E[R_n/s_n]\) is the reference subtracted in \(H_{AL}\) (R/S Analysis slide)
                    • Uncorrected estimates overstate memory by about \(0.04\)–\(0.05\) with daily data
                    ↩ Back

                    Appendix: Long Memory with Inference, the Local Whittle Estimator

                    Slide 159 in the PDF

                    Long memory can also be measured by the fractional parameter \(d = H - 0.5\), defined by the behaviour of the spectral density near frequency zero, \(f(\lambda) \sim G\lambda^{-2d}\) as \(\lambda \to 0\) with \(G > 0\) a constant; for \(d > 0\) the density explodes at low frequencies. The local Whittle estimator fits only the first \(m\) Fourier frequencies \(\lambda_j = 2\pi j/T\), where \(m\) is the bandwidth and \(I\) the periodogram:

                    \[\hat d = \arg\min_d \left[\ln\!\left(\frac1m\sum_{j=1}^m \lambda_j^{2d} I(\lambda_j)\right) - \frac{2d}{m}\sum_{j=1}^m \ln\lambda_j\right].\]
                    (2.28)

                    Robinson (1995) shows that, for \(|d| < 1/2\), \(m \to \infty\), \(m/T \to 0\), a smooth \(f\) near 0 and a linear process with conditionally homoskedastic martingale-difference innovations, \(\sqrt m(\hat d - d) \to N(0, 1/4)\) without Gaussianity, which gives a confidence interval instead of a band under the null. With \(m = T^{0.65}\) and the 95% interval \(\hat d \pm 1.96/(2\sqrt m)\), the S&P 500 has \(\hat d = -0.013\) \([-0.069, 0.043]\), Bitcoin 0.063 \([-0.002, 0.127]\), BET 0.118 \([0.062, 0.174]\) and Ethereum 0.112 \([0.045, 0.179]\). A large \(m\) reaches frequencies where short-run autocorrelation biases \(\hat d\) upward, a concern for BET, whose \(\hat\rho_1 = 0.109\); with \(m = T^{0.5}\), BET gives \(\hat d = 0.135\) (SE 0.056), a sensitivity check that supports, without proving, that its \(d > 0\) is not only short memory. Returns with GARCH dynamics also strain the conditions of the theorem.

                    In brief

                    • \(d = H - 0.5\), with spectral density \(f(\lambda) \sim G\lambda^{-2d}\) as \(\lambda \to 0\)
                      • \(G > 0\) a constant
                      • \(d > 0\): the density explodes at low frequencies, long memory
                      • fit only the first \(m\) Fourier frequencies \(\lambda_j = 2\pi j/T\) (\(m\): bandwidth)
                      • \(\hat d = \arg\min_d \Big[\ln\Big(\tfrac1m\sum_{j=1}^m \lambda_j^{2d} I(\lambda_j)\Big) - \tfrac{2d}{m}\sum_{j=1}^m \ln\lambda_j\Big]\) , \(I\): the periodogram (sample spectral density)
                      • Robinson (1995): for \(|d| < 1/2\), \(m \to \infty\), \(m/T \to 0\), a smooth \(f\) near 0 and a linear process with conditionally homoskedastic martingale-difference innovations, \(\sqrt m(\hat d - d) \to N(0, 1/4)\) without Gaussianity: a confidence interval instead of a band
                    • Estimates with \(m = T^{0.65}\) and the 95% CI \(\hat d \pm 1.96/(2\sqrt m)\)
                      • S&P 500 \(-0.013\) \([-0.069, 0.043]\); Bitcoin \(0.063\) \([-0.002, 0.127]\)
                      • BET \(0.118\) \([0.062, 0.174]\); Ethereum \(0.112\) \([0.045, 0.179]\)
                    • Bandwidth trade-off: a large \(m\) reaches frequencies where short-run autocorrelation biases \(\hat d\) upward
                      • BET \(\hat\rho_1 = 0.109\)
                      • with \(m = T^{0.5}\) BET gives \(\hat d = 0.135\) (SE \(0.056\)): a sensitivity check supporting, not proving, that its \(d > 0\) is not only short memory
                      • GARCH returns also strain the conditions above
                    ↩ Back

                    Appendix: EMH versus AMH

                    Slide 160 in the PDF

                    The table contrasts the EMH with the Adaptive Markets Hypothesis (AMH) on five dimensions. Under the EMH investors are rational and optimising, efficiency is an equilibrium property tested as a restriction, risk premia are equilibrium premia, possibly time-varying, and anomalies are omitted risk factors or data mining. Under the AMH investors have bounded rationality and use heuristics, efficiency is a matter of degree that changes with conditions, the risk–return relation changes with the population of investors, and anomalies are transient profit opportunities; the corresponding empirical tool is a test of variation over time and states, for example in rolling windows.

                    In brief

                    EMHAMH
                    InvestorsRational, optimisingBounded rationality, heuristics
                    EfficiencyAn equilibrium property, tested as a restrictionA matter of degree that changes with conditions
                    Risk–returnEquilibrium risk premia, possibly time-varyingChanges with the population of investors
                    AnomaliesOmitted risk factors or data miningTransient profit opportunities
                    Empirical toolTests of an equilibrium restrictionTests of time and state variation (rolling windows)
                    ↩ Back

                    Appendix: Is Bitcoin Efficient? The Literature

                    Slide 161 in the PDF

                    Urquhart (2016) finds Bitcoin inefficient over 2010–2016, with some tests indicating efficiency in the later subperiod; his battery of tests, Ljung–Box, runs, the rank test of Bartels (1982), the automatic VR test, BDS and R/S, includes the BDS test, which rejects under volatility clustering alone and is therefore not a test of the random-walk hypothesis RW3. Nadarajah & Chu (2017) find Bitcoin weak-form efficient after a power transformation of returns, Kristoufek (2018) finds Bitcoin markets mostly inefficient in 2010–2017 with efficiency changing over time, and Tran & Leirvik (2020) find time-varying efficiency across many crypto-assets, increasing over time. Pele et al. (2023) show that crypto-assets have become a distinct asset class whose returns share the stylised facts of other markets. The course's own estimates, calendar year by calendar year, give Bitcoin lag-1 autocorrelations of \(-0.002\) in 2011, \(-0.071\) in 2012 and \(0.069\) in 2013, and a robust \(|z^*(5)|\) of at most 1.71 over all crypto-asset years, below the critical value 1.96. In 2011–2013 the robust standard error of \(\hat\rho_1\) is 0.09–0.11, so the yearly non-rejections have little power and do not by themselves contradict Urquhart (2016).

                    In brief

                    • Urquhart (2016): Bitcoin inefficient over 2010–2016
                      • some tests indicate efficiency in the later subperiod
                      • His battery (Ljung–Box, runs, the rank test of Bartels (1982), automatic VR, BDS, R/S) includes BDS, which rejects under volatility clustering alone: not a test of RW3
                    • Nadarajah & Chu (2017): with a power transformation of returns, Bitcoin is weak-form efficient
                    • Kristoufek (2018): Bitcoin markets mostly inefficient in 2010–2017, with efficiency changing over time
                    • Tran & Leirvik (2020): time-varying efficiency across many crypto-assets, increasing over time
                    • Pele et al. (2023): crypto-assets have become a distinct asset class
                      • their returns share the stylised facts of other markets
                    • Our own estimates, calendar year by calendar year
                      • slide Crypto Efficiency Year by Year
                      • Bitcoin, lag-1 autocorrelation \(\hat\rho_1\): \(-0.002\) in 2011, \(-0.071\) in 2012, \(0.069\) in 2013, all close to zero
                      • Robust variance-ratio statistic \(z^*(5)\): at most \(1.71\) in absolute value over all crypto-asset years, below the critical value \(1.96\)
                      • In 2011–2013 the robust standard error of \(\hat\rho_1\) is \(0.09\)–\(0.11\): the yearly non-rejection has little power and does not by itself contradict Urquhart (2016)
                    ↩ Back

                    Appendix: Romania, Frontier then Emerging

                    Slide 162 in the PDF

                    FTSE Russell (2019) reclassified Romania from Frontier to Secondary Emerging status, a decision announced in September 2019 and effective on 21 September 2020. The expected channels are more foreign investors, more analysts and more liquidity, and therefore faster price discovery on the Bucharest Stock Exchange (Bursa de Valori București, BVB). Earlier evidence on the BVB covers crashes and log-periodic bubbles (Pele & Mazurencu-Marinescu, 2012) and herding in an illiquid market (Pele, Mazurencu-Marinescu & Nijkamp, 2013). Seminar C2 compares efficiency in the five years before and after the reclassification, with WIG20 and BUX as a comparison group and a block bootstrap (Künsch, 1989); such a before/after comparison remains descriptive unless confounders are controlled.

                    In brief

                    BVB: Bursa de Valori București — the Bucharest Stock Exchange.
                    • FTSE Russell (2019): Romania reclassified from Frontier to Secondary Emerging
                      • announced in September 2019
                      • effective 21 September 2020
                    • Expected channels: more foreign investors, more analysts, more liquidity \(\Rightarrow\) faster price discovery
                    • Earlier BVB evidence:
                      • crashes and log-periodic bubbles Pele & Mazurencu-Marinescu (2012)
                      • herding in an illiquid market Pele, Mazurencu-Marinescu & Nijkamp (2013)
                    • Did efficiency improve? Seminar C2 compares five years before and after
                      • with WIG20 and BUX as a comparison group

                      • A before/after comparison is descriptive unless confounders are controlled

                    Appendix: Reality Check, SPA and StepM explained

                    Slide 163 in the PDF

                    Three procedures test many trading rules jointly while allowing for the dependence between them. The Reality Check of White (2000) asks whether the best of all the rules tried beats a benchmark, using a bootstrap distribution of their maximum performance. The test of Superior Predictive Ability (SPA) of Hansen (2005) standardises each performance by its uncertainty (studentisation), which reduces the distortion caused by poor rules. The StepM procedure of Romano & Wolf (2005) tests the strongest statistic first and then the remaining family with jointly resampled critical values, and controls the familywise error rate (FWER) under its assumptions. In the chapter's example the same months are drawn for all 15 calendar tests, so common market shocks are kept; resampling each test separately would destroy that dependence.

                    In brief

                    SPA: Superior Predictive Ability (test).
                    • Reality Check asks whether the best of all tried rules beats a benchmark. White (2000)
                      • using a bootstrap distribution of their maximum performance.
                    • SPA standardises performance by its uncertainty (studentisation) and reduces distortion from poor rules. Hansen (2005)
                    • StepM tests the strongest statistic first, then the remaining family using jointly resampled critical values
                      • it controls FWER under its assumptions. Romano & Wolf (2005)
                    • Example: the same months are drawn for all 15 calendar tests, so common market shocks are kept
                      • separate resampling would destroy that dependence.
                    ↩ Back

                    Appendix: StepM on the 15 Calendar Tests

                    Slide 164 in the PDF

                    Bonferroni and Holm ignore the correlation between tests run on the same markets and days, whereas Reality Check, SPA and StepM allow for it. The chapter applies StepM to its 15 calendar tests with a bootstrap of blocks of 3 calendar months, common to all markets, and 999 replications. The critical \(p\)-value at the first step is 0.0016, stricter than the Bonferroni threshold of 0.0033, and the procedure gives 2 rejections, the turn-of-the-month (TOM) effects of BET and BUX, as Holm does. StepM is stricter than Bonferroni here because the HAC tests over-reject: at a nominal 5%, their bootstrap rejection rates are 6.0% for the day-of-week test, 6.4% for TOM and 6.4% for January. Chordia, Goyal & Saretto (2020), from over two million strategies generated at random from real accounting data, propose thresholds of \(|t| > 3.8\) for time-series and \(|t| > 3.4\) for cross-sectional tests, without which about 45% of rejections are false; Harvey & Liu (2020) recall that lowering false discoveries raises missed ones.

                    In brief

                    • Bonferroni and Holm ignore the correlation between tests (same markets, same days)
                      • Reality Check, SPA and StepM allow for it (explained on the previous slide)
                    • Our 15 calendar tests: StepM with a bootstrap of blocks of 3 calendar months
                      • common to all markets
                      • 999 replications
                      • Critical \(p\) at the first step \(0.0016\) (Bonferroni: \(0.0033\)); 2 rejections, BET and BUX TOM, as with Holm
                      • Why stricter than Bonferroni? At nominal 5%, the bootstrap rejection rates of the HAC tests are \(6.0\%\) (day of week), \(6.4\%\) (TOM), \(6.4\%\) (January)
                    • Chordia, Goyal & Saretto (2020): thresholds \(|t| > 3.8\) (time series) and \(|t| > 3.4\) (cross-section)
                      • from over two million strategies generated at random from real accounting data
                      • without them, about 45% of rejections are false
                    • Lowering false discoveries raises missed ones Harvey & Liu (2020)
                    ↩ Back
