                    Appendix: Case Study, Research Question

                    Slide 167 in the PDF

                    Martin & Nagel (2022) ask what a rejected no-predictability test means when the number \(J\) of characteristics is comparable to the number \(N\) of assets and the coefficients must be learned from past data, a high-dimensional prediction problem like those of machine learning. Semi-strong efficiency, prices that "fully reflect" public information (Fama, 1970), then has two readings: investors know the parameters of the cash-flow model, the rational-expectations null of most efficiency tests, or investors update their beliefs optimally, in the Bayesian sense, with the data available in real time. The question matters because of the hundreds of in-sample return predictors of the "factor zoo" (Harvey, Liu & Zhu, 2016), with text, satellite images and social media increasing \(J\) further, and because it links to out-of-sample predictability (Campbell & Thompson, 2008) and to the decay of anomalies after publication (McLean & Pontiff, 2016).

                    In brief

                    • Martin & Nagel (2022): what does a rejected no-predictability test mean?
                      • when the number \(J\) of characteristics is comparable to the number \(N\) of assets
                      • the coefficients must be learned from past data: a high-dimensional prediction problem, as in machine learning
                    • Semi-strong efficiency: prices “fully reflect” public information Fama (1970), with two readings
                      • investors know the parameters of the cash-flow model (rational expectations: the null of most efficiency tests)
                      • investors update their beliefs optimally (Bayes) with the data available in real time
                    • Why it matters: hundreds of in-sample return predictors, the “factor zoo” Harvey, Liu & Zhu (2016)
                      • text, satellite images and social media keep increasing \(J\)
                      • does every significant predictor need a risk-based or a behavioural explanation?
                    • Link to this chapter: out-of-sample predictability Campbell & Thompson (2008) and the decay of anomalies after publication McLean & Pontiff (2016)
                    ↩ Back

                    Appendix: Case Study, Model and Method (1/2)

                    Slide 168 in the PDF

                    In the model of Martin & Nagel (2022) (Sec. 2), cash-flow growth is \(\Delta y_t = Xg + e_t\) with \(e_t \sim N(0, I)\) and a zero interest rate, where \(\Delta y_t\) holds the \(N\) growth rates, \(X\) is the \(N \times J\) matrix of characteristics, \(g\) the \(J\) unknown coefficients and \(I\) the identity matrix. Investors start from the prior \(g \sim N(0, (\theta/J)\,I)\), the true distribution from which \(g\) is drawn, with \(\theta\) the signal strength. Schematically, after \(t\) periods the price is \(X\hat g_t\) with \(\hat g_t = (tX^\prime X + (J/\theta)I)^{-1}X^\prime\sum_{s \le t}\Delta y_s\), so that \(\mathbb{E}[\hat g_t \mid g] = Ag\) with \(A\) "smaller" than \(I\). The next return then decomposes as

                    \[r_{t+1} = X(I - A)g - X(\hat g_t - Ag) + e_{t+1},\]
                    (2.29)

                    the sum of underreaction from shrinkage, estimation noise and an unpredictable shock. The asymptotics (Sec. 4) let \(N, J \to \infty\) with \(J/N \to \psi > 0\), and the simulations use \(N = 1000\), entries of \(X\) drawn from the standard Normal distribution and \(\theta = 1\).

                    In brief

                    • Model (Martin & Nagel (2022), Sec. 2)
                      • cash-flow growth \(\Delta y_t = Xg + e_t\), with \(e_t \sim N(0, I)\) and a zero interest rate
                      • \(\Delta y_t\): the \(N\) cash-flow growth rates; \(X\): the \(N \times J\) matrix of characteristics; \(g\): the \(J\) unknown coefficients; \(I\): identity matrix
                    • Learning (Prop. 1–2)
                      • prior \(g \sim N(0, (\theta/J)\,I)\), the true distribution from which \(g\) is drawn; \(\theta\): signal strength
                      • schematically, one period: the price is \(X\hat g_t\), with \(\hat g_t = (tX^\prime X + (J/\theta)I)^{-1}X^\prime\sum_{s \le t}\Delta y_s\), so \(\E[\hat g_t \mid g] = Ag\), with \(A\) “smaller” than \(I\)
                      • \(r_{t+1} = X(I - A)g\) (underreaction from shrinkage) \(- X(\hat g_t - Ag)\) (estimation noise) \(+ e_{t+1}\) (unpredictable shock)
                    • Asymptotics and simulations (Sec. 4)
                      • \(N, J \to \infty\) with \(J/N \to \psi > 0\)
                      • simulations with \(N = 1000\), entries of \(X\) drawn from the standard Normal distribution and \(\theta = 1\)
                    ↩ Back

                    Appendix: Case Study, Model and Method (2/2)

                    Slide 169 in the PDF

                    The in-sample test is a cross-sectional OLS of \(r_{t+1}\) on \(X\), which requires \(J < N\), with a joint \(\chi^2\) test. The out-of-sample test uses a portfolio with weights \(\frac{1}{N} X h_{s+1}\) built from coefficients \(h_{s+1}\) estimated in other periods, with return \(r_{\text{OOS},t+1} = \frac{1}{N}\, h_{s+1}^{\prime} X^{\prime} r_{t+1}\) for \(s \neq t\); \(r_{\text{IS}}\) is the same quantity with \(s = t\). The empirical design (Sec. 7) uses US stocks from CRSP (Center for Research in Security Prices), excluding those below the 20th New York Stock Exchange (NYSE) size percentile or priced below 1 US dollar. The 238 predictors are the simple and squared monthly returns at lags \(t-2\) to \(t-120\), demeaned and standardised across stocks every month. The full-sample estimate is a ridge regression over 1971 – June 2019 with the penalty chosen by leave-one-year-out cross-validation, and a rolling 20-year OLS gives \(r_{\text{OOS}}\) from January 1956.

                    In brief

                    • CRSP: Center for Research in Security Prices.
                    • NYSE: New York Stock Exchange.
                    • Tests
                      • in sample: a cross-sectional OLS of \(r_{t+1}\) on \(X\) (requires \(J < N\)) with a joint \(\chi^2\) test
                      • out of sample: a portfolio with weights \(\frac{1}{N} X h_{s+1}\), from coefficients estimated in other periods
                      • \(r_{\text{OOS},t+1} = \frac{1}{N}\, h_{s+1}^{\prime} X^{\prime} r_{t+1}\), \(s \neq t\) (eq. 19); \(r_{\text{IS}}\): the same with \(s = t\)
                      • \(h_{s+1}\): the estimated coefficient vector; \(r_{t+1}\): the \(N\) returns
                    • Empirical design (Sec. 7)
                      • US stocks in CRSP, without those below the 20th NYSE size percentile or priced below $1
                      • predictors: simple and squared monthly returns at lags \(t - 2\) to \(t - 120\) (238 predictors), demeaned and standardised across stocks every month
                      • full-sample ridge, 1971 – June 2019, penalty chosen by leave-one-year-out cross-validation
                      • rolling 20-year OLS gives \(r_{\text{OOS}}\) from January 1956
                    ↩ Back

                    Appendix: Case Study, Formal Results (1/2)

                    Slide 170 in the PDF

                    In sample, the probability that the test does not reject is of the order \(e^{-0.015N}\) for \(\psi > 0.4\), with \(\theta = 1\) and one period of learning (Prop. 4 and Fig. 2 of Martin & Nagel (2022)). In simulations with \(N = 1000\) and four periods of learning, a test of nominal size 5% rejects with probability above 90% once \(J > N/2\) (Fig. 3b). This is not a size distortion: the in-sample predictable component really is in the prices. Single-predictor tests with \(k\)-FWER control, which bound at 5% the probability of \(k\) or more false rejections, do not help either: for \(k = 20\) and \(J/N = 0.05\) the probability of at least \(k\) rejections is 25% instead of 5%, and about 80% for \(J/N\) close to 1 (Fig. 4). The problem lies in the null hypothesis, not in the test.

                    In brief

                    • In sample: the probability of no rejection is of the order \(e^{-0.015N}\) (Fig. 2)
                      • Prop. 4 of Martin & Nagel (2022), main slide
                      • for \(\psi > 0.4\) (\(\theta = 1\), one period of learning)
                    • Simulations: a test of nominal size 5% rejects with probability above 90% once \(J > N/2\) (Fig. 3b)
                      • \(N = 1000\), four periods of learning
                      • not a size distortion: the in-sample predictable component really is in the prices
                    • Single-predictor tests with \(k\)-FWER control (Fig. 4)
                      • probability of \(k\) or more false rejections at most 5%
                      • for \(k = 20\) and \(J/N = 0.05\), the probability of at least \(k\) rejections is 25% instead of 5%
                      • about 80% for \(J/N\) close to 1
                      • multiple-testing corrections do not help: the problem is the null hypothesis, not the test
                    ↩ Back

                    Appendix: Case Study, Formal Results (2/2)

                    Slide 171 in the PDF

                    Out of sample, \(\mathbb{E}[r_{\text{OOS},t+1}] = 0\) holds both forward and backward in time (Prop. 6); it is positive only if investors shrink more than the data warrant (Fig. 6). On US stocks the ridge coefficients recover three known patterns, momentum, long-run reversal and momentum seasonality (Fig. 7), with \(r_{\text{IS}} \approx 0.006\%\) per month (Fig. 8).

                    In brief

                    • Out of sample: \(\E[r_{\text{OOS},t+1}] = 0\) holds forward and backward in time (Prop. 6)
                      • positive only if investors shrink more than the data warrant (Fig. 6)
                    • US stocks: the ridge coefficients recover three patterns (Fig. 7)
                      • momentum
                      • long-run reversal
                      • momentum seasonality
                      • \(r_{\text{IS}} \approx 0.006\%\) per month (Fig. 8)

                    Appendix: Case Study, In Sample vs Out of Sample in Detail

                    Slide 172 in the PDF

                    In the course replication on industry portfolios, \(r_{\text{IS}} = b^{\prime} G^{-1} b / 240 \ge 0\) in every 20-year window by construction, where \(G = \sum_s X_s^{\prime} X_s / N_s\) and \(b = \sum_s X_s^{\prime} r_s / N_s\) over the 240 months of the window, \(X_s\) is the \(N_s \times 238\) predictor matrix of month \(s\), \(r_s\) the \(N_s\) portfolio returns and \(G^{-1}b\) the pooled OLS coefficients. The mean in-sample \(R^2\) is 5.4%, against about 1% for individual stocks in Martin & Nagel (2022): 238 predictors against only 40–49 portfolios a month, pooled over 240 months, inflate the in-sample fit. Out of sample, from July 1956, the mean \(r_{\text{OOS}}\) is \(1.98 \times 10^{-5}\) until June 1986, 4.16 standard errors above zero, and \(0.36 \times 10^{-5}\) afterwards, only 0.62 standard errors. The 10-year average is last above two standard errors in the window ending in December 1987, and in the windows ending in 2014–2019 its largest ratio to the standard error is 1.25, as for stocks. Martin & Nagel (2022) suggest that the gap is consistent with investor learning, but it does not establish learning or rule out risk premia, and early out-of-sample gains are also consistent with excess shrinkage before the computer era.

                    In brief

                    • \(r_{\text{IS}} \geq 0\) in every 20-year window by construction: \(r_{\text{IS}} = b^{\prime} G^{-1} b / 240\)
                      • \(G = \sum_s X_s^{\prime} X_s / N_s\) and \(b = \sum_s X_s^{\prime} r_s / N_s\) over the 240 months \(s\) of the window
                      • \(X_s\): the \(N_s \times 238\) predictor matrix of month \(s\); \(r_s\): the \(N_s\) portfolio returns; \(G^{-1}b\): the pooled OLS coefficients
                      • mean in-sample \(R^2\) of \(5.4\%\), against about 1% for individual stocks in Martin & Nagel (2022): 238 predictors against only 40–49 portfolios a month, pooled over 240 months, inflate the in-sample fit
                    • Out of sample (from July 1956):
                      • until June 1986: mean \(r_{\text{OOS}} = 1.98 \times 10^{-5}\), \(4.16\) standard errors above 0
                      • afterwards \(0.36 \times 10^{-5}\), only \(0.62\) standard errors
                      • the 10-year average is last above two standard errors in the window ending in December 1987; in the windows ending in 2014–2019 its largest ratio to the standard error is \(1.25\), as for stocks
                    • Interpretation suggested by Martin & Nagel (2022): the gap is consistent with investor learning
                      • but does not establish it or rule out risk premia
                      • early out-of-sample gains are also consistent with excess shrinkage before the computer era (Fig. 6)
                    ↩ Back

                    Appendix: Case Study, Replication, Limitations and Extensions

                    Slide 173 in the PDF

                    The course data contain no stock-level CRSP files, so the replication uses the 49 value-weighted industry portfolios with the same predictors, standardisation and windows; the size and price filters do not apply to portfolios. The authors state three limits:

                    1. Lagged returns are equilibrium outcomes, not the exogenous \(X\) of the model, so the application illustrates the wedge but does not test the model;
                    2. A \(t\)-test on the monthly \(r_{\text{OOS}}\) is not a valid test of \(\mathbb{E}[r_{\text{OOS}}] = 0\), an expectation that also averages over the prior distribution of \(g\);
                    3. The backward result may rely on i.i.d. cash-flow growth, homogeneous investors and constant characteristics.

                    Three extensions remain open:

                    1. Why the dividend–price regression, with a single predictor (\(J = 1\)), still shows an in-sample \(R^2\) of 0.12% against \(R^2_{OS} = -0.51\%\);
                    2. Whether nonlinear models such as trees and neural networks (Gu, Kelly & Xiu, 2020), which add functional flexibility without necessarily changing \(J\), widen the wedge;
                    3. Whether, in the sense of the AMH, early out-of-sample predictability reflects the excess shrinkage of investors with less computing power.

                    In brief

                    • Replicate with the course data (stock-level CRSP files are not part of them)
                      • the 49 value-weighted industry portfolios of the main replication slides; same predictors, standardisation and windows
                      • the size and price filters do not apply to portfolios
                    • Limits stated by the authors
                      • lagged returns are equilibrium outcomes, not the exogenous \(X\) of the model: the application illustrates the wedge, it does not test the model
                      • a \(t\)-test on the monthly \(r_{\text{OOS}}\) is not a valid test of \(\E[r_{\text{OOS}}] = 0\): an expectation that also averages over the prior distribution of \(g\)
                      • the backward result may rely on i.i.d. cash-flow growth, homogeneous investors and constant characteristics
                    • Open extensions
                      • time series: the dividend–price regression of section 3 has a single predictor (\(J = 1\)), yet an in-sample \(R^2\) of \(0.12\%\) and \(R^2_{OS} = -0.51\%\): what explains the gap, if not the number of predictors?
                      • nonlinear models (trees, neural networks Gu, Kelly & Xiu (2020)) add functional flexibility without necessarily changing \(J\): does the wedge grow?
                      • AMH: is early out-of-sample predictability the excess shrinkage of investors with less computing power?
                    ↩ Back

                    Appendix: The Discovery Loop, Reusable Prompts (1/2)

                    Slide 174 in the PDF

                    The first three stages of the discovery loop are written as prompts applied to one question, whether spot Bitcoin ETFs changed Bitcoin's serial dependence. The literature prompt asks for peer-reviewed studies since 2020 on ETF introductions and the efficiency of the underlying, with DOI, design (event study, difference-in-differences (DiD), structural break) and efficiency measure, and every DOI is then resolved on Crossref with matching title and authors. The hypothesis prompt asks for three mechanisms, arbitrage, investor mix and trading hours, each with the sign of the change in \(\rho_1\) and VR(\(q\)) and a variable that separates it from the others. The data-and-code prompt computes \(\rho_1\) with a HAC (Newey–West) standard error and the Lo–MacKinlay VR(5) with \(z^*\) in two windows of equal length around 11 January 2024.

                    In brief

                    • Literature
                      • List peer-reviewed studies since 2020 on how ETF introductions change the efficiency or price discovery of the underlying. For each: DOI, design (event study, difference-in-differences (DiD), structural break), efficiency measure. Flag any entry you are unsure exists.
                      • Resolve every DOI on Crossref; the title and authors must match
                    • Hypothesis
                      • Give three mechanisms by which spot ETFs could change Bitcoin's serial dependence (arbitrage, investor mix, trading hours). For each, the sign of the change in rho1 and VR(q), and a variable that separates it from the others.
                    • Data and code
                      • Read the daily Bitcoin closing prices from the course data; log returns; rho1 with a HAC (Newey-West) standard error of the estimate and the Lo-MacKinlay VR(5) with z*, in two windows of equal length around 11 January 2024.
                    ↩ Back

                    Appendix: The Discovery Loop, Reusable Prompts (2/2)

                    Slide 175 in the PDF

                    The robustness prompt asks for a pre-registered plan: window lengths, weekday-only closes, VR(\(q\)) for \(q = 2\) to 20 with Chow–Denning, placebo dates, and Ethereum as a control until its own spot ETFs in July 2024. The critique prompt casts the AI as a hostile referee: if Bitcoin was already efficient before 2024, what could the test detect, what is the minimum detectable change in \(\rho_1\), and which outcome variable would be better. The report gives the estimates, robust standard errors, the placebo distribution and every test run, with a multiple-testing correction, and the prompts that shaped a decision go into the file AI_USE.md.

                    In brief

                    • Robustness
                      • Write a pre-registered robustness plan: window lengths, weekday-only closes, VR(q) for q = 2 to 20 with Chow-Denning, placebo dates, and Ethereum as a control until its own spot ETFs (July 2024).
                    • Critique
                      • Act as a hostile referee: if Bitcoin was already efficient before 2024, what could this test ever detect? Compute the minimum detectable change in rho1 and name a better outcome variable.
                    • Report
                      • Estimates, robust standard errors, the placebo distribution, every test run (with a multiple-testing correction)
                      • Prompts that shaped a decision go into AI_USE.md
                    ↩ Back

                    Appendix: What the Researcher Must Check

                    Slide 176 in the PDF

                    Six checks apply to an AI-assisted before/after study of efficiency. The i.i.d. standard error \(1/\sqrt T\) rejects too often under volatility clustering, so the test uses the robust SE under \(H_0\) and \(z^*\), and HAC standard errors serve for intervals and before/after comparisons. Unequal windows give different power before and after, so the lengths are equal and fixed in advance. An event date chosen from the data is replaced by the launch date (first trading day) and a placebo distribution at dates without an event. A before/after difference is not causal, so a control asset, Ethereum before July 2024, enters a difference-in-differences, which is causal only under the conditions of the difference-in-differences slide and has a short common post-period, January–July 2024. Many statistics, \(\rho_1\), VR(2–20) and runs, require Chow–Denning or Holm rather than the smallest single \(p\)-value, and low power requires reporting the minimum detectable effect (MDE) of the estimation-and-power slide.

                    In brief

                    • i.i.d. standard errors: \(1/\sqrt{T}\) rejects too often under volatility clustering
                      • test: robust SE under \(H_0\) and \(z^*\), as in the weak-form tests of this chapter; HAC SE for intervals and before/after comparisons
                    • Unequal windows: different power before and after
                      • test: equal lengths, fixed in advance
                    • Event date chosen from the data
                      • test: the launch date (first trading day) only, plus a placebo distribution at dates without an event
                    • Confounding: before/after is not causal
                      • test: a control asset (Ethereum before July 2024) in a difference-in-differences
                      • causal only under the conditions of the difference-in-differences slide; the common post-period (Jan–Jul 2024) is short
                    • Many statistics: \(\rho_1\), VR(2–20), runs
                      • test: Chow–Denning or Holm, not the smallest single \(p\)-value
                    • Low power: report the MDE of the Estimation and Power slide
                    ↩ Back
